# Mechanical Engineering Curriculum
## 7-Week Program | 35 Contact Hours

**Target Audience:** FRC Robotics Teams & Engineering Students
**Prerequisites:** None - designed for beginners
**Format:** 7 weeks × 4-5 hours/week = 35 hours total
**Edition:** FRC Robotics — Umoja 7712

---

# WEEK 1: Introduction to Robotics & Design Process

**Duration:** 4-5 hours
**Learning Style:** Interactive exploration, collaborative discovery, design thinking

Students begin their FRC journey by understanding what makes a robot and learning the systematic design process that guides all successful robotics projects. This foundational week establishes the engineering mindset and collaborative culture essential for the program.

**Week 1 Learning Objectives:**
- Define what constitutes a robot and its essential characteristics
- Apply the 7-stage robot design process to engineering challenges
- Understand the cyclical nature of engineering design
- Work effectively in collaborative teams
- Recognize the connection between systematic design and competition success

---

## Session 1.1: What is a Robot?
**Duration:** 60 minutes | **Format:** Interactive discussion and hands-on exploration

### Introduction

Welcome to your first session in mechanical engineering for FRC robotics! Before we can build anything, we need to ask a fundamental question: what exactly IS a robot? You might think you already know the answer, but engineers define robots very precisely. In this session, we will explore that definition, look at real FRC robots, and start thinking like engineers.

A robot is hardware or software — or a combination of both — designed to simplify or automate a task. What separates a robot from a simple machine is its ability to sense its environment, make decisions based on that information, and take physical action. Your FRC robot must do all three: sense the field, decide what to do, and move or manipulate game pieces.

Understanding what a robot IS helps you understand what every component on your robot is FOR. Every motor, sensor, and structural member serves a specific role in the overall system. By the end of this program, you will be able to look at any FRC robot and explain why every part exists.

### Core Concepts

**What Makes Something a Robot?**
Engineers define a robot by four key characteristics. First, it must have some form of automation — the ability to perform tasks without constant human direction. Second, it must process sensor input — information coming from the environment like camera images, distance measurements, or encoder counts. Third, it must have decision-making ability, whether through a simple program or complex artificial intelligence. Fourth, it must physically interact with its environment through actuators like motors, pneumatic cylinders, or servos.

**FRC Robot Subsystems**
Every FRC robot is a system made of subsystems that work together. The drive base provides movement and field positioning. The intake system collects game pieces from the floor or human player station. The scoring mechanism places or launches game pieces into scoring positions. The control system — the RoboRIO, radio, and electronics — acts as the brain that coordinates everything. Understanding these subsystems helps you think about robot design systematically rather than as a jumble of parts.

**Robots vs. Simple Machines**
A simple machine like a ramp or pulley does not qualify as a robot because it cannot sense or decide — it just converts force or motion mechanically. A robot like your FRC robot takes input from sensors, runs that data through a processor, and commands actuators to respond. This sense-think-act loop is the defining characteristic of robotics.

**Why Definitions Matter in Engineering**
In engineering, precise definitions lead to precise design. When your team debates whether to add a feature to your robot, the question "does this help us sense, decide, or act more effectively?" gives you a framework for making that decision. Sloppy thinking leads to overbuilt, unreliable robots. Clear thinking about what each part does leads to elegant, effective machines.

**FRC Competition Context**
In FRC competitions, robots must operate in two modes: autonomous (15 seconds, fully pre-programmed) and teleoperated (2 minutes 15 seconds, driver-controlled). Both modes require the same physical robot — the design must support both. This constraint shapes every mechanical decision you make. A mechanism that works great under driver control but jams during autonomous will cost you points when it matters most.

### Video Lessons

#### What is a Robot? Introduction to Robotics (~8 min)
**Channel:** FIRST Robotics Competition
**Search:** "FIRST Robotics what is a robot introduction" on YouTube
**What to watch for:** Notice how the video distinguishes robots from simple machines, and how it describes the sense-think-act loop. Pay attention to examples of FRC robots performing autonomous tasks.

#### FRC Robot Subsystems Breakdown (~10 min)
**Channel:** Team 254 or similar top FRC team
**Search:** "FRC robot subsystems explained drivetrain intake mechanism" on YouTube
**What to watch for:** Identify each subsystem as it is discussed and ask yourself what sensing, decision-making, or action each one performs. Notice how subsystems connect and depend on each other.

### Practice Exercises

**Exercise 1: Robot or Not Robot?**
Create a two-column chart. In the left column, list 10 devices from everyday life (washing machine, thermostat, smoke alarm, TV remote, vending machine, automatic door, traffic light, elevator, dishwasher, Roomba). In the right column, classify each as "Robot," "Automated Machine," or "Simple Machine" and write one sentence explaining your classification. Be prepared to defend your answers to the group.

**Exercise 2: FRC Robot Subsystem Map**
Find three photos of different FRC robots from different years (search "FRC robot [year]" on Google Images). For each robot, draw a simple diagram labeling the drive base, intake, scoring mechanism, and any additional systems you can identify. Write one sentence describing what each subsystem does. Note which robots look simple versus complex.

**Exercise 3: Sense-Think-Act Analysis**
Choose one FRC robot action (for example: "pick up a ball from the floor"). Break that action into steps and label each step as Sense, Think, or Act. For example: Sense (camera detects ball location), Think (program calculates path), Act (drivetrain moves toward ball). Try to find at least 6 steps in the sequence.

**Exercise 4: Your Robot Vision**
Without worrying about technical details, sketch your dream FRC robot for a simple challenge: move a ball from the floor to a 3-foot-high goal. Label the parts and write what each one does. Keep it simple — this is about dreaming big, not engineering yet. You will return to this sketch at the end of the program to see how your thinking has changed.

### Knowledge Check

**1.** What is the best definition of a robot?
- A) Any machine with moving parts
- B) Hardware or software designed to automate a task through sensing, deciding, and acting
- C) A device controlled entirely by a human operator at all times
- D) A computer program that simulates human behavior

**Answer:** B

**2.** Which of the following is NOT one of the four key characteristics of a robot?
- A) Automation capability
- B) Sensor input processing
- C) Being made of metal
- D) Physical interaction with the environment

**Answer:** C

**3.** What is the "sense-think-act" loop?
- A) A safety checklist for the workshop
- B) The three-phase process where a robot detects input, processes it, and responds physically
- C) The three periods of an FRC match
- D) A method for organizing robot parts in storage

**Answer:** B

**4.** What does the drive base subsystem primarily provide?
- A) Game piece collection
- B) Scoring points
- C) Movement and field positioning
- D) Electronic control

**Answer:** C

**5.** How long is the autonomous period in an FRC match?
- A) 30 seconds
- B) 2 minutes 15 seconds
- C) 15 seconds
- D) 1 minute

**Answer:** C

**6.** What separates a robot from a simple machine like a ramp?
- A) Robots are always more expensive
- B) Robots can sense their environment and make decisions; simple machines only convert force or motion
- C) Simple machines are more reliable
- D) Robots must have wheels

**Answer:** B

**7.** Which FRC robot subsystem acts as the "brain" of the robot?
- A) The drive base
- B) The intake system
- C) The scoring mechanism
- D) The control system (RoboRIO and electronics)

**Answer:** D

**8.** Why does understanding robot subsystems help with design?
- A) It makes robots look more organized
- B) It allows you to think systematically about what each component does and how they connect
- C) It is required by FRC rules
- D) It reduces the number of parts needed

**Answer:** B

**9.** During which FRC match period does the robot operate without any driver input?
- A) Teleoperated period
- B) Endgame period
- C) Autonomous period
- D) Setup period

**Answer:** C

**10.** What is the main reason engineers use precise definitions?
- A) To impress judges at competition
- B) To write longer reports
- C) Precise definitions lead to precise design and clearer decision-making
- D) It is required by the engineering code of ethics

**Answer:** C

### Reflections

1. Think about a device you use every day. Using the four characteristics of a robot (automation, sensing, decision-making, physical action), explain whether it qualifies as a robot and why. What would need to change for it to become a robot?

2. Look at your "dream robot" sketch from Exercise 4. Now that you understand subsystems, which subsystem did you draw most detail for? Which did you leave vague? What does that tell you about where your interests and blind spots are?

3. FRC robots must perform well in both autonomous and teleoperated modes. If you were designing a robot for a game where the autonomous period scores double points, how would that change your design priorities compared to a game where autonomous barely matters?

4. The sense-think-act loop describes how robots work, but it also describes how engineers work: we observe problems, analyze data, and take action. Describe a time outside of robotics when you used this loop — even without realizing it. How did sensing more information change your decision?

### Summary

- A **robot** is hardware or software designed to automate tasks through a sense-think-act loop: detecting environmental input, processing it, and responding physically.
- Every FRC robot is composed of **subsystems** — drive base, intake, scoring mechanism, and control system — each serving a specific function.
- What distinguishes a robot from a simple machine is **decision-making ability**: robots process sensor data and respond; simple machines only convert force.
- FRC robots must operate in both **autonomous mode** (15 seconds, pre-programmed) and **teleoperated mode** (2:15, driver-controlled), which shapes every design decision.
- Clear thinking about **what each part does** leads to better engineering decisions and more reliable competition robots.

## Session 1.2: The 7-Stage Design Process
**Duration:** 60 minutes | **Format:** Interactive instruction with immediate application

### Introduction

Every successful FRC robot begins not with a wrench or a welder, but with a process. The 7-stage design process is the systematic framework that engineers use to transform a game manual into a competition-ready robot. Without a process, teams build random ideas and hope they work. With a process, teams build solutions that solve real problems reliably.

This session walks through all seven stages in detail. You will not just memorize them — you will apply them immediately to a real design challenge. By the end of today, you will have practiced moving from "here is a problem" to "here is a documented plan," which is exactly what championship teams do during kickoff weekend.

The 7-stage process is not just for robotics. It is the same framework used by aerospace engineers designing spacecraft, civil engineers designing bridges, and product designers creating the devices you use every day. Learning it now gives you a professional tool you will use for the rest of your engineering career.

### Core Concepts

**Stage 1: Ask Questions and Set Goals**
Every engineering project starts with questions. What does the robot need to accomplish? What are the constraints — size, weight, power, time? What materials and manufacturing capabilities are available? What has been tried before and what worked? In FRC, this stage happens during kickoff when teams analyze the game manual and identify exactly what actions score points and which are most efficient to pursue.

**Stage 2: Brainstorm Designs and Concepts**
With goals clear, teams generate as many solutions as possible without judging them. This is ideation — quantity over quality. Rough sketches, wild ideas, and combinations of existing designs all belong here. The worst mistake in brainstorming is eliminating ideas too early. A "crazy" idea often contains the seed of an elegant solution once refined.

**Stage 3: Create a Basic Layout Drawing**
From brainstormed concepts, the team selects the most promising approach and creates a basic layout — a rough spatial arrangement showing where major components will be located. This is not a detailed technical drawing; it is a planning tool. It shows whether the battery fits, whether mechanisms have room to move, and whether the overall concept is physically feasible.

**Stage 4: Detail Design**
Now the engineering gets precise. Detail design produces technical drawings with exact dimensions, tolerances, material specifications, and assembly instructions. Each part is defined well enough that someone who never saw the original concept could manufacture it correctly. In FRC teams using CAD, this stage produces the full 3D model and 2D manufacturing drawings.

**Stage 5: Build a Prototype**
Prototyping turns designs into physical reality — but intentionally imperfect reality. Prototypes are built fast, cheap, and disposable. Their purpose is to answer questions: Does the mechanism work in principle? What did we get wrong? What surprised us? A prototype that fails is not a failure — it is data. The fastest way to learn in engineering is to build something and watch it break.

**Stage 6: Test and Evaluate**
Testing is systematic, not casual. Engineers define test criteria before testing begins: what does success look like? What metrics will be measured? Test results are documented and compared against goals from Stage 1. Problems discovered in testing get fed back into the design — this is the iteration loop that separates good engineering from guesswork.

**Stage 7: Communicate Results**
Engineering knowledge only has value if it can be shared. Stage 7 means documenting what was built, what was learned, and what should be done differently. In FRC, this includes the engineering notebook, design reviews, and knowledge transfer between team generations. Great teams communicate so well that next year's students can build on this year's work rather than starting over.

### Video Lessons

#### The Engineering Design Process Explained (~7 min)
**Channel:** Crash Course Engineering
**Search:** "Crash Course Engineering design process" on YouTube
**What to watch for:** Notice how the video emphasizes iteration — the process is not linear but cyclical. Pay attention to how testing results feed back into design changes, which mirrors exactly what FRC teams do during build season.

#### FRC Kickoff and Design Strategy (~12 min)
**Channel:** FRC teams or FIRST official channel
**Search:** "FRC kickoff game analysis design strategy" on YouTube
**What to watch for:** Watch how experienced teams move from reading the game manual (Stage 1) to sketching robot concepts (Stages 2-3). Notice how they prioritize which game actions to pursue and which to ignore.

### Practice Exercises

**Exercise 1: Stage Identification**
Read through a simplified FRC game description (provided by mentor or written out for this exercise). For each of the 7 stages, write 2-3 specific questions or decisions this team would need to make. For example, for Stage 1: "What is the maximum point value of each scoring action?" This exercise makes the stages concrete rather than abstract.

**Exercise 2: Brainstorming Sprint**
Given the challenge "design a mechanism to pick up a 6-inch foam ball from the floor," set a timer for 10 minutes and sketch as many different intake concepts as you can. No idea is too simple or too complex. Aim for at least 8 different concepts. After time is up, share with a partner and combine your lists. Then each person selects their top 2 concepts and explains why.

**Exercise 3: Layout Drawing Practice**
Choose your top concept from Exercise 2 and create a basic layout drawing on graph paper. Mark where the motor goes, where the ball travels, how large the mechanism is, and how it connects to a robot chassis. Use approximate dimensions — precision comes later. The goal is to check whether the concept is physically possible.

**Exercise 4: Design Review Simulation**
In groups of 3-4, each person presents their layout drawing from Exercise 3. The group asks three questions: "What problem does this solve?" "What is the biggest risk with this design?" "What would you test first in a prototype?" The presenter answers without getting defensive. Practice giving and receiving technical feedback professionally.

### Knowledge Check

**1.** What is the primary purpose of Stage 1 (Ask Questions and Set Goals) in the design process?
- A) To start building immediately before competitors do
- B) To clearly define what the design must accomplish and what constraints exist
- C) To assign team roles and responsibilities
- D) To order materials and parts

**Answer:** B

**2.** During brainstorming (Stage 2), what is the most important rule?
- A) Only sketch ideas that are definitely buildable
- B) Generate as many ideas as possible without judging them prematurely
- C) Focus on the single best idea and develop it fully
- D) Copy successful designs from previous years

**Answer:** B

**3.** What is the difference between a basic layout drawing (Stage 3) and a detail design (Stage 4)?
- A) There is no meaningful difference
- B) Layout drawings show spatial arrangement and feasibility; detail designs provide precise dimensions and specifications
- C) Detail designs are always done on a computer; layout drawings are always on paper
- D) Layout drawings are more accurate than detail designs

**Answer:** B

**4.** What is the primary purpose of building a prototype (Stage 5)?
- A) To create the final competition robot
- B) To impress sponsors with a finished product
- C) To quickly test whether a concept works and discover problems with minimal cost
- D) To practice manufacturing skills

**Answer:** C

**5.** A prototype fails during testing. What should an engineer conclude?
- A) The entire design concept should be abandoned
- B) The prototype was built incorrectly
- C) Valuable data has been collected that can improve the next design iteration
- D) Testing was done too early in the process

**Answer:** C

**6.** What makes Stage 7 (Communicate Results) essential rather than optional?
- A) FRC rules require it for judged awards
- B) Engineering knowledge only has value if it can be shared and built upon by others
- C) It is the easiest stage and deserves more time
- D) Communication skills are more important than technical skills

**Answer:** B

**7.** During FRC kickoff, which stage of the design process are teams primarily in when they read and analyze the game manual?
- A) Stage 3: Basic Layout Drawing
- B) Stage 5: Prototype
- C) Stage 1: Ask Questions and Set Goals
- D) Stage 7: Communicate Results

**Answer:** C

**8.** What does "iteration" mean in the context of the design process?
- A) Repeating the same test multiple times to confirm results
- B) Cycling back through design stages as new information is discovered to progressively improve the design
- C) Building multiple copies of the same design simultaneously
- D) Having team members rotate through different roles

**Answer:** B

**9.** Which stage produces technical drawings with exact dimensions and tolerances?
- A) Stage 2: Brainstorm
- B) Stage 3: Basic Layout
- C) Stage 6: Test and Evaluate
- D) Stage 4: Detail Design

**Answer:** D

**10.** Why do top FRC teams create an engineering notebook throughout the season?
- A) It is required by FIRST for all teams
- B) It supports Stage 7 by documenting decisions and learnings so future team members can build on past work
- C) Judges award extra points for longer notebooks
- D) It replaces the need for CAD models

**Answer:** B

### Reflections

1. Think about the last time you built or created something — whether for school, a hobby, or at home. Which of the 7 stages did you actually go through, even informally? Which stages did you skip? How did skipping those stages affect the result?

2. Many FRC teams rush directly from game analysis to building, skipping detailed brainstorming and layout planning. Based on what you learned today, what specific problems would you expect to see in a robot built that way? What would it look like at competition?

3. Stage 7 (Communicate Results) is often the stage teams invest the least time in. Why do you think that is? What would change in your team's culture if you treated documentation as equally important as building?

4. The design process is described as cyclical, not linear. At what point in an FRC build season would you most want to cycle back to Stage 1 and re-examine your original goals? What might cause you to do that?

### Summary

- The **7-stage design process** provides a systematic framework: Ask Questions, Brainstorm, Layout Drawing, Detail Design, Prototype, Test, and Communicate.
- **Brainstorming** requires suspending judgment — generating many ideas before evaluating any of them produces better final solutions.
- **Prototypes** are intentionally cheap and fast; their purpose is to generate data about what works, not to create a finished product.
- The process is **cyclical**: test results feed back into design changes, which are then tested again, progressively improving the solution.
- **Stage 7 (Communicate)** is not optional — it is what transforms individual team knowledge into organizational knowledge that future members can build upon.

## Session 1.3: Cyclical Design & Iteration
**Duration:** 60 minutes | **Format:** Hands-on iteration challenge

### Introduction

The most dangerous misconception in engineering is that great designs come from single moments of inspiration. In reality, great designs come from many cycles of build, test, learn, and improve. This process is called iteration, and understanding it will change how you think about your work throughout the entire build season.

Iteration is not a sign of failure — it is the mechanism of progress. Every successful FRC robot at championship level has been through dozens or hundreds of design iterations. Teams that win championships do not get lucky with their first design; they iterate faster and learn more efficiently than their competitors.

Today you will experience iteration firsthand through a rapid design challenge. You will build something, test it, discover what is wrong, and improve it — in a single session. The goal is not to create a perfect solution but to internalize the feeling of the improvement loop.

### Core Concepts

**What is Iteration?**
Iteration means repeating a process with the goal of approaching a target. In engineering, each iteration produces a version of a design that is tested, evaluated, and used to inform the next version. The key word is "informed" — random changes are not iteration, they are guessing. True iteration involves analyzing test results, forming a hypothesis about what change will improve performance, making that specific change, and testing again.

**The Iteration Loop**
The iteration loop has four steps: Build (create a version), Test (evaluate it against criteria), Analyze (understand why it performed as it did), and Improve (make a specific, hypothesis-driven change). This loop can cycle many times in a single day during intensive prototyping, or over weeks during detailed design refinement.

**Why Iteration Beats Perfect Planning**
No matter how carefully you design something on paper or in CAD, physical reality always surprises you. Materials behave differently than expected, dimensions are slightly off, friction appears where it wasn't predicted, and game pieces interact with mechanisms in unexpected ways. Iteration is the only reliable way to close the gap between the design you imagined and the design that actually works.

**Iteration in FRC Competition**
Championship FRC teams often bring major mechanism changes to competition events — they iterate between regional events based on match performance data. A team might completely redesign their intake mechanism between events after discovering it jams in 30% of cycles. This willingness to iterate under pressure is a hallmark of elite engineering culture.

**Managing Iteration: Time and Scope**
Iteration must be managed deliberately. In FRC, build season is six weeks — you cannot iterate endlessly. Effective teams set "decision gates": points in time where a design must be frozen even if it is not perfect. They then prototype aggressively early, make decisions based on data, and lock in designs with enough time to manufacture quality final parts. Endless iteration without deadline discipline leads to robots that are not finished for competition.

### Video Lessons

#### Iterative Design in Engineering (~6 min)
**Channel:** Real Engineering or Practical Engineering
**Search:** "iterative design process engineering prototyping" on YouTube
**What to watch for:** Notice the examples of famous products or structures that went through many iterations before reaching their final form. Identify the moment in each story where a failed test led to a crucial insight that the design team would not have discovered any other way.

#### FRC Build Season Time Management (~8 min)
**Channel:** FRC team channel or FRC blog content
**Search:** "FRC build season schedule iteration timeline prototyping" on YouTube
**What to watch for:** Pay attention to how teams allocate time between prototyping and final build. Notice when teams make "freeze" decisions and what criteria they use to decide when a design is good enough to move forward.

### Practice Exercises

**Exercise 1: Paper Tower Iteration**
Using only 10 sheets of paper and 30cm of tape, build the tallest freestanding tower you can in 5 minutes. Measure the height. Now analyze: what failed? What worked? Make one specific change hypothesis. Rebuild for 5 more minutes. Measure again. Repeat for a third cycle. Record your heights across all three iterations and describe the specific change you made and why between each cycle.

**Exercise 2: Iteration Log**
Create a simple iteration log template with these columns: Version Number, Change Made, Reason for Change, Test Result, Next Hypothesis. Fill in 3 rows based on your paper tower exercise. This is the actual format engineering teams use to track design evolution. Save this template — you will use it throughout the program.

**Exercise 3: FRC Mechanism Iteration Case Study**
Research or discuss with your mentor: find one example of a real FRC team that significantly changed a mechanism between competitions or during a build season. Write a one-paragraph summary describing what the original design was, what problem was discovered through testing, what change was made, and what the result was. If you cannot find a specific example, create a plausible hypothetical based on what you know about common FRC mechanisms.

**Exercise 4: Iteration Planning**
Given the constraint that your team has 3 weeks left in build season and needs to finalize a ball intake design, create an iteration plan. Specify: How many prototype iterations will you attempt? What specific question does each iteration answer? What is your decision gate — at what point do you freeze the design regardless of results? This exercise practices the discipline of bounded iteration.

### Knowledge Check

**1.** What is the definition of engineering iteration?
- A) Building the same design multiple times to practice manufacturing
- B) Repeating a design-test-analyze-improve cycle to progressively approach a target
- C) Making random changes until something works
- D) Testing a design once and accepting the result

**Answer:** B

**2.** What are the four steps of the iteration loop in order?
- A) Plan, Execute, Review, Archive
- B) Design, Build, Ship, Support
- C) Build, Test, Analyze, Improve
- D) Sketch, Model, Manufacture, Deploy

**Answer:** C

**3.** Why does physical testing always reveal information that pure planning cannot?
- A) Physical materials are always cheaper than simulations
- B) Real-world conditions involve friction, tolerances, and unexpected interactions that are difficult to predict perfectly
- C) Testing equipment is more accurate than computer models
- D) Physical tests take less time than analytical calculations

**Answer:** B

**4.** What is a "decision gate" in the context of FRC build season?
- A) A physical gate on the robot that controls game piece flow
- B) A scheduled point in time where a design is frozen even if imperfect, to ensure manufacturing time remains available
- C) A team vote on which design concept to pursue
- D) The moment when a design is approved by a mentor

**Answer:** B

**5.** What distinguishes true iteration from random guessing?
- A) True iteration costs more money
- B) True iteration takes longer
- C) True iteration is based on analyzing test results and making a specific hypothesis-driven change
- D) True iteration always produces improvements

**Answer:** C

**6.** Championship FRC teams are known to make major changes between competition events. What does this demonstrate?
- A) That their original designs were poorly planned
- B) That iteration under competitive pressure is a hallmark of elite engineering culture
- C) That they have more money to spend on parts
- D) That the FRC rules change between events

**Answer:** B

**7.** An FRC team's intake mechanism jams 30% of the time during testing. Using the iteration loop, what should be their FIRST step?
- A) Replace the entire mechanism with a different design type
- B) Continue testing to see if the problem gets better on its own
- C) Analyze why it jams — identify the root cause before making changes
- D) Add more motors to force through the jam

**Answer:** C

**8.** Why do engineering teams keep iteration logs?
- A) To show mentors they are working hard
- B) To track what changes were made, why, and what resulted — enabling informed decisions and knowledge transfer
- C) FRC competition rules require documentation
- D) To calculate how many hours were spent on a design

**Answer:** B

**9.** In the paper tower exercise, why is it important to make only ONE specific change between iterations rather than many changes at once?
- A) It makes the exercise take longer
- B) Making one change at a time allows you to clearly identify which change caused any improvement or degradation
- C) Multiple changes at once are physically impossible
- D) It reduces the amount of materials used

**Answer:** B

**10.** What is the greatest risk of iterating without a decision deadline?
- A) The design becomes too good and complex to manufacture
- B) The team runs out of iteration ideas
- C) The robot is never completed in time for competition
- D) Other teams copy the iterative approach

**Answer:** C

### Reflections

1. Think about the paper tower exercise. At what moment did you learn the most — when you were planning, when you were building, or when you were analyzing a failure? What does this tell you about where engineering learning actually happens?

2. Many students feel frustrated or embarrassed when their first design does not work well. How does understanding iteration change the emotional experience of engineering? What would you say to a teammate who is discouraged after a prototype fails?

3. Top FRC teams iterate more cycles in the same amount of time as average teams. What practices or habits do you think allow them to iterate faster? What would your team need to change to increase your iteration speed?

4. Consider a time in your life outside of robotics where you used iteration — maybe learning a sport, mastering a video game, or improving at a skill. Describe the iteration loop you went through. What was your "test criteria"? What did you change between attempts?

### Summary

- **Iteration** is the core mechanism of engineering progress: each cycle of build-test-analyze-improve moves the design closer to the goal.
- Great designs come from **many cycles of refinement**, not single moments of inspiration — even championship FRC robots have been through dozens of iterations.
- Each iteration change should be **hypothesis-driven**: analyze results, form a specific theory about what change will help, implement only that change, then test again.
- **Decision gates** are essential in FRC — teams must freeze designs with enough time remaining to manufacture quality final parts, even if the design is not perfect.
- Keeping an **iteration log** documents what was changed, why, and what resulted, enabling teams to make informed decisions and transfer knowledge across seasons.

## Session 1.4: Team Collaboration & Communication
**Duration:** 60 minutes | **Format:** Team dynamics workshop with practical exercises

### Introduction

Engineering is almost never a solo activity. Every FRC robot is the product of a team — students with different skills, knowledge, and perspectives working toward a common goal. The quality of that teamwork directly determines the quality of the robot. You can have brilliant engineers on a team that communicates poorly and lose to a team of average engineers who collaborate exceptionally well.

This session focuses on the human side of engineering: how to divide work effectively, how to communicate technical information clearly, how to give and receive criticism productively, and how to make decisions when team members disagree. These skills are just as important as knowing how to use CAD software or a drill press.

In professional engineering, communication failures cause disasters — from software bugs to structural collapses. In FRC, poor communication causes missed deadlines, duplicate work, conflicting designs, and robots that do not perform their intended function at competition. Learning to communicate like an engineer now will serve you in every technical role you ever hold.

### Core Concepts

**Team Roles in FRC Mechanical Engineering**
Effective FRC mechanical teams assign clear roles. A lead engineer or design lead is responsible for overall design coherence and making final decisions when the team disagrees. Sub-system leads own specific mechanisms (drivetrain, intake, scoring) and are accountable for their performance. Manufacturing leads execute fabrication and ensure quality. Documentation leads maintain the engineering notebook. All roles contribute to all discussions, but clear ownership prevents decisions from falling through the cracks.

**Technical Communication**
Technical communication means sharing engineering information precisely enough that the recipient can act on it correctly. Vague communication like "make the arm longer" causes problems; precise communication like "extend the arm by 4 inches to move the pivot point from 18 inches to 22 inches from the chassis centerline" enables correct action. Engineers develop the habit of communicating with numbers, references, and drawings rather than approximations and gestures.

**Design Reviews**
A design review is a structured meeting where a designer presents their work to a group of reviewers who ask questions and identify potential problems. Good design reviews are not criticism sessions — they are collaborative problem-finding sessions. The designer is not defending their design; they are inviting others to help make it better. Mentors and experienced students should model this culture explicitly.

**Disagreement and Decision-Making**
Technical disagreements are healthy — they surface different perspectives and often lead to better designs. Destructive disagreements are personal and emotional rather than technical. Effective teams establish clear decision-making processes: who has final authority on design decisions? How do you escalate when consensus cannot be reached? In FRC, the lead engineer typically has final authority, but good lead engineers seek input broadly before deciding.

**Documentation as Communication**
Every design decision, test result, and build process should be documented. Documentation is not bureaucracy — it is communication across time. When a student graduates, their knowledge leaves with them unless it was written down. When a mechanism breaks at competition, the team that documented their design can diagnose the problem faster. Documentation is the most leveraged communication investment a team can make.

### Video Lessons

#### Teamwork and Communication in Engineering (~8 min)
**Channel:** Engineering with Rosie or similar engineering education channel
**Search:** "engineering teamwork communication technical roles" on YouTube
**What to watch for:** Notice how the video describes the difference between technical disagreements (healthy) and personal disagreements (harmful). Pay attention to any specific strategies mentioned for reaching consensus when team members disagree about design choices.

#### FRC Team Organization and Roles (~6 min)
**Channel:** FRC team or FIRST official content
**Search:** "FRC team organization subteam roles mechanical electrical programming" on YouTube
**What to watch for:** Compare the team structure shown to what you understand about your own team. What roles exist that you did not know about? Which roles seem most critical during early build season versus late build season?

### Practice Exercises

**Exercise 1: Communication Precision Challenge**
One person draws a simple mechanical assembly (three rectangles connected with circles representing bolts — keep it simple) without showing their partner. They must verbally describe the drawing precisely enough for their partner to recreate it without seeing the original. After 3 minutes, compare drawings. Discuss: What information was missing? What would an engineer add to make the description clearer? Repeat with roles switched.

**Exercise 2: Design Review Practice**
In groups of 3-4, one person presents their intake sketch from Session 1.2. Reviewers must ask exactly three questions (no statements, only questions) and then summarize in one sentence what they think the biggest risk of the design is. The presenter responds technically, not defensively. Rotate so everyone presents. Debrief: what made the feedback most useful? What made it least useful?

**Exercise 3: Role Assignment Simulation**
Given a simplified FRC build season scenario (mentor provides a one-paragraph description of a robot project), your team must assign the following roles: Design Lead, Drivetrain Lead, Mechanism Lead, Manufacturing Lead, Documentation Lead. Write one sentence per role describing what that person is accountable for this week. Discuss: what happens if two roles conflict? How do you resolve it?

**Exercise 4: Documentation Template**
Create a one-page design decision document for a choice you made in Session 1.2 or 1.3. Include: Decision Made, Options Considered, Reasons for Choice, Expected Risks, and Who Made the Decision. This format is used in professional engineering and FRC engineering notebooks. Save your template — you will use this format throughout the program.

### Knowledge Check

**1.** Why does clear role assignment improve team performance in FRC?
- A) It reduces the number of people needed on the team
- B) It prevents decisions from falling through the cracks and establishes clear accountability
- C) It allows mentors to evaluate each student individually
- D) FRC rules require specific team roles

**Answer:** B

**2.** What is the difference between technical communication and casual communication?
- A) Technical communication is always written; casual communication is always verbal
- B) Technical communication uses precise numbers, references, and drawings so the recipient can act correctly without ambiguity
- C) Technical communication is only used in formal meetings
- D) There is no meaningful difference in engineering contexts

**Answer:** B

**3.** What is the primary purpose of a design review?
- A) To criticize and judge the designer's work
- B) To collaboratively find potential problems in a design before they become expensive build problems
- C) To formally approve a design for manufacturing
- D) To compare multiple competing designs and select one

**Answer:** B

**4.** What distinguishes a healthy technical disagreement from a destructive one?
- A) Healthy disagreements are short; destructive ones take a long time
- B) Healthy disagreements focus on technical merits; destructive ones become personal or emotional
- C) Healthy disagreements are resolved by vote; destructive ones require mentor intervention
- D) Healthy disagreements only occur early in the season

**Answer:** B

**5.** Why is documentation described as "communication across time"?
- A) It takes a long time to write good documentation
- B) It preserves knowledge so future team members can build on past decisions rather than rediscovering them
- C) Documentation must be submitted to FIRST at the end of the season
- D) It allows teams to communicate with teams in other time zones

**Answer:** B

**6.** In the communication precision challenge, what is the most common reason two drawings end up different?
- A) People draw at different speeds
- B) The describer uses vague spatial language (bigger, to the right) instead of specific measurements and reference points
- C) The listener is not paying attention
- D) Simple mechanical assemblies are inherently ambiguous

**Answer:** B

**7.** When a team member disagrees with a design decision that has already been made, what is the most professional response?
- A) Ignore the decision and implement their preferred approach anyway
- B) Publicly criticize the decision at every opportunity
- C) Raise the concern through appropriate channels, accept the final decision, and document their alternative view
- D) Immediately escalate to the mentor to overrule the decision

**Answer:** C

**8.** What information should a design decision document always include?
- A) Only the final decision made
- B) The decision, options considered, reasons for the choice, expected risks, and who decided
- C) The cost of each option and timeline estimates
- D) A list of all team members who were present

**Answer:** B

**9.** Why do engineering teams suffer when only one person holds all the technical knowledge?
- A) One person cannot have enough ideas
- B) If that person is absent, sick, or leaves the team, critical knowledge is lost
- C) Engineers are required to share knowledge by professional codes
- D) One person working alone is always less efficient than a team

**Answer:** B

**10.** What should a design lead do when team members cannot reach consensus on a technical decision?
- A) Delay the decision until everyone agrees
- B) Choose randomly to save time
- C) Seek broad input, synthesize the perspectives, make a decision based on engineering criteria, and explain the reasoning
- D) Always defer to the most experienced person in the room

**Answer:** C

### Reflections

1. Think about the best team experience you have had — in sports, school, a group project, or elsewhere. What specific practices made that team effective? Which of those practices would translate directly to an FRC mechanical engineering team?

2. The session described documentation as "the most leveraged communication investment a team can make." Do you agree? What is the cost of good documentation (time, effort) versus the cost of poor documentation (lost knowledge, repeated mistakes)? When does the investment pay off?

3. Design reviews require presenting your work to be criticized. Many people find this uncomfortable, especially students who are used to school environments where their work is only judged by a teacher. What would you need to feel safe presenting your design for review? What would a mentor need to do to create that environment?

4. In professional engineering, communication failures have caused disasters — spacecraft crashes, building collapses, medical device failures. Without looking up specific examples, think about how a miscommunication could cause a serious problem in an FRC context. What practices from today's session would prevent that specific problem?

### Summary

- Clear **role assignment** prevents duplicate work and missed responsibilities — every significant task should have one person accountable for it.
- **Technical communication** uses numbers, references, and drawings rather than approximations; the test of good communication is whether the recipient can act on it correctly.
- **Design reviews** are collaborative problem-finding sessions, not criticism sessions — the reviewer helps the designer make their work better.
- **Documentation** is communication across time — it preserves team knowledge so future members can build on past work rather than rediscovering it.
- Healthy teams develop a culture where **technical disagreements** are welcomed and resolved through engineering criteria, not personality or seniority.

---

## Session 1.5: Design Documentation & Next Steps
**Duration:** 60 minutes | **Format:** Documentation workshop and week synthesis

### Introduction

This final session of Week 1 brings everything together. You have learned what a robot is, how to design one systematically, how iteration improves designs, and how teams collaborate effectively. Now you will practice the skill that ties all of it together: documentation.

An engineering notebook is not a journal or a diary. It is a technical record of decisions, experiments, and outcomes that allows any engineer — including future you — to understand exactly what was done, why, and what was learned. In FRC, the engineering notebook is also evaluated by judges for the Engineering Inspiration Award and the Chairman's Award, making good documentation valuable beyond just team knowledge.

By the end of this session, you will have created the foundation of your personal engineering notebook for this program, synthesized the key lessons of Week 1, and set specific learning goals for the remaining six weeks. This is the point where individual sessions become a coherent learning journey.

### Core Concepts

**What Goes in an Engineering Notebook**
A complete engineering notebook entry for a design session includes: the date, who participated, what problem was being addressed, what options were considered, what decision was made and why, what was built or tested, what the results were, and what the next steps are. Sketches, calculations, and test data belong in the notebook — not just text descriptions. The standard is: could someone who was not present understand exactly what happened?

**Engineering Drawings vs. Sketches**
There are two types of visual documentation with different purposes. Engineering sketches are quick, freehand drawings used to capture ideas during brainstorming and planning — speed and clarity matter more than precision. Engineering drawings are precise, dimensioned documents used for manufacturing — accuracy and completeness are critical. Both have their place in the notebook; knowing which is needed at each stage of the design process is an important skill.

**Digital vs. Physical Documentation**
Modern engineering teams use both. Physical notebooks capture hand-drawn sketches and handwritten notes that are sometimes faster than typing. Digital tools like shared drives, wikis, CAD systems, and project management software enable collaboration and version control. Effective FRC teams develop a documentation system that captures both — a physical notebook for quick capture and a digital system for permanent storage and sharing.

**The FRC Engineering Notebook Standard**
FIRST evaluates engineering notebooks on specific criteria: evidence of the design process, clear documentation of design decisions and their rationale, test data and results, iteration evidence, and reflection on what was learned. A notebook that only shows finished designs misses the point — judges want to see the messy, iterative process behind the finished robot.

**Setting Learning Goals**
Effective learners are intentional about what they want to learn and how they will know they have learned it. For the remaining six weeks, you should identify specific skills you want to develop, resources you will use to develop them, and how you will demonstrate mastery. Goal-setting transforms passive attendance into active learning.

### Video Lessons

#### How to Keep an Engineering Notebook (~7 min)
**Channel:** FRC team documentation channel or FIRST official
**Search:** "FRC engineering notebook how to keep examples" on YouTube
**What to watch for:** Notice the specific types of content that appear in high-quality engineering notebooks — not just the final designs, but the brainstorming, failed attempts, and analysis of what went wrong. Compare to your own documentation habits and identify two specific things you want to do differently.

#### Technical Sketching for Engineers (~5 min)
**Channel:** Engineering drawing or technical sketching channel
**Search:** "engineering technical sketching freehand drawing basics" on YouTube
**What to watch for:** Focus on the specific techniques that make freehand sketches readable: light construction lines, consistent proportions, clear labeling, and dimension notation. These techniques take practice but dramatically improve how well your sketches communicate.

### Practice Exercises

**Exercise 1: Retroactive Notebook Entry**
Write a complete engineering notebook entry for one of the sessions from this week. Include the date, participants, problem statement, options considered, decision made and rationale, results or outcomes, and next steps. Add at least one sketch. Use the standard: "Could someone who was not here understand what happened?" Have a partner read your entry and give you specific feedback on what was unclear.

**Exercise 2: Engineering Sketch Practice**
Practice the three most important freehand sketching techniques: (1) Drawing straight lines freehand by making many short overlapping strokes, (2) Drawing circles by sketching squares first and then rounding the corners, (3) Drawing isometric (3D) boxes to represent mechanical components. Fill one full page with practice. Freehand sketching is a skill that improves rapidly with deliberate practice.

**Exercise 3: Documentation System Design**
Design a documentation system for your team (or a hypothetical team). Decide: What will be captured in a physical notebook? What will be stored digitally? How will sketches be transferred from paper to digital? How will you ensure entries are complete before they are archived? Write a one-page "Documentation Protocol" that any new team member could follow.

**Exercise 4: Six-Week Learning Goals**
Write three specific learning goals for the remaining six weeks of this program. Each goal should follow this format: "By the end of Week [N], I will be able to [specific, observable skill], which I will demonstrate by [specific evidence]." Example: "By the end of Week 3, I will be able to create a dimensioned 3D part in Onshape, which I will demonstrate by modeling the intake bracket from Session 6.2." Share your goals with a partner who will check in with you on progress.

### Knowledge Check

**1.** What is the standard for a complete engineering notebook entry?
- A) A brief description of what was built
- B) Could someone who was not present understand exactly what happened, why decisions were made, and what was learned?
- C) A list of materials used and time spent
- D) A photograph of the finished component

**Answer:** B

**2.** What is the difference between an engineering sketch and an engineering drawing?
- A) Sketches are always done by hand; drawings are always digital
- B) Sketches are quick ideation tools prioritizing speed and clarity; drawings are precise manufacturing documents prioritizing accuracy
- C) Sketches are only used in school; drawings are used in industry
- D) There is no meaningful difference — both serve the same purpose

**Answer:** B

**3.** What specific content does FIRST look for when evaluating FRC engineering notebooks for awards?
- A) Only the final robot design and competition results
- B) Evidence of the design process including brainstorming, iteration, test data, and reflection — not just finished results
- C) The number of pages and quality of photographs
- D) Proof that all team members contributed equally

**Answer:** B

**4.** Why do effective FRC teams use both physical and digital documentation?
- A) FIRST requires both formats
- B) Physical tools capture ideas quickly; digital tools enable collaboration, version control, and permanent storage
- C) Physical notebooks are required for award judging; digital systems are optional
- D) Digital documentation is not reliable enough for engineering purposes

**Answer:** B

**5.** What should appear in an engineering notebook entry beyond just text descriptions?
- A) Only photographs of finished parts
- B) Sketches, calculations, test data, and diagrams that help someone who was not present understand what happened
- C) Team member contact information and attendance records
- D) Quotes from engineering textbooks relevant to the work

**Answer:** B

**6.** What format should a good learning goal follow?
- A) "I want to learn more about CAD software"
- B) "By the end of Week [N], I will be able to [specific skill], demonstrated by [specific evidence]"
- C) "I will work hard and pay attention in every session"
- D) "My goal is to help my team win competitions"

**Answer:** B

**7.** Why does an engineering notebook entry need to record options that were considered but NOT chosen?
- A) To prove the team worked harder than competitors
- B) So future team members understand why a particular path was taken, preventing repeated consideration of rejected options
- C) FIRST award judges count the number of options considered
- D) Rejected options are often the best ideas and should be preserved for later use

**Answer:** B

**8.** In technical sketching, what technique helps you draw a more accurate circle freehand?
- A) Draw the circle in one continuous stroke without lifting the pen
- B) First sketch a square, then round the four corners to approximate the circle
- C) Use your thumb as a radius pivot point
- D) Trace a circular object on the page

**Answer:** B

**9.** What is the primary benefit of setting specific learning goals at the start of a program?
- A) It allows mentors to assess student progress more easily
- B) It transforms passive attendance into active, intentional learning with clear targets and evidence of mastery
- C) It reduces the time needed to complete the program
- D) Goals are required by school programs that use this curriculum

**Answer:** B

**10.** Which of the following best describes what "documentation is communication across time" means?
- A) Documentation takes a long time to write
- B) Documents written today can be read by future team members who were not present, preserving knowledge that would otherwise be lost
- C) Engineers communicate differently at different points in their careers
- D) Documentation should be updated regularly as time passes

**Answer:** B

### Reflections

1. Look at your engineering notebook entry from Exercise 1. Be honest: would someone who was not in the session truly understand what happened from reading it? What specific information was hardest to write clearly? What would make engineering documentation feel more natural and less like a chore?

2. You have now spent one week on the design process, collaboration, and documentation — before touching any physical robot parts. Some students might feel impatient to "get to the real work." How would you explain to an impatient teammate why this foundation week is worth the time investment?

3. Review the learning goals you wrote in Exercise 4. Are they specific enough that your partner could objectively verify whether you achieved them? If not, revise one of them to be more specific. What skill from this week's sessions do you most want to develop over the program?

4. Engineering documentation is sometimes described as "the most boring part of engineering that matters the most." Do you think this is accurate? What would change in engineering culture if documentation was genuinely valued and celebrated rather than treated as an afterthought?

### Summary

- A complete **engineering notebook entry** includes the date, participants, problem, options considered, decision and rationale, results, and next steps — with sketches and data, not just text.
- **Engineering sketches** are quick ideation tools; **engineering drawings** are precise manufacturing documents — knowing which to use and when is an important skill.
- FIRST evaluates engineering notebooks for **evidence of the design process** — brainstorming, iteration, and reflection — not just polished final results.
- Effective teams use both **physical and digital documentation**: physical for quick capture, digital for collaboration, version control, and long-term storage.
- Setting **specific, evidence-based learning goals** transforms passive participation into intentional skill development throughout the program.

# WEEK 2: Simple Machines & Mechanical Advantage

**Duration:** 4-5 hours
**Learning Style:** Hands-on experimentation, calculation practice, mechanism analysis

Students explore the six simple machines that form the foundation of all mechanical systems, learn to calculate and apply mechanical advantage, and analyze how these principles appear in FRC robot mechanisms. This week builds the physics intuition needed to design effective, efficient robot mechanisms.

**Week 2 Learning Objectives:**
- Identify and describe the six types of simple machines
- Calculate mechanical advantage for levers, pulleys, gears, and wheels
- Analyze FRC mechanisms and identify their simple machine components
- Design intake and drive mechanisms using mechanical advantage principles
- Apply mechanical advantage concepts to solve real FRC design challenges

---

## Session 2.1: Simple Machines Foundation
**Duration:** 60 minutes | **Format:** Interactive exploration with hands-on examples

### Introduction

Every mechanism on your FRC robot — no matter how complex it looks — is built from combinations of six simple machines. Levers, wheels and axles, pulleys, inclined planes, wedges, and screws are the fundamental building blocks of all mechanical systems. Understanding them deeply is not academic trivia; it is the foundation of knowing why mechanisms work and how to make them work better.

Simple machines do one thing: they transform force or motion. They can make a small force produce a large force (at the cost of distance), or they can make a slow motion produce a fast motion (at the cost of force). This trade-off is universal in mechanics — you cannot get something for nothing, but you can get the force or speed you need by trading the other. This session builds that intuition through hands-on experimentation.

By the end of this session, you will be able to look at any FRC mechanism and identify which simple machines it uses, predict whether it prioritizes force or speed, and begin reasoning about how changing the design would change its performance.

### Core Concepts

**The Six Simple Machines**
The lever uses a rigid beam rotating about a fixed point (fulcrum) to multiply force or distance. The wheel and axle uses a large wheel connected to a small axle to multiply force or speed. The pulley redirects or multiplies force using a rope or belt over a wheel. The inclined plane reduces the force needed to lift an object by spreading the effort over a longer distance. The wedge converts force applied along its length into force perpendicular to its surface. The screw converts rotational force (torque) into linear force.

**Mechanical Advantage**
Mechanical advantage (MA) is the ratio of output force to input force. An MA greater than 1 means the machine multiplies force — you push with less force than the output force produced. An MA less than 1 means the machine multiplies speed — the output moves faster than the input, but with less force. MA = 1 means no force multiplication, just direction change. The formula is simple: MA = Output Force / Input Force, or equivalently, MA = Input Distance / Output Distance.

**Conservation of Energy in Simple Machines**
Simple machines do not create energy — they redirect it. The work input always equals the work output (in an ideal, frictionless machine). Work = Force × Distance, so if MA = 2 (you double the force), the output moves half the distance of the input. This is the fundamental trade-off: force versus distance. In FRC, this means a mechanism with high mechanical advantage can lift heavy game pieces but moves slowly; a mechanism with low MA moves fast but cannot handle heavy loads.

**Levers in FRC**
Levers appear everywhere in FRC robots. An arm mechanism is a lever with the pivot at one end. A shooter hood adjustment is a lever. Even the way a chassis frame transfers load to the wheels involves lever principles. The three classes of levers differ in the relative positions of the fulcrum, effort, and load: Class 1 (fulcrum between effort and load), Class 2 (load between fulcrum and effort), Class 3 (effort between fulcrum and load).

**Wheels, Axles, and Gears in FRC**
The wheel-and-axle principle is the basis of all gear systems. When you mesh two gears of different sizes, the larger gear moves more slowly but with more torque; the smaller gear moves faster with less torque. Gear ratio = teeth on driven gear / teeth on driving gear. A ratio greater than 1 is a reduction (more torque, less speed); a ratio less than 1 is an overdrive (less torque, more speed). FRC drivetrains use gear reductions to convert fast motor rotation into useful wheel torque.

### Video Lessons

#### Simple Machines Overview (~8 min)
**Channel:** CrashCourse Kids or Khan Academy
**Search:** "simple machines lever pulley wheel axle inclined plane overview" on YouTube
**What to watch for:** Focus on how each simple machine trades force for distance or vice versa. Try to think of one example of each machine you might find on an FRC robot as the video describes each one.

#### Mechanical Advantage in Robot Design (~6 min)
**Channel:** WPI Robotics or FRC team engineering channel
**Search:** "mechanical advantage FRC robot arm gears drivetrain explained" on YouTube
**What to watch for:** Notice how real FRC robots use gear reductions to get usable force from small motors. Pay attention to how designers choose their gear ratios based on the task — lifting versus shooting versus driving.

### Practice Exercises

**1. Simple Machine Scavenger Hunt**
Walk through your workspace and find at least one example of each of the six simple machines. For each one, write: (a) what machine type it is, (b) what task it performs, and (c) whether it trades force for speed or speed for force. If you cannot find examples in the workspace, use a photo of an FRC robot and complete the same task.

**2. Lever Lab**
Using a ruler as a lever and a pencil as a fulcrum, place a small weight (eraser, coin) on one end. Experiment with moving the fulcrum to three different positions. For each position, estimate how hard you must push to lift the weight and how far the effort end moves versus the load end. Calculate the mechanical advantage for each position and graph fulcrum position vs. mechanical advantage.

**3. Gear Ratio Calculation**
A motor spins at 5,000 RPM with a 10-tooth driving gear meshed to a 40-tooth driven gear. Calculate: (a) the gear ratio, (b) the output RPM, and (c) if the motor produces 1 Nm of torque, what is the output torque? Then design a two-stage gear reduction that takes the same motor from 5,000 RPM to approximately 100 RPM. Show all work.

**4. Simple Machine Combination Challenge**
Design on paper a mechanism that uses at least three different simple machines to accomplish this task: "Pick up a ball from floor level and place it on a shelf 24 inches high." Label each simple machine used, identify whether each provides force advantage or speed advantage, and explain why you chose each one.

### Knowledge Check

**1.** Which simple machine converts rotational force (torque) into linear force?
- A) Lever
- B) Pulley
- C) Screw
- D) Inclined plane

**Answer:** C

**2.** What does a mechanical advantage of 3 mean?
- A) The machine moves 3 times faster than input
- B) The output force is 3 times the input force
- C) The machine uses 3 different simple machines
- D) The efficiency of the machine is 3%

**Answer:** B

**3.** If mechanical advantage = 4, how far must the input move to produce 1 inch of output movement?
- A) 1 inch
- B) 2 inches
- C) 4 inches
- D) 0.25 inches

**Answer:** C

**4.** In a gear reduction, which gear is the driven gear?
- A) The smaller gear
- B) The larger gear
- C) Both gears are the same size in a reduction
- D) It depends on the material of the gears

**Answer:** B

**5.** A 10-tooth gear drives a 50-tooth gear. What is the gear ratio?
- A) 0.2
- B) 10
- C) 5
- D) 50

**Answer:** C

**6.** Which class of lever has the fulcrum between the effort and the load?
- A) Class 1
- B) Class 2
- C) Class 3
- D) Class 4

**Answer:** A

**7.** Why can simple machines not create energy?
- A) They are made of materials that absorb energy
- B) Conservation of energy requires that work input always equals work output minus friction losses
- C) Simple machines are not efficient enough to create energy
- D) Only complex machines can create energy

**Answer:** B

**8.** An FRC arm mechanism lifts slowly but handles very heavy game pieces. This indicates it has:
- A) A low mechanical advantage
- B) A high mechanical advantage
- C) A mechanical advantage of exactly 1
- D) No mechanical advantage

**Answer:** B

**9.** What is the formula for calculating gear ratio when two gears are meshed?
- A) Gear Ratio = Driving Gear Teeth / Driven Gear Teeth
- B) Gear Ratio = Driven Gear Teeth / Driving Gear Teeth
- C) Gear Ratio = Driving RPM × Driven RPM
- D) Gear Ratio = Driving Gear Diameter + Driven Gear Diameter

**Answer:** B

**10.** Which simple machine is the basis of all gear systems?
- A) Lever
- B) Pulley
- C) Wheel and axle
- D) Inclined plane

**Answer:** C

### Reflections

1. A motor spinning at 5,000 RPM getting reduced to 100 RPM represents a 50:1 reduction. Why would a robot drivetrain need such a large reduction? What would happen if you connected a motor directly to a wheel with no gear reduction at all?

2. The conservation of energy tells us simple machines trade force for distance. In FRC, would you rather have more force or more speed in your drivetrain? Does your answer change depending on the game challenge? Describe a game scenario where each would be the right priority.

3. Friction is not included in simple machine ideal calculations, but it always exists in real mechanisms. Where do you think friction has the biggest impact in FRC robots — and how might a designer try to reduce it?

4. You found six simple machines in your workspace. Were any hidden inside other mechanisms? This is common in engineering — complexity emerges from combinations of simple fundamentals. What does this tell you about how to approach analyzing a complex FRC mechanism you have never seen before?

### Summary

- The **six simple machines** — lever, wheel and axle, pulley, inclined plane, wedge, and screw — are the building blocks of all mechanical systems including every FRC robot.
- **Mechanical advantage** is the ratio of output force to input force; MA > 1 multiplies force, MA < 1 multiplies speed, and this trade-off is universal in mechanics.
- **Conservation of energy** means simple machines never create energy — if force is doubled, the output distance is halved correspondingly.
- **Gear ratio** = driven gear teeth / driving gear teeth; ratios greater than 1 reduce speed and increase torque, essential for FRC drivetrains and lifting mechanisms.
- All FRC mechanisms combine multiple simple machines — learning to identify them in complex assemblies is the foundation of mechanism analysis and design.

---

## Session 2.2: Mechanical Advantage Calculations
**Duration:** 60 minutes | **Format:** Calculation workshop with FRC applications

### Introduction

Understanding mechanical advantage conceptually is the first step. Being able to calculate it precisely is what allows you to design mechanisms that work as intended. In this session, you will practice the calculations that FRC mechanical engineers use daily: gear ratios, belt and chain drive ratios, speed and torque conversions, and multi-stage power transmission.

These calculations are not just math exercises — they are the tool that lets you predict whether your mechanism will be fast enough or strong enough before you build it. A team that calculates their drivetrain gear ratio ends up with a robot that drives as intended. A team that guesses often ends up either too slow to play the game effectively or too fast to control with precision.

By the end of this session, you will be able to take a motor specification, a desired output speed or torque, and calculate the exact gear or sprocket sizes needed. This is a skill you will use in every mechanism design throughout the program.

### Core Concepts

**Torque and Speed in Motors**
Electric motors produce torque (rotational force, measured in Newton-meters or inch-pounds) and spin at a certain RPM (revolutions per minute). Every FRC motor has a torque-speed curve: at maximum load (stall), the motor produces maximum torque but zero speed; at no load (free spin), the motor produces maximum speed but zero torque. The operating point in real use falls between these extremes. Gear reductions shift the operating point toward higher torque and lower speed.

**Single-Stage Gear and Sprocket Ratios**
For a single pair of gears or sprockets: Gear Ratio = Driven Teeth / Driver Teeth. Output RPM = Input RPM / Gear Ratio. Output Torque = Input Torque × Gear Ratio × Efficiency. Efficiency accounts for friction losses — a well-lubricated gear mesh is approximately 98% efficient; a chain drive is approximately 98-99% efficient per stage.

**Multi-Stage Power Transmission**
Real FRC gearboxes use multiple stages to achieve large overall reductions. The overall gear ratio of a multi-stage system is the product of the individual stage ratios: Overall Ratio = Ratio1 × Ratio2 × Ratio3. This allows large reductions using reasonably-sized gears — a 100:1 reduction through three stages of ~4.6:1 each rather than one impractically large single-stage gear pair.

**Belt and Chain Drive Calculations**
Belts and chains work identically to gears in ratio calculations, but based on pulley/sprocket diameter or tooth count: Ratio = Driven Pulley Diameter / Driver Pulley Diameter (belts) or Ratio = Driven Sprocket Teeth / Driver Sprocket Teeth (chain). Belt and chain drives allow power transmission over a distance, useful for routing power from a motor to a mechanism that cannot be directly adjacent.

**Free Speed Calculation for FRC Drivetrains**
FRC teams calculate theoretical free speed to predict robot top speed: Free Speed (ft/s) = (Motor Free RPM / Overall Gear Ratio) × (Wheel Circumference in feet). For 6-inch wheels (circumference = π × 0.5 ft ≈ 1.57 ft), a motor at 5,000 RPM, and an 8:1 overall ratio: Speed = (5,000/8) × 1.57 ≈ 981 ft/min ≈ 16.4 ft/s. Typical FRC drivetrain speeds range from 10-18 ft/s depending on game requirements.

### Video Lessons

#### FRC Drivetrain Gear Ratio Calculations (~10 min)
**Channel:** FRC team technical channel or WPI robotics
**Search:** "FRC drivetrain gear ratio calculation free speed torque explained" on YouTube
**What to watch for:** Follow along with the calculations and try to compute the answers before the video reveals them. Notice how changing the gear ratio affects both speed and pushing force, and how teams decide which to optimize for a specific game.

#### Understanding Motor Torque-Speed Curves (~6 min)
**Channel:** Engineering Explained or motor technology channel
**Search:** "electric motor torque speed curve stall free speed explained" on YouTube
**What to watch for:** Focus on the relationship between torque and speed — when one is high, the other is low. Think about where on the torque-speed curve an FRC motor should ideally operate for maximum power output.

### Practice Exercises

**1. Single-Stage Ratio Practice**
Complete these calculations showing all work: (a) Driver sprocket: 16 teeth, Driven sprocket: 48 teeth — calculate ratio, output RPM if input is 6,000 RPM, and output torque if input torque is 0.5 Nm at 98% efficiency. (b) Driver pulley: 1-inch diameter, Driven pulley: 3.5-inch diameter — calculate ratio and output RPM if input is 4,000 RPM.

**2. Multi-Stage Gearbox Design**
Design a two-stage gearbox reducing 6,000 RPM to approximately 200 RPM. Available sprocket sizes: 12, 16, 18, 24, 32, 48 teeth. Choose two stages that get as close to 200 RPM as possible. Calculate the exact output RPM, overall ratio, and output torque if motor produces 0.4 Nm at 97% total efficiency.

**3. Drivetrain Free Speed Calculation**
Your team uses 4-inch wheels, a motor with 5,880 RPM free speed, and a gearbox with 8.45:1 ratio. Calculate: (a) theoretical free speed in ft/s, (b) whether this meets a 14 ft/s target for a fast-paced game, (c) what single gear ratio change would bring you closest to 14 ft/s, and (d) how that change affects pushing force.

**4. Mechanism Torque Requirement**
An arm must hold a 5 lb game piece at the end of an 18-inch arm. Motor produces 0.8 Nm of torque. Calculate: (a) the torque required to hold the game piece (convert 5 lb at 18 inches to Nm), (b) the minimum gear ratio needed, and (c) whether a single 3:1 reduction is sufficient.

### Knowledge Check

**1.** A motor produces 1,000 RPM connected to a 4:1 gear reduction. What is the output RPM?
- A) 4,000 RPM
- B) 250 RPM
- C) 1,000 RPM
- D) 400 RPM

**Answer:** B

**2.** A motor produces 0.5 Nm through a 10:1 reduction at 95% efficiency. What is output torque?
- A) 0.5 Nm
- B) 5.0 Nm
- C) 4.75 Nm
- D) 50 Nm

**Answer:** C

**3.** A two-stage gearbox has Stage 1 ratio of 3:1 and Stage 2 ratio of 4:1. Overall ratio?
- A) 7:1
- B) 1.33:1
- C) 12:1
- D) 3.5:1

**Answer:** C

**4.** For a belt drive, how is the ratio calculated?
- A) Driver belt length / Driven belt length
- B) Driven pulley diameter / Driver pulley diameter
- C) Driver pulley diameter / Driven pulley diameter
- D) Sum of both pulley diameters

**Answer:** B

**5.** Where on the torque-speed curve does a motor produce maximum power output?
- A) At stall (zero speed)
- B) At free speed (zero torque)
- C) At approximately the midpoint between stall and free speed
- D) Power output is constant across the entire curve

**Answer:** C

**6.** A robot has 6-inch wheels, motor at 6,000 RPM free speed, and 10:1 drive ratio. Approximate free speed?
- A) About 9.4 ft/s
- B) About 15.7 ft/s
- C) About 3.1 ft/s
- D) About 94 ft/s

**Answer:** A

**7.** Why do real FRC gearboxes use multiple reduction stages rather than one large reduction?
- A) Multiple stages are required by FRC rules
- B) A single large reduction requires impractically large gears; multiple stages achieve large ratios with reasonably-sized components
- C) Multiple stages are always more efficient
- D) Single-stage reductions cannot exceed 3:1

**Answer:** B

**8.** A chain drive has a 15-tooth driver sprocket and 45-tooth driven sprocket. Gear ratio?
- A) 0.33:1
- B) 15:1
- C) 3:1
- D) 45:1

**Answer:** C

**9.** To get more pushing force (higher stall torque at the wheels), you should:
- A) Increase the gear ratio (more reduction)
- B) Decrease the gear ratio (less reduction)
- C) Use larger diameter wheels
- D) Increase motor voltage

**Answer:** A

**10.** What happens to output torque when you increase gear reduction ratio?
- A) Output torque decreases proportionally
- B) Output torque increases proportionally minus efficiency losses
- C) Output torque is unaffected by gear ratio
- D) Output torque doubles regardless of ratio

**Answer:** B

### Reflections

1. You calculated that increasing gear ratio increases torque but decreases speed. FRC games vary widely in their demands — some favor fast robots, others favor powerful pushers. How would you decide what gear ratio to use for a specific game? What information from the game manual would guide that decision?

2. The torque-speed curve shows motors produce the most power at approximately half their free speed. Most FRC drivetrains aim to run motors near this point. Why is this important? What would happen to a robot whose gear ratio kept the motor running near stall speed continuously?

3. In the multi-stage gearbox exercise, were you able to hit exactly 200 RPM? What does this tell you about real engineering design — the gap between target values and achievable values with discrete component sizes?

4. Multi-stage gearboxes introduce more friction losses than single-stage. If each stage loses 2% to friction, a three-stage gearbox loses about 6%. Is this a significant concern for FRC applications? At what point does efficiency loss become a problem worth solving differently?

### Summary

- **Single-stage ratio** = Driven Teeth / Driver Teeth; output RPM = Input RPM / Ratio; output torque = Input Torque × Ratio × Efficiency.
- **Multi-stage systems** multiply individual stage ratios together — three stages of 5:1 each produce an overall 125:1 reduction with reasonably-sized components.
- **Free speed** for a drivetrain is: (Motor RPM / Gear Ratio) × Wheel Circumference — giving the robot's theoretical top speed before efficiency losses.
- Increasing **gear reduction** increases output torque and decreases output speed — this trade-off is the central calculation in all FRC mechanism design.
- Every gear, belt, and chain stage introduces **efficiency losses** (typically 1-3% per stage) that must be accounted for in power budget calculations.
