## Session 2.3: FRC Mechanisms Analysis
**Duration:** 60 minutes | **Format:** Case study analysis with hands-on inspection

### Introduction

The best way to learn mechanism design is to study mechanisms that already work. FRC has 30+ years of competition history, and the best mechanisms from those competitions represent some of the most creative and effective mechanical engineering done by high school students anywhere in the world. This session teaches you to analyze existing FRC mechanisms systematically — to look at a robot and understand not just what it does, but why each design decision was made.

Mechanism analysis is a skill every experienced engineer uses. When a professional engineer encounters a new design challenge, they research existing solutions first. They study what has been tried, what worked, what failed, and why. This research prevents reinventing the wheel and reveals insights that would take years of trial and error to discover independently.

By the end of this session, you will be able to analyze any FRC mechanism, identify its component simple machines, calculate its mechanical advantage, evaluate its trade-offs, and articulate what design decisions the original engineers made and why.

### Core Concepts

**The Four Questions of Mechanism Analysis**
When analyzing any FRC mechanism, ask four questions in sequence: (1) What exactly does this mechanism do? Be specific — not "it shoots" but "it accelerates a 6-inch foam ball to approximately 30 ft/s with a flywheel system." (2) What simple machines does it use? Identify every lever, gear, belt, wheel-and-axle. (3) What are the key design trade-offs? Where did the designer choose force over speed, or simplicity over capability? (4) What would you change and why? Based on the mechanism's performance, what are its weaknesses and how might they be addressed?

**Drivetrain Mechanism Types**
FRC drivetrains fall into several categories. Tank drive (skid steer) uses independent left and right sides for differential steering — simple, reliable, great pushing power, cannot strafe. Mecanum drive uses angled rollers on wheels to enable strafing — more complex, less efficient. Swerve drive uses independently steered wheel modules for full omnidirectional movement — maximum maneuverability, highest mechanical complexity and cost. Understanding these trade-offs helps teams choose the right drivetrain for a specific game.

**Intake Mechanism Types**
Game piece collection mechanisms follow several patterns. Roller intakes use spinning compliant rollers to pull game pieces in — effective for balls and similar objects, simple and reliable. Claw intakes use pneumatic or motor-driven jaws to grip game pieces — effective for cubes, discs, and irregular shapes. Conveyor intakes use a moving belt or chain with paddles — effective for high-volume cycling. Each type excels in specific scenarios.

**Scoring Mechanism Types**
FRC scoring mechanisms range from simple placement to high-speed launching. Elevator mechanisms provide linear vertical travel for placing game pieces at height — reliable, repeatable, precise. Arm mechanisms provide rotational travel — simpler than elevators but height changes non-linearly with angle. Flywheel shooters launch game pieces by pinching between spinning wheels — high speed, excellent for launching, difficult to aim precisely. Selecting the right type requires matching mechanism characteristics to game demands.

**Reliability as a Design Criterion**
A mechanism that works 99% of the time is vastly more valuable than one that works perfectly on a perfect cycle but jams 20% of the time. FRC competition is won on reliability — a robot that consistently scores in an efficient, boring way will beat a spectacular mechanism that breaks regularly. When analyzing mechanisms, always ask: "What are the failure modes?" A failure mode is any scenario where the mechanism does not perform as intended. Experienced designers think about failure modes before building.

### Video Lessons

#### Behind the Bumpers: FRC Robot Analysis (~12 min)
**Channel:** FRC Behind the Bumpers series
**Search:** "Behind the Bumpers FRC robot mechanism breakdown interview" on YouTube
**What to watch for:** Listen for the moments where designers explain why they chose one approach over another. These are the trade-off decisions you are learning to identify. Notice how often reliability is mentioned as a design priority over raw capability.

#### FRC Drivetrain Comparison Guide (~8 min)
**Channel:** FRC technical education channel
**Search:** "FRC drivetrain comparison tank swerve mecanum advantages disadvantages" on YouTube
**What to watch for:** Make a simple comparison table as you watch — list tank, mecanum, and swerve, and for each note the key advantage and key disadvantage. Think about which drivetrain type would be best for different game scenarios.

### Practice Exercises

**1. Mechanism Identification Chart**
Find photographs of five different FRC robots from different years (search "FRC robot [year] reveal" on YouTube or Google Images). For each robot, complete a table: Robot Year/Team, Drive Type, Primary Intake Type, Primary Scoring Mechanism, One Observed Trade-off. This builds your library of mechanism examples.

**2. Four-Question Analysis**
Choose one robot from Exercise 1 and apply all four analysis questions in depth: (1) Exactly what task does the main mechanism perform? (2) List all simple machines you can identify. (3) What are the two most significant design trade-offs? (4) What would you change and why? Write at least two sentences for each question.

**3. Failure Mode Analysis**
Choose a simple FRC mechanism (roller intake). Brainstorm as many failure modes as you can — situations where it would not work as intended. For each failure mode, rate: How likely? How severe would the impact be? What design change could prevent it? This is a simplified Failure Mode and Effects Analysis (FMEA) — a real engineering tool used in safety-critical systems.

**4. Mechanism Trade-off Debate**
In pairs, debate: "A swerve drive is always better than a tank drive in FRC." One person argues for, one against. Use specific technical arguments about mechanical advantage, reliability, manufacturing difficulty, and game-specific scenarios. After 5 minutes, switch sides. This teaches you to understand both sides of an engineering trade-off — critical for design reviews.

### Knowledge Check

**1.** What are the four questions of mechanism analysis in order?
- A) Build, Test, Analyze, Improve
- B) What does it do, what simple machines does it use, what are the trade-offs, what would you change?
- C) Cost, weight, speed, reliability
- D) Input, process, output, feedback

**Answer:** B

**2.** What is the primary advantage of a tank drive over mecanum drive?
- A) Tank drives can strafe sideways
- B) Tank drives are simpler, more reliable, and provide better pushing power
- C) Tank drives have a lower center of gravity
- D) Tank drives are required by FRC rules for rookie teams

**Answer:** B

**3.** What does "compliance" mean in the context of a roller intake?
- A) The intake complies with FRC rules
- B) The rollers are made of soft, flexible material that deforms to grip different game piece sizes
- C) The intake has been approved by a safety inspector
- D) The rollers spin at a consistent speed

**Answer:** B

**4.** Why is an elevator mechanism more repeatable than an arm for placing game pieces at a target height?
- A) Elevators are always faster than arms
- B) An elevator's height corresponds directly to its linear position; an arm's effective height changes non-linearly with angle
- C) Elevators use less motor power than arms
- D) Elevators are simpler to manufacture

**Answer:** B

**5.** A flywheel shooter is best suited for which scoring scenario?
- A) Precisely placing a game piece on a narrow peg
- B) Launching a game piece at high speed toward a distant target
- C) Stacking game pieces in a vertical column
- D) Collecting game pieces from the floor

**Answer:** B

**6.** What is a "failure mode" in engineering?
- A) A design approach rejected during brainstorming
- B) Any scenario in which a mechanism does not perform as intended
- C) A calculation error in the design process
- D) A component that breaks during manufacturing

**Answer:** B

**7.** Why does a 99%-reliable mechanism often outperform a spectacular but 80%-reliable one in competition?
- A) Judges award bonus points for consistency
- B) Reliable scoring accumulates more total points than inconsistent peak performance
- C) FRC rules penalize mechanisms that fail during matches
- D) Perfect cycle mechanisms are too complex to maintain

**Answer:** B

**8.** What is the primary purpose of a Failure Mode and Effects Analysis (FMEA)?
- A) A post-competition report on what broke during matches
- B) Systematically identifying potential failure modes, rating their likelihood and severity, and defining preventive design changes
- C) Analyzing other teams' robot failures to avoid copying them
- D) A quality inspection performed after manufacturing

**Answer:** B

**9.** Why is it valuable to argue BOTH sides of a technical design debate?
- A) It helps students practice public speaking
- B) Understanding the strongest arguments on both sides leads to better-informed design decisions
- C) FRC rules require teams to consider multiple options
- D) It saves time compared to researching the topic independently

**Answer:** B

**10.** When a swerve drive is described as "omnidirectional," what does that mean?
- A) The robot can drive on any surface type
- B) The robot can move in any direction without rotating the chassis first
- C) The drive can be programmed in any language
- D) The robot can operate in any FRC game

**Answer:** B

### Reflections

1. After analyzing five FRC robots from different years, what patterns did you notice? Are there mechanism types that appear repeatedly across different games? What does this suggest about convergent engineering solutions?

2. The session emphasized that reliability often beats capability in FRC. Can you think of a situation where this is NOT true — where higher capability but lower reliability would be the right choice? What game conditions would create that scenario?

3. During the trade-off debate on swerve vs. tank drive, what was the strongest argument on each side? Did arguing the opposite of your initial opinion change your thinking at all?

4. Failure mode analysis identified ways your chosen mechanism could fail. Which failure mode surprised you most? What does that surprise reveal about your assumptions going into the analysis?

### Summary

- The **four questions of mechanism analysis** — what does it do, what simple machines does it use, what are the trade-offs, what would you change — provide a systematic framework for understanding any FRC mechanism.
- **Drivetrain types** (tank, mecanum, swerve) each have distinct trade-offs in maneuverability, complexity, reliability, and cost that must be matched to game requirements.
- **Intake types** (roller, claw, conveyor) and **scoring types** (elevator, arm, flywheel) each excel in specific scenarios — selecting the right type requires analyzing game demands.
- **Failure mode analysis** (FMEA) identifies potential failure scenarios before building, allowing designers to prevent problems proactively rather than reactively.
- **Reliability** is often more valuable than raw capability in FRC competition — consistent performance accumulates more points than spectacular mechanisms that fail regularly.

---

## Session 2.4: Intake Systems & Drive Mechanisms
**Duration:** 60 minutes | **Format:** Design workshop with prototype building

### Introduction

Now it is time to apply everything you have learned about simple machines, mechanical advantage, and mechanism analysis to actual design work. In this session, you will design and begin building a simple intake mechanism and learn the specific engineering principles that make FRC drive mechanisms effective.

This session bridges the gap between calculation and construction. You know how to calculate gear ratios and identify simple machines — now you will make specific design decisions for a real mechanism: what gear ratio to use, what mechanism type to choose, and how to implement it with available materials. These decisions require integrating multiple considerations simultaneously: game requirements, available components, time constraints, and manufacturing capability.

The intake you sketch and begin building today will serve as your test platform for the mechanism design challenge in Session 2.5. Do not aim for perfection — aim for something that works and teaches you something specific.

### Core Concepts

**Roller Intake Design Principles**
Effective roller intakes follow several principles. Contact surface matters — compliant materials (rubber, foam, surgical tubing wrapped around a roller) grip game pieces better than hard surfaces. Roller surface speed determines intake speed — faster linear velocity at the roller surface means faster collection, but too fast can damage or overshoot game pieces. Multiple rollers create a geometry that guides game pieces inward. The intake opening should be wider than the game piece with closing geometry that ensures reliable capture.

**Drive Mechanism Engineering**
FRC drive mechanisms must balance several competing requirements. Speed determines how quickly the robot crosses the field — a 54-foot field at 15 ft/s takes 3.6 seconds to cross. Pushing force determines how well the robot defends or resists defense, requiring high torque and high gear reduction. Maneuverability determines how quickly the robot changes direction, depending on turning radius and wheel grip. Most FRC games favor 12-16 ft/s as a balanced target speed for tank drivetrains.

**Wheel Selection for Drivetrains**
Wheel type significantly affects drivetrain behavior. Traction wheels (rubber, high-friction) provide maximum pushing force but resist sideways sliding. Omni wheels (rollers perpendicular to the wheel direction) allow free sideways sliding — often used on the corners of a four-wheel tank drive to reduce turning scrubbing. Mecanum wheels use angled rollers to enable strafing at the cost of pushing force. Pneumatic wheels absorb shock but add weight and flex.

**Motor Selection and Matching**
FRC rules allow specific motors for specific applications. Drive motors need high power output and reasonable free speed — the NEO, Falcon 500, and Kraken X60 are current top choices. Intake motors need high free speed and reasonable torque for spinning rollers. Arm motors need high torque and low speed. Matching motor characteristics to mechanism requirements — not just picking the most powerful motor available — is disciplined engineering practice.

**Simplicity as a Design Virtue**
The most reliable FRC mechanisms are often the simplest. Every additional component adds weight, potential failure points, manufacturing time, and complexity. Before adding any component to a mechanism, ask: "What problem does this solve? Could I achieve the same result with fewer parts?" Championship-winning teams routinely build mechanisms that look surprisingly simple — because simplicity is reliability by design.

### Video Lessons

#### FRC Intake Mechanism Design Principles (~8 min)
**Channel:** FRC team design breakdown channel
**Search:** "FRC intake mechanism design roller compliant wheels geometry" on YouTube
**What to watch for:** Focus on how designers choose the contact geometry — the shape of the intake opening, roller spacing, and angle of approach. Notice how the funnel shape ensures reliable game piece capture even when the driver approaches from slightly off-angle.

#### FRC Drivetrain Selection Guide (~7 min)
**Channel:** FRC team or robotics education channel
**Search:** "FRC drivetrain selection guide speed torque wheel type comparison" on YouTube
**What to watch for:** Pay attention to how experienced designers calculate their target free speed and work backward to choose gear ratios. Notice whether they prioritize speed or pushing force, and what game characteristics drove that decision.

### Practice Exercises

**1. Intake Design for a Specific Game Piece**
Design a roller intake on paper for collecting a 9.5-inch diameter foam ring. Your design must specify: (a) roller diameter and material, (b) motor selection and gear ratio, (c) intake opening geometry with funnel angle, and (d) how it connects to the robot chassis. Use a dimensioned sketch with labels.

**2. Drivetrain Specification Calculation**
Calculate a complete drivetrain specification: Target free speed = 15 ft/s, Wheel diameter = 4 inches, Motor = 6,380 RPM free speed. Find the gear ratio needed. Then choose from available sprocket sizes (12, 15, 18, 22, 32, 48 teeth) to build a two-stage drive system as close to your target ratio as possible. Show all calculations.

**3. Rapid Intake Prototype**
Using available materials (pool noodle, PVC pipe, rubber tubing, or similar), build a simple hand-powered intake roller. Hold the roller against a tennis ball and spin it by hand — does it pull the ball in reliably? Experiment with different contact materials and speeds. Document: what material gripped best? What surface speed worked best?

**4. Drive System Comparison Table**
Create a comparison table for three drivetrain options for a game where robots quickly collect balls scattered across the field and shoot from a fixed distance. Compare tank drive, mecanum, and swerve on: speed capability, ball collection compatibility, maneuverability, estimated build time, and reliability estimate. Make a final recommendation with justification.

### Knowledge Check

**1.** What material property makes compliant rollers effective for intake mechanisms?
- A) High hardness to withstand impact
- B) Flexibility that allows deformation to grip game pieces of varying sizes
- C) High electrical conductivity for sensing
- D) Low density to minimize mechanism weight

**Answer:** B

**2.** What is "surface speed" of a roller intake and why does it matter?
- A) The speed the robot drives across the field
- B) The linear velocity at the roller surface — determines how fast game pieces are pulled in
- C) The rotational speed of the intake motor in RPM
- D) The speed at which game pieces exit into storage

**Answer:** B

**3.** Why do many FRC four-wheel tank drivetrains use omni wheels on corner positions?
- A) Omni wheels provide more traction than traction wheels
- B) Omni wheels allow free lateral sliding, reducing scrubbing and turning resistance
- C) Omni wheels are required at corners by FRC rules
- D) Omni wheels are lighter than traction wheels

**Answer:** B

**4.** A robot driving at 15 ft/s crosses a 54-foot FRC field in approximately how long?
- A) 5.4 seconds
- B) 3.6 seconds
- C) 1.5 seconds
- D) 8.1 seconds

**Answer:** B

**5.** What is the primary advantage of selecting a motor that matches mechanism requirements?
- A) More powerful motors are always more expensive
- B) Matched motors operate efficiently near their peak power point; oversized motors operate inefficiently and waste current
- C) FRC rules limit total motor power per mechanism
- D) Matched motors are lighter and smaller

**Answer:** B

**6.** In an intake funnel design, why should the opening be wider than the game piece?
- A) Wider openings are easier to manufacture
- B) A wider opening allows game piece capture even when the robot approaches from slightly off-center
- C) Wider openings allow higher intake speed
- D) FRC rules require a minimum opening width

**Answer:** B

**7.** What does "simplicity as a design virtue" mean in FRC mechanism design?
- A) Simple designs are always better than complex ones in all cases
- B) Every additional component adds weight, failure points, and complexity — add components only when they solve a specific problem
- C) Student teams cannot manufacture complex mechanisms
- D) Simple designs are faster to build regardless of performance requirements

**Answer:** B

**8.** For a defense-heavy FRC game, which wheel type would you prioritize for your drivetrain?
- A) Mecanum wheels for strafing capability
- B) Traction wheels for maximum pushing force
- C) Omni wheels for reduced turning resistance
- D) Pneumatic wheels for shock absorption

**Answer:** B

**9.** How does the intake funnel shape improve reliability?
- A) It reduces the weight of the intake mechanism
- B) It guides game pieces from a wide entry zone into the capture position, compensating for imprecise robot positioning
- C) It increases the surface speed of the intake rollers
- D) It prevents game pieces from bouncing off the robot

**Answer:** B

**10.** What characteristic makes the Falcon 500, NEO, and Kraken X60 popular FRC drive motors?
- A) They are the cheapest motors available for FRC
- B) They offer high power output and reasonable free speed suitable for drivetrain gear ratios
- C) They are the lightest motors allowed by FRC rules
- D) They require no gearbox and connect directly to wheels

**Answer:** B

### Reflections

1. In Exercise 3, you experimented with different contact materials for your intake roller. What surprised you about how different materials performed? What physical property seemed to matter most — surface texture, stiffness, or something else?

2. You have now calculated drivetrain specifications mathematically. Before this session, did you think about robot speed in precise ft/s, or did you think "faster is better"? How does precise specification change the design process?

3. The session emphasized simplicity as a virtue. Think of the most complex FRC mechanism you have seen. Do you think its complexity was necessary? Was there a simpler approach that might have worked as well?

4. Motor selection involves matching the motor's torque-speed curve to the mechanism's load profile. Can you think of a non-engineering example of matching the tool to the task? What happens when tools and tasks are mismatched?

### Summary

- **Roller intake design** requires compliant contact materials, appropriate surface speed, and a funnel geometry that ensures reliable game piece capture even with imprecise robot positioning.
- **Drivetrain specification** starts with a target free speed (typically 12-16 ft/s for FRC), then works backward through gear ratio to wheel size and motor selection.
- **Wheel type** (traction, omni, mecanum) significantly affects drivetrain behavior — match the wheel type to the game's defense and maneuverability requirements.
- **Motor matching** means selecting motors whose torque-speed characteristics suit the mechanism's load, not always choosing the most powerful available motor.
- **Simplicity is reliability** — every additional component adds potential failure points; add complexity only when it solves a specific problem that simpler solutions cannot address.

---

## Session 2.5: Mechanism Design Challenge
**Duration:** 60 minutes | **Format:** Team design-build-test competition

### Introduction

This is your first real engineering challenge in the program. Everything you have learned about simple machines, mechanical advantage, mechanism types, and design principles comes together today. Your team will design, build, and test a mechanism to accomplish a specific game task — under time pressure, just like FRC build season.

Design challenges teach things that lectures cannot. You will discover that your first idea rarely works as planned. You will experience the pressure of a deadline approaching while your mechanism still does not function. You will make real trade-off decisions with imperfect information. And when something finally works — even a little — you will feel the specific satisfaction of engineering: transforming an idea into a physical system that does something useful.

This session also gives your mentor valuable information about where your team is technically, shaping the rest of the curriculum. Pay attention not just to whether your mechanism works, but to what you learn about your own engineering instincts in the process.

### Core Concepts

**Challenge Definition and Constraints**
Clear challenge definition is the first engineering task. Before picking up any material, your team must agree on: exactly what the mechanism must do, what constraints apply (size, materials, time), and how success will be measured. Vague challenges produce vague designs. The discipline of writing a clear challenge statement — even for a simple exercise — is one of the most valuable engineering habits you can develop.

**Rapid Prototyping Mindset**
In a 60-minute challenge, you cannot afford to design comprehensively before building. Rapid prototyping means making the minimum viable version of your concept as quickly as possible, testing it, and learning what changes are needed. The first version will be wrong in some way — the goal is to discover that wrongness as early as possible so you have time to fix it.

**Division of Labor Under Pressure**
When time is short, teams that divide work effectively outperform teams that crowd around a single task. One person designs the mounting while another builds the roller mechanism and a third prepares materials. Clear communication about interfaces — how the parts connect — is essential. Building two pieces that should connect but do not fit wastes precious time.

**Test-Driven Iteration**
Define your test criteria before you start building: What does success look like? How will you measure it? Tests should be repeatable so you can track improvement across iterations. After each test, the whole team should discuss observations before deciding what to change — combined observations almost always reveal more than any individual noticed alone.

**Debrief and Reflection**
The debrief after the challenge is as valuable as the challenge itself. What did you plan versus what actually happened? Where did you lose time? What would you do differently with a second chance? Engineering culture values honest post-mortems — the ability to analyze failures candidly, without defensiveness, is what separates teams that improve from those that repeat the same mistakes.

### Video Lessons

#### FRC Prototyping Techniques and Best Practices (~8 min)
**Channel:** FRC team prototyping video
**Search:** "FRC prototyping techniques rapid build cardboard wood roller" on YouTube
**What to watch for:** Notice the materials and methods experienced FRC teams use for rapid prototyping — not final materials, but materials that are fast to work with. Observe how quickly teams move from concept to testable prototype, and what they learn from early, imperfect tests.

#### Engineering Design Challenge Debrief (~5 min)
**Channel:** Engineering education or STEM challenge channel
**Search:** "engineering design challenge debrief lessons learned reflection process" on YouTube
**What to watch for:** Listen for how educators and engineers structure post-challenge reflection. What specific questions do they ask? Notice how the debrief focuses on process (what decisions were made and why) rather than just outcome (did it work or not).

### Practice Exercises

**1. Challenge Statement Writing**
Before building anything, write a precise challenge statement. Include: the task (what the mechanism must do), the constraints (size, materials, time limit, power source), and the success criteria (how you will measure whether it worked, including a minimum acceptable threshold and a stretch goal). Share with your mentor and revise until it is unambiguous.

**2. Rapid Prototyping Sprint**
Using only provided materials (typically cardboard, tape, rubber bands, wooden dowels, pool noodles, and hand tools), build your mechanism in 30 minutes. Focus on core function — do not spend time on aesthetics or polish. Test against success criteria from Exercise 1. Document: Did it work? What was the first failure mode you encountered?

**3. Iteration Round**
Based on your test, make ONE specific change to address the most significant failure mode. This change must be hypothesis-driven: write "I believe [change] will improve [specific aspect] because [reason]" before implementing it. Test again. Measure whether the hypothesis was confirmed. Document the result in your iteration log.

**4. Team Debrief**
As a team, answer these questions in writing: (1) What was your original design concept? (2) What did you actually build in the first iteration? (3) What was the biggest gap between your plan and reality? (4) What would you do differently in the first 5 minutes? (5) What was the most important thing you learned about mechanism design today?

### Knowledge Check

**1.** Why must success criteria be defined BEFORE building begins?
- A) It is required by the challenge rules
- B) Pre-defined criteria enable objective evaluation and prevent adjusting standards after seeing results
- C) Success criteria are only useful for award submissions
- D) Building should begin before criteria so the design can evolve naturally

**Answer:** B

**2.** What is the core principle of the rapid prototyping mindset?
- A) Build the most complete and polished version possible
- B) Create the minimum viable version as quickly as possible to discover problems early
- C) Plan comprehensively before touching any materials
- D) Use only final-quality materials even in early prototypes

**Answer:** B

**3.** What is a hypothesis-driven change in prototype iteration?
- A) Changing multiple things at once to save time
- B) Stating "I believe [change] will improve [outcome] because [reason]" before implementing — creating a testable prediction
- C) Having an expert guess what change is needed
- D) Making the most obvious change without explaining why

**Answer:** B

**4.** How should teams divide work during a time-limited design challenge?
- A) Everyone works on the same component to ensure quality
- B) Assign parallel workstreams with clear interface requirements between them
- C) The most experienced person builds while others observe
- D) Work sequentially — complete one phase before starting the next

**Answer:** B

**5.** What is the purpose of a post-challenge debrief?
- A) To assign blame for what went wrong
- B) To honestly analyze the gap between plan and reality, identify lessons learned, and determine what to do differently
- C) To celebrate success and ignore failures
- D) To formally document the challenge for competition award submissions

**Answer:** B

**6.** What is the most important question to ask after a prototype test?
- A) Why didn't it work perfectly?
- B) Not just "did it work" but "what did we learn about why it worked or didn't?"
- C) How long did it take to build?
- D) How does it compare to other teams' prototypes?

**Answer:** B

**7.** In a 60-minute challenge, when should teams stop designing and start building?
- A) After at least 30 minutes of planning
- B) As soon as a testable concept exists — even if the design is incomplete
- C) Only when the design is fully detailed
- D) At the 45-minute mark regardless of planning status

**Answer:** B

**8.** Which is the best example of a clear success criterion?
- A) "The mechanism should work well"
- B) "The mechanism collects at least 8 out of 10 game pieces placed at floor level within 5 seconds each"
- C) "The mechanism is fast and reliable"
- D) "The mechanism impresses the mentor"

**Answer:** B

**9.** Why does a debrief focus on PROCESS rather than just OUTCOME?
- A) Outcomes are too difficult to measure objectively
- B) Process analysis reveals replicable lessons; outcome analysis only reveals whether this specific attempt succeeded
- C) Mentors are not qualified to evaluate outcomes
- D) FRC judges only evaluate process, not outcomes

**Answer:** B

**10.** What does "minimum viable version" mean in rapid prototyping?
- A) The cheapest version possible
- B) The simplest version that tests the core function without unnecessary features
- C) The version with the minimum number of parts
- D) The version built in the minimum amount of time

**Answer:** B

### Reflections

1. At what moment during today's challenge did you feel most uncertain? What did you do in response to that uncertainty — and was it the right call in hindsight? What would a more experienced engineer have done in that moment?

2. Your team had to divide work under time pressure. Did the division work well? What miscommunications occurred at the interfaces between different workstreams? How would you structure the division differently next time?

3. Compare your challenge debrief answers from Exercise 4 to your original challenge statement from Exercise 1. What was the biggest gap between your plan and execution? Is that gap typical for engineering projects, or was it unusually large?

4. You experienced the rapid prototyping mindset for the first time. How did it feel to build something intentionally imperfect? What mental barriers did you have to overcome? What would help you enter that mindset faster in future sessions?

### Summary

- **Clear challenge statements** with specific success criteria defined before building prevent teams from moving goalposts and enable objective evaluation of results.
- **Rapid prototyping** means building the minimum viable version as quickly as possible — the goal is to discover failures early, not to build a perfect product on the first try.
- **Hypothesis-driven iteration** means stating a specific prediction before each change so that test results either confirm or refute your engineering understanding.
- **Division of labor** with clear interface requirements allows parallel workstreams that make better use of limited time than sequential, crowded construction.
- **Post-challenge debriefs** focused on process rather than outcome generate the replicable lessons that compound into engineering expertise over time.

# WEEK 3: CAD Fundamentals & Digital Design

**Duration:** 4-5 hours
**Learning Style:** Visual + Hands-On Digital Practice

CAD (Computer-Aided Design) software is the language modern engineers use to communicate designs before a single piece of metal is cut. In FRC, teams that master CAD can simulate mechanisms, catch interference problems digitally, and hand fabricators precise drawings that eliminate guesswork. This week you will learn Onshape, the free browser-based CAD platform adopted by hundreds of FRC teams worldwide, starting from sketching fundamentals and building to full assemblies.

**Week 3 Learning Objectives:**
- Navigate the Onshape interface and create a new part studio document
- Create constrained 2D sketches using lines, circles, arcs, and dimensions
- Apply 3D features (extrude, revolve, fillet, chamfer) to convert sketches into solid bodies
- Assemble multiple parts using mates and verify fit before fabrication
- Export a technical drawing with standard views, dimensions, and a title block

---

## Session 3.1: CAD Software Introduction
**Duration:** 60 minutes | **Format:** Guided exploration with hands-on setup

### Introduction

Before FRC teams cut a single piece of metal, they build their robot digitally. CAD software lets engineers catch problems that would otherwise cost hours of rework: a gearbox that won't fit, a tube that collides with a wheel, a bracket hole drilled in the wrong spot. Learning CAD is like learning a superpower — the ability to test ideas at the speed of thought rather than the speed of manufacturing.

Onshape is the CAD platform of choice for this curriculum because it runs entirely in a web browser, requires no installation, and is completely free for students. Many top FRC teams — including Hall of Fame and world-championship caliber programs — use Onshape for their entire robot design workflow.

**What You Will Learn:**
- How to create and navigate an Onshape account and document
- The purpose of each main workspace: Part Studio, Assembly, and Drawing
- How to orient and view a 3D model using the mouse and the view cube

### Core Concepts

#### What CAD Software Does and Why FRC Teams Use It
CAD software creates mathematical representations of physical objects. Every surface, edge, and feature exists as precise geometry defined by numbers — not as a picture, but as a model that knows its own dimensions. When you design a robot arm in CAD, the software knows that the tube is exactly 12.000 inches long and that the hole is exactly 0.201 inches in diameter for a 10-32 thread. That precision flows from digital model to fabricated part without introducing human measurement error.

In the FRC build season, time is the scarcest resource. A team that designs in CAD can have one student fabricating a bracket while another is still designing the mechanism that holds it — because the bracket dimensions were locked in digitally first. Teams can also run collision detection, which automatically finds places where two parts occupy the same space (an impossible condition in the physical world). Catching a collision in CAD takes thirty seconds to fix; catching it at 11 PM during week five of build season is a crisis.

Onshape adds a collaboration dimension that traditional CAD software lacks: multiple students can work on the same document simultaneously, like a Google Doc for 3D models. A design lead can watch in real time as a new member sketches their first bracket, offer guidance, and the change appears instantly in the shared assembly. Version history means that no work is ever truly lost — every saved state can be restored.

#### The Onshape Workspace
Onshape organizes your work into Documents, each of which can contain three types of tabs: Part Studios, Assemblies, and Drawings. A Part Studio is where you create individual 3D parts by building sketch geometry and applying features. An Assembly is where you bring multiple parts together, position them relative to each other, and simulate how they move. A Drawing is where you generate flat, dimensioned views of a part or assembly for fabrication.

The Part Studio interface has three main regions. The Feature List on the left is a chronological record of every operation that built the part — first a sketch, then an extrude, then a fillet. This is called a parametric model: changing an early sketch automatically updates every downstream feature. The graphics window in the center shows your 3D model. The toolbar at the top contains sketch tools, feature tools, and view controls. Understanding this layout deeply is the prerequisite for efficient modeling.

Navigation in Onshape uses the middle mouse button to orbit, scroll to zoom, and right-click to pan. The view cube in the upper-right corner shows your current orientation and lets you snap to standard views (Front, Top, Right, Isometric) with a single click. Developing smooth navigation habits early saves enormous time — engineers who fight their CAD interface are engineers who aren't thinking about the design.

#### Onshape Document Management for FRC Teams
FRC teams typically organize their Onshape workspace with one main robot document containing all mechanisms as separate Part Studios and Assemblies, a folder system by subsystem (drivetrain, intake, shooter, climber), and a team library of standard hardware (REV Robotics tubes, gussets, motors). Setting up this structure at the start of season prevents the chaos of searching through unnamed documents at 2 AM before a competition.

Student accounts on Onshape (edu.onshape.com) are free and include all professional features. Your documents default to public visibility — meaning anyone with the link can view them. Most FRC teams accept this tradeoff; their designs become part of the open-source community that makes FRC great. If your team requires privacy, the educator plan offers private documents. Either way, always add your team number and robot year to every document name so documents remain findable years later when future members research historical designs.

### Video Lessons

#### Getting Started with Onshape for FRC (~12 min)
**Channel:** Onshape for Education
**Search:** "Onshape beginner tutorial FRC robotics" on YouTube
**What to watch for:** How the instructor creates a new document, names it, and navigates between tabs. Notice how they use the view cube to snap between standard views — this habit saves constant mouse work. Pay attention to the Feature List on the left and how it grows with each operation.

---

#### FRC Team Onshape Workspace Tour (~8 min)
**Channel:** FRC team design channels (search for recent season reveal videos with CAD tours)
**Search:** "FRC Onshape robot design walkthrough" on YouTube
**What to watch for:** How real teams organize their documents and assemblies. Notice the naming conventions, the number of Part Studios per document, and how they handle standard hardware libraries. This gives you a realistic target for your own workflow.

### Practice Exercises

#### Exercise 1: Account Setup & Navigation (15 min)
**Objective:** Create an Onshape student account and navigate a provided sample document

1. Go to edu.onshape.com and create a free student account using your school email
2. Open the Onshape Learning Center and find the "Introduction to Onshape" course
3. Open a sample part document from the Onshape public library (search "FRC sample part" in the public documents search)
4. Practice orbiting, zooming, and panning until navigation feels fluid — orbit around the part 20 times without stopping
5. Click each face of the view cube and identify what standard engineering view (Front, Top, Right) it corresponds to

**Success Criteria:** You can navigate a 3D model smoothly using mouse controls and snap to any standard view using the view cube within 2 seconds.

---

#### Exercise 2: Creating Your First Document (20 min)
**Objective:** Set up a properly named Onshape document with organized tabs

1. Create a new Onshape document named "TeamXXXX_Week3_Practice" (replace XXXX with your team number)
2. Rename the default Part Studio tab to "Bracket_v1"
3. Add a second Part Studio tab named "Tube_v1"
4. Add an Assembly tab named "Bracket_Assembly"
5. Add a Drawing tab named "Bracket_Drawing"
6. Write a brief description in the document description field explaining what this document will contain
7. Share the document link with your instructor or mentor for review

**Success Criteria:** Your document has four named tabs following a consistent naming convention, and your mentor can access it via the shared link.

---

#### Exercise 3: Exploring the Feature List (15 min)
**Objective:** Read and understand the parametric history of an existing part

1. Open a publicly shared FRC part from Onshape's public library (search "gusset plate FRC")
2. Click each item in the Feature List to understand what each feature added to the model
3. Right-click a feature and choose "Edit" to see its parameters (do not save changes)
4. Try suppressing one feature by right-clicking and choosing "Suppress" — observe how the model changes
5. Unsuppress the feature to restore the part to its original state
6. Sketch out on paper: what order of operations created this part? Write your reconstruction of the build sequence.

**Success Criteria:** You can identify at least 4 distinct features in the Feature List and describe in words what each one does to the geometry.

### Knowledge Check

**1.** What is the primary advantage of using Onshape over traditional installed CAD software for FRC teams?
- A) Onshape produces more accurate models
- B) Onshape runs in a browser with no installation, enabling real-time collaboration
- C) Onshape is faster at generating toolpaths for CNC machines
- D) Onshape has more features than any other CAD platform

**Answer:** B

**2.** What does the Feature List in a Part Studio represent?
- A) A list of all the parts in the assembly
- B) A library of standard hardware components
- C) The chronological history of operations that built the part
- D) A checklist of quality control steps

**Answer:** C

**3.** In Onshape, which tab type is used to bring multiple parts together and simulate their movement?
- A) Part Studio
- B) Drawing
- C) Assembly
- D) Feature

**Answer:** C

**4.** What is a parametric model?
- A) A model that only contains parameters, not geometry
- B) A model where changing an early feature automatically updates downstream features
- C) A model imported from a different CAD program
- D) A model that cannot be edited after creation

**Answer:** B

**5.** Why do many FRC teams accept that their Onshape documents are publicly visible?
- A) They are required to by FRC rules
- B) Public visibility allows faster rendering
- C) Their designs contribute to the open-source FRC community
- D) Private documents cost extra in Onshape

**Answer:** C

**6.** What happens when you middle-click and drag in the Onshape graphics window?
- A) You zoom in and out
- B) You pan the view left and right
- C) You orbit (rotate) around the model
- D) You select multiple features

**Answer:** C

**7.** Which of the following is a realistic benefit of collision detection in CAD?
- A) It detects when two people are editing the same sketch simultaneously
- B) It automatically fixes parts that overlap
- C) It finds places where two parts occupy the same space before fabrication
- D) It checks whether the robot violates FRC size rules

**Answer:** C

**8.** What type of Onshape tab would you use to create a 2D dimensioned drawing for a machinist?
- A) Part Studio
- B) Assembly
- C) Sketch
- D) Drawing

**Answer:** D

**9.** What is the recommended FRC team practice for document naming in Onshape?
- A) Use single-letter abbreviations to keep names short
- B) Include team number and robot year so documents remain findable
- C) Name documents after the person who created them
- D) Use sequential numbers (Doc1, Doc2, Doc3)

**Answer:** B

**10.** What does "suppressing" a feature in the Feature List do?
- A) Permanently deletes the feature from the model
- B) Hides the feature from the drawing view
- C) Temporarily removes the feature's effect on the geometry without deleting it
- D) Locks the feature so it cannot be edited

**Answer:** C

### Reflections

1. Before today, how did you imagine professional engineers communicated their designs to machinists and fabricators? How does what you learned about CAD change or confirm that picture?

2. Think about a time your FRC team built something that didn't fit or didn't work as expected. How might CAD have caught that problem before you reached the build stage?

3. Onshape's real-time collaboration is similar to Google Docs. What would be the biggest challenge of having multiple people edit the same 3D model at the same time? What rules or norms would your team need to establish?

4. Professional engineers sometimes say "the model is always right" — meaning they trust the CAD over their intuition about how a part looks. Is that a safe attitude? When might the model be wrong even if the software shows no errors?

### Summary

- **CAD (Computer-Aided Design):** Software that creates precise mathematical representations of physical objects, allowing engineers to design, simulate, and communicate designs before fabrication.
- **Onshape:** A free, browser-based parametric CAD platform widely used by FRC teams for its collaboration features and zero-installation setup.
- **Parametric modeling:** A design approach where all geometry is driven by changeable parameters, so modifying an early feature automatically updates the entire model.
- **Feature List:** The chronological record of operations (sketches, extrudes, fillets) that built a part; the "recipe" of the model.
- **Collision detection:** A CAD tool that identifies locations where two parts occupy the same physical space, enabling interference to be fixed digitally before fabrication.

---

## Session 3.2: 2D Sketching Fundamentals
**Duration:** 60 minutes | **Format:** Tutorial with guided practice

### Introduction

Every 3D feature in CAD starts as a 2D sketch. A sketch is a flat profile drawn on a plane — like drawing on paper, except the lines have mathematical relationships to each other and to the coordinate system. Mastering sketching is the single most important skill in CAD, because a poorly constrained sketch produces unreliable geometry that breaks when you change a dimension. FRC teams that design accurately constrained sketches can modify designs in seconds; teams with messy sketches spend hours troubleshooting broken models mid-season.

**What You Will Learn:**
- How to create a sketch on a plane and use basic sketch tools (line, circle, rectangle, arc)
- How to apply geometric constraints (horizontal, vertical, tangent, coincident) and dimensional constraints
- How to recognize a fully defined sketch and why it matters for robust parametric design

### Core Concepts

#### Sketch Planes and the Coordinate System
Every sketch lives on a flat plane. Onshape provides three default planes: Front (XZ), Top (XY), and Right (YZ). Choosing the correct starting plane matters because it determines how your part orients in 3D space and in your assemblies. FRC convention typically puts the Front plane facing the robot's front, the Top plane facing upward, and the Right plane facing the right side of the robot — consistent orientation across all Part Studios makes assemblies much easier.

Once you activate a sketch on a plane, you are drawing in 2D using X and Y coordinates on that plane. The origin point (where X and Y equal zero) appears as a red cross. Locking features to the origin prevents the model from "floating" — an unanchored sketch can drift and cause unpredictable behavior when downstream features are added. Good sketching practice always starts by fixing a key point of the sketch to the origin.

Sketch geometry appears in two colors: blue for under-constrained geometry (still free to move), and black for fully constrained geometry (completely defined, cannot move without changing a dimension). A fully black sketch is called fully defined, and it is the goal for every production sketch. A sketch with blue geometry is ambiguous — it could represent more than one physical shape, which is dangerous in a parametric model.

#### Sketch Tools and Geometric Constraints
The basic sketch tools you will use constantly in FRC CAD are: Line (draws straight line segments), Circle (draws a circle by center and radius), Rectangle (draws a rectangle by two corners or center), Arc (draws a circular arc), and Construction Line (draws reference geometry that doesn't become 3D, shown as dashed). These five tools handle the majority of FRC part profiles.

Geometric constraints tell the software about relationships between sketch elements: Horizontal makes a line perfectly horizontal. Vertical makes a line perfectly vertical. Coincident forces two points to share the same location. Tangent makes a line smoothly meet a curve without a corner. Equal makes two lengths or radii identical. Parallel makes two lines run in the same direction. Perpendicular makes two lines meet at 90 degrees. These constraints reduce the number of dimensions you need to type, and they encode design intent — if two holes should always be the same size, an Equal constraint ensures that remains true even when you change the hole size.

Dimensional constraints assign specific numerical values to lengths, radii, angles, and positions. Adding a dimension is as simple as clicking a line and typing a number. The power of parametric CAD emerges when you change a dimension — every constrained feature updates instantly. FRC teams frequently run "what-if" design studies by changing tube lengths or hole positions and watching the entire mechanism update in seconds.

#### Common Sketching Mistakes and How to Avoid Them
The most dangerous sketching mistake is leaving a sketch under-defined (with blue geometry) and proceeding to add 3D features on top of it. The sketch appears to work, but the blue element can shift to any valid position — and when you later edit a dimension, the geometry may jump to a completely different configuration. Always confirm your sketch is fully black before clicking "Extrude" or any other feature.

Duplicate lines are another common trap. If you draw a line on top of an existing line, Onshape creates two overlapping edges — which causes errors in subsequent features. Before closing a sketch, zoom in to suspected overlapping areas and check. The sketch solver will often warn you with orange error indicators when geometry is over-constrained (too many conflicting constraints) or invalid. Read those warnings carefully before proceeding.

In FRC-specific sketching, always use your actual design dimensions rather than approximate values. If a COTS (commercial off-the-shelf) tube is 1.000 inch square, sketch it at exactly 1.000 — not 1 or "about 1 inch." Accumulating approximation errors across dozens of parts in an assembly can produce collision errors or gaps that don't reflect the real robot. When in doubt, measure the physical part with calipers and use that exact number.

### Video Lessons

#### Onshape Sketching Fundamentals (~15 min)
**Channel:** Onshape
**Search:** "Onshape sketch tutorial beginner" on YouTube
**What to watch for:** How the instructor adds constraints one at a time and watches the sketch color change from blue to black. Notice when they choose dimensions versus geometric constraints — they prefer constraints when they encode a relationship, and dimensions when they need a specific value.

---

#### Fully Defined Sketches Explained (~8 min)
**Channel:** FRC CAD tutorials (multiple channels)
**Search:** "Onshape fully defined sketch FRC" on YouTube
**What to watch for:** Examples of under-defined sketches causing problems when dimensions change. This illustrates why the blue-to-black discipline matters in a real design workflow.

### Practice Exercises

#### Exercise 1: Basic Shapes Sketch (15 min)
**Objective:** Practice the five basic sketch tools on a single sketch plane

1. Open your Week 3 document and go to the "Bracket_v1" Part Studio
2. Create a new sketch on the Front plane
3. Draw a 2.000 x 3.000 inch rectangle centered on the origin (use the center-point rectangle tool)
4. Draw a 0.500-inch diameter circle at each corner of the rectangle (four circles total)
5. Add a 0.250-inch radius arc connecting two adjacent sides
6. Apply dimensions to make the sketch fully defined (all geometry should turn black)
7. Close the sketch and verify no yellow warnings appear

**Success Criteria:** All geometry is black (fully defined), the rectangle is exactly 2.000 x 3.000 inches centered at the origin, and all four circles are exactly 0.500 inches in diameter.

---

#### Exercise 2: FRC Gusset Profile (20 min)
**Objective:** Sketch a simplified version of a common FRC gusset plate

1. Create a new sketch on the Top plane
2. Sketch a right-angle L-shape: two 2.000-inch legs meeting at 90 degrees, 0.750-inch wide
3. Add 0.201-inch diameter holes (for 10-32 screws) at 0.500-inch from each end, spaced 1.000 inch apart
4. Add fillets (rounded corners) of 0.125-inch radius at the inside corner
5. Make the sketch fully defined using dimensions and geometric constraints
6. Review your sketch: would a machinist be able to fabricate this part from your sketch alone?

**Success Criteria:** The L-shaped profile is fully defined, has correctly sized holes, and has the inside fillet. Another student should be able to reproduce your sketch from scratch by reading your dimensions.

---

#### Exercise 3: Parametric Change Test (15 min)
**Objective:** Demonstrate that a well-defined sketch updates cleanly when dimensions change

1. Using the gusset sketch from Exercise 2, double-click the 2.000-inch leg dimension and change it to 3.000 inches
2. Observe how the entire sketch updates while maintaining all constraints
3. Check that all hole positions updated correctly (they should still be 0.500 inches from the ends)
4. Change the hole diameter from 0.201 to 0.257 inches (for 1/4-20 screws) — both holes should update simultaneously if you used Equal constraints
5. Restore the original dimensions (2.000 inch legs, 0.201 holes)
6. Document your observations: what changed correctly, and what (if anything) did not behave as expected?

**Success Criteria:** All four dimension changes produce clean, correct updates with no sketch errors or orange warnings.

### Knowledge Check

**1.** What does a blue sketch element indicate in Onshape?
- A) The element is a construction line
- B) The element is selected
- C) The element is under-constrained and can still move
- D) The element has an error

**Answer:** C

**2.** What is the primary purpose of geometric constraints like Horizontal, Perpendicular, and Equal?
- A) To set exact numerical values for dimensions
- B) To encode design relationships that remain true even when dimensions change
- C) To change the color of sketch elements
- D) To convert 2D sketches to 3D features

**Answer:** B

**3.** Why should you always anchor a sketch to the origin?
- A) To make the sketch print correctly
- B) To prevent the model from floating and causing unpredictable behavior
- C) The origin is required for extrude features to work
- D) Onshape will not accept sketches not connected to the origin

**Answer:** B

**4.** What hole diameter should you use for a 10-32 machine screw clearance hole in a sketch?
- A) 0.132 inches (the screw's major diameter)
- B) 0.250 inches
- C) 0.201 inches
- D) 0.500 inches

**Answer:** C

**5.** What does a "fully defined" sketch mean?
- A) The sketch has been converted to a 3D feature
- B) All sketch geometry is black and cannot move without changing a constraint or dimension
- C) The sketch contains all the features needed for the final part
- D) The sketch has been reviewed and approved by a mentor

**Answer:** B

**6.** What tool would you use to draw reference geometry that helps you locate features but doesn't become part of the 3D solid?
- A) Construction Line
- B) Reference Plane
- C) Mirror tool
- D) Offset Edge

**Answer:** A

**7.** Which constraint forces two points to share the same location?
- A) Equal
- B) Tangent
- C) Parallel
- D) Coincident

**Answer:** D

**8.** What is the danger of leaving blue (under-constrained) geometry in a sketch before adding 3D features?
- A) The feature will be slower to compute
- B) The sketch will appear gray instead of black
- C) The geometry may jump to an unexpected position when other dimensions are changed later
- D) Onshape will automatically delete the under-constrained elements

**Answer:** C

**9.** What happens when you apply an Equal constraint between two circles?
- A) The circles become concentric (same center)
- B) The circles become the same radius and update together when either radius changes
- C) The circles are constrained to be the same distance from the origin
- D) One circle is deleted

**Answer:** B

**10.** In FRC CAD practice, why should you use exact dimensions (e.g., 1.000 inch) rather than approximate values?
- A) Onshape rounds all dimensions anyway, so it doesn't matter
- B) FRC rules require exact CAD models
- C) Approximate values accumulate across parts and can cause collision errors or gaps in assemblies
- D) Approximate values cannot be changed parametrically

**Answer:** C

### Reflections

1. Describe the feeling of working with a fully constrained (all-black) sketch versus an under-constrained (partially blue) sketch. Which feels more comfortable to work in, and why might that comfort level be misleading for a beginner?

2. Geometric constraints encode "design intent" — the relationships that must remain true even as dimensions change. Think of a FRC mechanism you've worked on. What geometric relationships were critical to its function? How would you encode those as constraints in a sketch?

3. You practiced parametric changes: changing one dimension and watching the model update. In a real FRC build season, how often do designs change after the initial sketch? What are the main drivers of those changes?

4. A teammate insists that approximate dimensions are "close enough" for the robot's intake bracket. How would you explain to them — using what you learned today — why that attitude creates compounding problems later in the design process?

### Summary

- **Sketch plane:** The flat 2D surface on which a sketch is drawn; choosing the correct starting plane determines how the part orients in 3D space and in assemblies.
- **Fully defined sketch:** A sketch where all geometry is black, meaning every element is completely determined by constraints and dimensions with no remaining degrees of freedom.
- **Geometric constraints:** Rules about relationships between sketch elements (Horizontal, Perpendicular, Equal, Coincident) that encode design intent independently of specific dimensions.
- **Dimensional constraints:** Numerical values assigned to lengths, radii, and angles that drive the size and position of sketch geometry parametrically.
- **Under-constrained geometry:** Blue sketch elements that retain freedom to move, which can cause unpredictable model behavior when other parameters change later.

---

## Session 3.3: 3D Feature Creation
**Duration:** 60 minutes | **Format:** Tutorial with progressive part-building exercise

### Introduction

A 2D sketch becomes a 3D part through features. Features are the vocabulary of solid modeling: Extrude pushes a profile along a straight path, Revolve spins a profile around an axis, Fillet rounds a sharp edge, Chamfer cuts a bevel on an edge. Every FRC bracket, tube, plate, and custom part can be created from combinations of these fundamental features. This session takes you from flat sketches to real 3D solids.

**What You Will Learn:**
- How to apply Extrude to add and remove material from a part
- How to apply Revolve, Fillet, and Chamfer features
- How to use reference geometry (planes, axes) to sketch on non-standard surfaces

### Core Concepts

#### Extrude: The Foundation Feature
The Extrude feature takes a closed sketch profile and pushes it through space along a direction, creating a solid volume. There are two fundamental extrude modes: Add (builds up material from a blank) and Remove (cuts material away from an existing solid). Almost every FRC part starts with an Add extrude to create the basic shape, followed by Remove extrudes to cut holes, slots, and pockets.

Extrude direction is typically perpendicular to the sketch plane by default, but you can specify any direction. Extrude depth can be a fixed dimension, symmetric (equal depth in both directions from the sketch plane), or "up to face/vertex" (extending exactly to an existing surface). For FRC tube stock, you often extrude a 1.000 x 1.000 inch square profile to the exact tube length, then use a shell feature or a second Remove extrude to hollow the interior to the correct wall thickness.

The "Extrude Remove" function is how holes are made. You sketch a circle (or rectangle for a slot) on the surface of a solid and extrude it inward. Choosing "Through All" as the depth removes the material all the way through the part regardless of thickness — perfect for through-holes in plates and brackets. Using a specific depth creates blind holes. This is the mechanism you'll use to add every bolt hole and access slot in your FRC parts.

#### Revolve, Fillet, and Chamfer
The Revolve feature spins a 2D profile around an axis line to create rotationally symmetric geometry. Shafts, wheels, pulleys, and flanged hubs are all natural candidates for revolve. To model a 0.500-inch hex shaft, for example, you would draw a hexagon profile on the Front plane and revolve it 360 degrees around its center axis. Revolve can also be used in Remove mode to cut a circular groove into an existing solid.

Fillets round sharp edges by replacing them with a curved surface of a specified radius. In FRC, fillets serve two purposes: functional (removing sharp edges that cut hands or snag cables) and structural (distributing stress concentration away from corners, increasing fatigue life of aluminum parts under repeated robot impact). Adding a 0.062-inch fillet to all exposed edges of a bracket is considered good practice on competitive FRC teams. Fillets appear at the end of the Feature List — always add them after all cuts and holes are complete, not at the beginning.

Chamfers bevel an edge by cutting a flat angled surface instead of a curve. They are faster to machine than fillets on a manual mill, and they are standard at the entry face of holes to help bolts and dowel pins start cleanly. A 0.030 x 45-degree chamfer at every hole entry is a professional fabrication detail that FRC teams should adopt. Both fillets and chamfers are editing features — they act on edges of existing geometry rather than creating new sketches.

#### Reference Planes and Multi-Feature Parts
As parts become more complex, you often need to sketch on surfaces other than the three default planes. Onshape lets you create offset planes (parallel to a default plane but shifted by a distance), midplane between two surfaces, and planes at angles to existing geometry. You can also sketch directly on any flat face of an existing solid — just click the face and start sketching.

Building complex FRC parts follows a logical sequence: start with the largest, simplest feature (the base extrude), then add material for bosses and flanges, then remove material for holes and pockets, then apply fillets and chamfers last. This mirrors how a machinist thinks: rough out the shape first, then add precision detail. Keeping the Feature List organized in this sequence makes models easier to debug and modify. Rename your features descriptively ("Hex Bore" instead of "Extrude 7") so anyone reading the Feature List understands what each step does.

### Video Lessons

#### Onshape Extrude Feature Tutorial (~10 min)
**Channel:** Onshape
**Search:** "Onshape extrude feature tutorial" on YouTube
**What to watch for:** The difference between Extrude Add and Extrude Remove, and how the instructor switches between them for the same profile. Pay special attention to the "Through All" option and when to use it versus a fixed depth.

---

#### Building an FRC Bracket in Onshape (~20 min)
**Channel:** FRC-focused CAD channels (e.g., "FRC CAD Tutorial")
**Search:** "FRC bracket Onshape tutorial step by step" on YouTube
**What to watch for:** The sequence of features in the Feature List — how the instructor builds up from a base extrude, adds holes, and finishes with fillets. Notice how they rename features and keep the list organized.

### Practice Exercises

#### Exercise 1: Simple Block with Holes (15 min)
**Objective:** Create a rectangular block with through-holes using Extrude Add and Remove

1. In your "Bracket_v1" Part Studio, create a new sketch on the Front plane
2. Sketch a 3.000 x 2.000 inch rectangle centered at the origin
3. Extrude it 0.250 inches (Add mode) to create a flat plate
4. Create a new sketch on the front face of the plate
5. Sketch four 0.201-inch circles at each corner (0.500 inches from each edge)
6. Extrude Remove the circles "Through All" to create bolt clearance holes
7. Apply 0.062-inch fillets to all four outer vertical edges

**Success Criteria:** You have a 3.000 x 2.000 x 0.250 inch plate with four clean through-holes and filleted edges. The Feature List has at least three named features.

---

#### Exercise 2: L-Bracket 3D Part (20 min)
**Objective:** Build a functional FRC-style L-bracket in 3D

1. Sketch the L-profile (from Session 3.2 Exercise 2) on the Front plane: two 2.000-inch legs, 0.750-inch wide, 0.125-inch thick
2. Extrude the profile 1.500 inches (the bracket's depth)
3. Add 0.201-inch through-holes in the pattern from your sketch on the appropriate faces
4. Apply 0.030 x 45-degree chamfers to all hole entries
5. Apply 0.062-inch fillets to all outer long edges
6. Rename your features descriptively in the Feature List

**Success Criteria:** A complete 3D L-bracket with holes, chamfers, and fillets. The Feature List has descriptively named features that a teammate could read and understand the build sequence.

---

#### Exercise 3: Shaft Modeling with Revolve (15 min)
**Objective:** Use the Revolve feature to model a simple shaft

1. Create a new Part Studio named "Shaft_v1"
2. Sketch on the Front plane: draw a horizontal centerline along the X-axis
3. Sketch a profile above the centerline: a rectangle 4.000 inches long and 0.250 inches tall (representing a 0.500-inch diameter shaft — the height is the radius)
4. Revolve the rectangle 360 degrees around the centerline to create a cylinder
5. Add a 0.030-inch chamfer on each end of the shaft
6. Measure the resulting solid's diameter using the "Measure" tool to verify it is exactly 0.500 inches

**Success Criteria:** A 4.000-inch long cylindrical shaft with 0.500-inch diameter and chamfered ends, created using Revolve. The Measure tool confirms 0.500-inch diameter.

### Knowledge Check

**1.** What is the difference between Extrude Add and Extrude Remove?
- A) Extrude Add creates a new sketch; Extrude Remove deletes a sketch
- B) Extrude Add builds up material; Extrude Remove cuts material away
- C) Extrude Add works on curved surfaces; Extrude Remove only works on flat surfaces
- D) There is no difference; they produce the same geometry

**Answer:** B

**2.** When should you use "Through All" as the extrude depth for a hole?
- A) When you want the hole to stop exactly 0.100 inches from the back face
- B) When the hole should extend all the way through the part regardless of thickness
- C) When the hole is larger than 0.500 inches in diameter
- D) Only when the part has already been filleted

**Answer:** B

**3.** In what order should features typically appear in the Feature List?
- A) Fillets first, then holes, then base extrude
- B) Holes first, then base extrude, then fillets
- C) Base extrude first, then holes and cuts, then fillets and chamfers last
- D) The order does not matter; Onshape computes them simultaneously

**Answer:** C

**4.** What does a Revolve feature do?
- A) Rotates the entire part in the graphics view
- B) Spins a 2D profile around an axis to create rotationally symmetric geometry
- C) Copies a feature and rotates the copies around a center point
- D) Animates the part rotating in an assembly

**Answer:** B

**5.** What is the functional purpose of fillets on FRC robot parts?
- A) They make parts heavier and more rigid
- B) They remove sharp edges and reduce stress concentration at corners
- C) They are required by FRC inspection rules
- D) They make parts easier to sketch in 2D

**Answer:** B

**6.** What is a chamfer, and where is it most commonly applied in FRC fabrication?
- A) A curved rounded edge, applied to the outside of tubes
- B) A flat beveled edge, commonly applied at hole entries to guide fasteners
- C) A countersink for flat-head screws, applied to all bracket faces
- D) A type of groove cut into a shaft to retain a retaining ring

**Answer:** B

**7.** How do you sketch on a surface of an existing solid that is not one of the three default planes?
- A) You must always use the default Front, Top, or Right plane
- B) Click on the flat face of the solid and start a sketch directly on that surface
- C) Export the surface as a separate file and import it back
- D) Create a new document for each additional sketch direction

**Answer:** B

**8.** What does renaming features in the Feature List accomplish?
- A) It changes the part's file name on Onshape's server
- B) It speeds up model rebuild times
- C) It makes the build sequence readable to teammates and easier to debug
- D) It is required before exporting to a Drawing tab

**Answer:** C

**9.** If you want to model a hex shaft in CAD, which feature is most appropriate?
- A) Extrude a hexagon profile
- B) Revolve a rectangle profile around a centerline
- C) Apply a chamfer to a cylindrical shaft
- D) Mirror a square profile along two axes

**Answer:** A

**10.** What does the Shell feature do (a feature not covered in depth but worth knowing)?
- A) Exports the part as a mesh for 3D printing
- B) Hollows out a solid part to a specified wall thickness
- C) Creates a shell of reference geometry around the part
- D) Splits the part into two separate bodies

**Answer:** B

### Reflections

1. You built physical parts before in FRC — brackets, tubes, gussets. How did the 3D modeling experience compare to working with physical material? What can you do in CAD that you cannot do with physical material, and vice versa?

2. The instruction to "rename your features descriptively" might seem like unnecessary extra work when you are in a hurry. Describe a scenario where a poorly named Feature List would cause a real problem for your FRC team mid-season.

3. Fillets and chamfers are described as "professional details." Do you think FRC teams that skip these steps are making a reasonable tradeoff given time pressure, or are these non-negotiable? What is your reasoning?

4. You modeled a shaft using a Revolve feature. Compare this to modeling a tube (hollow cylinder) — what additional steps would be needed? How would you think through that sequence before touching the keyboard?

### Summary

- **Extrude Add:** Pushes a sketch profile through space to create new solid material; the foundational feature of most 3D parts.
- **Extrude Remove:** Cuts a sketch profile through existing solid material; the standard method for creating holes, slots, and pockets.
- **Revolve:** Spins a 2D profile around an axis to create rotationally symmetric solids; ideal for shafts, pulleys, and hubs.
- **Fillet:** Replaces a sharp edge with a curved surface of specified radius; removes hazardous sharp edges and reduces stress concentration.
- **Chamfer:** Replaces a sharp edge with a flat beveled surface; common at hole entries to guide fasteners and at ends of shafts to ease assembly.
