## Session 3.4: Assembly Modeling
**Duration:** 60 minutes | **Format:** Guided workshop with team exercise option

### Introduction

Individual parts become a robot through assembly. In Onshape's Assembly environment, you bring multiple Part Studio parts together and position them relative to each other using Mates. Mates are parametric relationships between part geometry — like declaring that this shaft lives inside this hole, or that this plate rests flush against this face. A good assembly model lets you check fit before fabrication, simulate motion, and share a complete robot model with the whole team.

**What You Will Learn:**
- How to insert parts into an Onshape Assembly and position them using Mates
- How to use the Fastened, Revolute, and Slider mate types appropriate to FRC mechanisms
- How to check for part interference and verify range of motion in an assembly

### Core Concepts

#### Inserting Parts and the Assembly Environment
When you open an Assembly tab in Onshape, the first step is inserting parts. You can insert from any Part Studio in the same document, from other documents in your workspace, or from Onshape's public library of standard hardware. Parts appear in the assembly grayed out and floating in space until they are constrained by mates. The first part you insert is typically fixed (anchored to the assembly origin) — this is your reference, usually the chassis or a main structural plate.

The Assembly Feature List works similarly to the Part Studio Feature List but contains mates and patterns instead of sketches and extrudes. Every mate you add shows up here, named automatically with the two parts it connects. Good mate management means the assembly rebuilds reliably when you change parts — a significant advantage when a designer edits a bracket in the Part Studio and the assembly automatically reflects the updated geometry.

Hardware libraries save enormous time in FRC assembly modeling. The FRC standard hardware library (maintained by various team CAD leads and available publicly in Onshape) contains pre-modeled versions of COTS components: REV tubes, gussets, motors, gearboxes, wheels, and fasteners. Rather than modeling every 10-32 x 0.500-inch socket head cap screw from scratch, you insert the standard part from the library. This practice accelerates assembly work and ensures hardware matches real-world dimensions exactly.

#### Mate Types for FRC Mechanisms
The Fastened mate rigidly connects two parts at specific mate connectors, locking all six degrees of freedom. Use Fastened mates for bolted joints — a gusset plate bolted to a tube, a motor bolted to a bracket. The two mate connectors (one on each part) are aligned in position and orientation. When you later move or edit one part, the Fastened mate keeps the other in its locked relative position.

The Revolute mate allows rotation around one axis while locking all other motion. This is the correct mate for pivots, hinges, and any shaft-in-bearing situation. When you mate a wheel hub to a bearing and the bearing to a bracket, the Revolute mate between hub and bearing lets you simulate the wheel spinning while the bracket stays fixed. You can also set rotation limits in a Revolute mate, which is useful for modeling the range of travel of an arm or claw mechanism.

The Slider mate allows linear translation along one axis. Use it for telescoping mechanisms, linear actuators, and sliding carriage assemblies. By combining mate types — for example, a Revolute at one end of a piston and a Slider along its extension axis — you can simulate pneumatic actuators and verify that a mechanism's full range of motion is achievable without hitting structure. This is the digital equivalent of manually moving a robot mechanism to confirm it doesn't hit the frame.

#### Interference Detection and Motion Studies
Once your assembly has mates defined, Onshape can check for interference — solid geometry from two different parts occupying the same space. Run Interference Detection from the Tools menu. The results show every pair of parts with overlapping geometry, the volume of overlap, and the location highlighted in the model. For FRC, even a 0.001-inch interference is worth investigating: it might be an intentional press fit, a modeling error, or a real fabrication problem.

Motion studies let you drag mates through their range of motion and watch what the assembly does. Click the Revolute mate handle and drag the wheel to spin it, or pull a slider mate to extend a linear mechanism. This visual simulation immediately reveals issues: does the arm clear the bumper at its lowest position? Does the intake fold within the robot's perimeter? Does the gearbox output shaft align with the wheel bore at all positions? These questions that might take an hour to investigate physically can be answered in minutes in a well-built assembly model.

### Video Lessons

#### Onshape Assembly Mates Tutorial (~15 min)
**Channel:** Onshape
**Search:** "Onshape assembly mates tutorial" on YouTube
**What to watch for:** How the instructor selects mate connectors on each part and what determines which face/edge/vertex to choose. Notice how the parts "snap" together when the mate is applied correctly. Pay attention to the Revolute mate and how the rotation axis is defined.

---

#### FRC Robot Assembly in Onshape (~18 min)
**Channel:** FRC team design channels
**Search:** "FRC robot CAD assembly Onshape complete" on YouTube
**What to watch for:** How the team organizes subassemblies (drivetrain assembly, intake assembly) before combining into a full robot assembly. Notice the hardware library usage and how mate names are managed in the Feature List.

### Practice Exercises

#### Exercise 1: Two-Part Fastened Assembly (15 min)
**Objective:** Assemble the L-bracket and flat plate from Session 3.3 using Fastened mates

1. Open your "Bracket_Assembly" tab
2. Insert the flat plate from your "Bracket_v1" Part Studio — fix it to the assembly origin
3. Insert the L-bracket from your "Bracket_v1" Part Studio (or Session 3.3 work)
4. Add Fastened mates to position the bracket flush against the plate's face, with holes aligned
5. Insert two 10-32 x 0.500-inch SHCS (socket head cap screw) from the Onshape hardware library
6. Mate each screw through its corresponding hole
7. Verify the assembly looks correct from multiple viewing angles

**Success Criteria:** The bracket is correctly positioned against the plate, holes align, and two fasteners are correctly placed. No parts overlap except at designed mating surfaces.

---

#### Exercise 2: Revolute Mate Simulation (20 min)
**Objective:** Create a simple pivot joint and verify range of motion

1. Create two simple rectangular parts in a new Part Studio: a fixed "arm base" (3.000 x 1.000 x 0.250 inch plate) and an "arm link" (4.000 x 0.500 x 0.250 inch plate)
2. Add a 0.250-inch through-hole at one end of each part (the pivot location)
3. In the Assembly, fix the arm base to the origin
4. Insert the arm link and add a Revolute mate between the two pivot holes
5. Set rotation limits of -30 degrees to +90 degrees (simulating a mechanism with travel limits)
6. Drag the arm link through its range of motion and confirm it stays within limits

**Success Criteria:** The arm link pivots freely between -30 and +90 degrees and stops cleanly at both limits without interfering with the base plate.

---

#### Exercise 3: Interference Check (15 min)
**Objective:** Intentionally create and then detect an interference condition

1. In your existing two-part assembly, move the L-bracket so it intentionally overlaps the flat plate by 0.100 inches (edit a mate offset to create the interference)
2. Run Tools > Interference Detection
3. Read the interference report: which parts are colliding, and what is the interference volume?
4. Correct the mate offset to eliminate the interference
5. Run Interference Detection again to confirm zero interference pairs
6. Write a brief note: in a real FRC robot, what are three situations where interference detection would save significant rework time?

**Success Criteria:** You successfully detect and resolve the interference, and can articulate three realistic FRC scenarios where this tool provides value.

### Knowledge Check

**1.** What is the purpose of fixing the first inserted part to the assembly origin?
- A) To make it the heaviest part in the assembly
- B) To establish a reference that all other parts are positioned relative to
- C) Fixed parts cannot be edited in the Part Studio
- D) Onshape requires exactly one fixed part for rendering

**Answer:** B

**2.** Which mate type allows rotation around one axis while locking all other motion?
- A) Fastened
- B) Revolute
- C) Slider
- D) Planar

**Answer:** B

**3.** What does the Fastened mate do to the degrees of freedom between two parts?
- A) Allows one translational degree of freedom
- B) Allows one rotational degree of freedom
- C) Locks all six degrees of freedom, rigidly connecting the parts
- D) Allows three degrees of freedom: X, Y, and Z translation

**Answer:** C

**4.** Where can you find pre-modeled FRC standard hardware (motors, gearboxes, tubes) to insert into your assembly?
- A) Only from the vendor's website as STEP files
- B) From Onshape's public document library maintained by FRC community members
- C) Hardware must be modeled from scratch for legal use in FRC
- D) Hardware library is only available in the paid Onshape plan

**Answer:** B

**5.** What does Interference Detection check for?
- A) Missing mates between parts
- B) Parts that are not connected to the assembly origin
- C) Solid geometry from two different parts occupying the same space
- D) Parts that are moving faster than allowed by FRC rules

**Answer:** C

**6.** What mate type would you use for a telescoping tube mechanism?
- A) Fastened
- B) Revolute
- C) Slider
- D) Ball

**Answer:** C

**7.** What is the benefit of using subassemblies (e.g., a separate drivetrain assembly) before combining into a full robot assembly?
- A) Subassemblies are required by Onshape's licensing terms
- B) They organize work by mechanism and allow different team members to work simultaneously
- C) Subassemblies render faster than single large assemblies
- D) They prevent interference detection from running on the full robot

**Answer:** B

**8.** What does dragging a Revolute mate handle allow you to do?
- A) Edit the mate's rotation limits
- B) Visually simulate the rotating motion of the constrained part
- C) Copy the mate to another pair of parts
- D) Convert the Revolute mate to a Slider mate

**Answer:** B

**9.** If you want to simulate a pneumatic cylinder in an Onshape assembly, which combination of mates is most appropriate?
- A) Two Fastened mates
- B) A Revolute mate at the pivot and a Slider mate along the extension axis
- C) A Planar mate for the piston face and a Ball mate for the rod end
- D) One Slider mate at the piston and one Slider mate at the rod end

**Answer:** B

**10.** In the FRC context, what is the most important question that a well-built assembly model answers before fabrication begins?
- A) How much will the robot weigh?
- B) Which motors should be used?
- C) Does every part fit together without interference, and does every mechanism achieve its required range of motion?
- D) What is the robot's center of gravity?

**Answer:** C

### Reflections

1. Before today's session, how did your FRC team verify that parts would fit together before fabricating them? How does assembly modeling change or improve that process?

2. The Revolute mate with rotation limits models a mechanism's travel range. Think of a mechanism your team has built — an arm, an intake, a climber hook. What limits would you set on a Revolute mate for that mechanism, and how would you determine those limits?

3. Interference detection found a geometric conflict in your exercise. In the physical world, what does a geometric interference correspond to? What are the consequences of discovering this interference during a competition vs. during CAD review?

4. Large FRC assemblies can become very complex — dozens of parts, hundreds of mates. What organizational habits would you establish from day one to keep the assembly manageable throughout a six-week build season?

### Summary

- **Assembly mate:** A parametric relationship in an Onshape Assembly that defines how two parts are positioned and allowed to move relative to each other.
- **Fastened mate:** Locks all six degrees of freedom between two parts; used for bolted, welded, or otherwise rigidly connected joints.
- **Revolute mate:** Allows rotation around one axis only; used for pivots, hinges, bearings, and shaft connections in FRC mechanisms.
- **Slider mate:** Allows linear translation along one axis only; used for telescoping mechanisms, linear actuators, and sliding carriages.
- **Interference detection:** A CAD tool that identifies geometry from two parts occupying the same space, enabling collision problems to be found and fixed before fabrication.

## Session 3.5: Technical Drawings & Documentation
**Duration:** 60 minutes | **Format:** Guided documentation workshop

### Introduction

A 3D model in Onshape is a complete digital representation of a part — but it is not a fabrication document. Machinists and fabricators need technical drawings: flat, dimensioned views with standard annotations that communicate exactly what to build without ambiguity. In FRC, technical drawings serve another purpose: they let teammates who are not in the CAD model understand what a part looks like, create a record of design decisions, and support ordering COTS parts when you specify exactly what you need.

**What You Will Learn:**
- How to create an Onshape Drawing with standard orthographic views of a 3D part
- How to add dimensions, tolerances, and annotations to a drawing
- How to create and fill out a standard title block

### Core Concepts

#### Orthographic Views and the Standard Drawing Sheet
A technical drawing presents a 3D object as a set of 2D views projected onto a flat sheet. The standard set of views for a simple part is: Front view, Top view, Right-side view, and an Isometric view (a 3D perspective view for orientation). These four views together let a fabricator reconstruct the 3D shape from flat projections — similar to the way an X-ray and a CT scan together give a complete picture of an object.

Onshape generates these views automatically from your 3D model when you create a Drawing tab. You choose the base view (usually Front), and Onshape projects the other orthographic views in first-angle or third-angle projection (US standard is third-angle). The model geometry appears as solid lines for visible edges and dashed lines for hidden edges. Centerlines (thin dash-dot lines) mark symmetry axes and hole centers.

The drawing sheet has a standard frame and a title block in the lower-right corner. The title block records: part name, drawing number, revision, designer's name, date, material, surface finish, and general tolerances. Filling out the title block completely is a professional habit — a drawing with an incomplete title block cannot be confidently reproduced six months later when the original designer has graduated and a new team member needs to re-fabricate the part.

#### Dimensions and Tolerances
Dimensions on a technical drawing communicate the exact size of every feature a fabricator must create. Good dimensioning follows three rules: every feature must be dimensioned exactly once (no redundancy), dimensions must be arranged logically in the view where the feature is most clearly visible, and the fabricator should be able to read all dimensions without performing arithmetic.

Tolerances specify the acceptable range around a nominal dimension. A dimension of 1.000 ± 0.005 inches means the fabricator can produce anything from 0.995 to 1.005 inches and the part will function. FRC robots typically use general tolerances of ±0.010 inches for most features, with tighter tolerances (±0.001 inches) only for precision fits like bearings and shaft bushings. Over-tolerancing (specifying tighter tolerances than necessary) wastes machining time and money; under-tolerancing (too loose) produces parts that don't fit or function.

Geometric Dimensioning and Tolerancing (GD&T) is an advanced system of symbols that specifies not just size but geometric form, orientation, and position. A flatness callout, for example, specifies that a surface must lie within two parallel planes separated by the tolerance value — which is different from controlling the average size of the surface. FRC teams rarely implement full GD&T, but understanding the symbols helps you read drawings from vendors and interpret COTS part specifications correctly.

#### FRC Documentation Practices
Professional FRC teams maintain a drawing package: a set of numbered drawings for every custom-fabricated part on the robot. The drawing package serves as the manufacturing record, the basis for ordering spare parts, and the historical archive that future team members can study. When a bracket breaks at a competition, a team with a complete drawing package can hand the drawing to a local machine shop and have a replacement in hours. A team without documentation must reconstruct the part from memory or measurement of the broken piece.

Drawing revision control tracks changes to a design. Each revision gets a letter (Rev A, Rev B, Rev C) and a note in the revision block explaining what changed and when. When a bracket's hole pattern is modified after initial fabrication, the drawing is revised to match — both the old and new versions are preserved. FRC teams that adopt revision control from the start of their CAD program build an institutional knowledge base that survives student graduation and mentor turnover.

The Onshape Drawing tab updates automatically when the underlying Part Studio model changes — if you revise the bracket's hole diameter, the drawing dimensions update to match. This parametric link between model and drawing is a major advantage over drawing tools that require manual updates. However, it also means you must review the drawing after every model change to ensure dimensions are still complete and correct, and update the revision block accordingly.

### Video Lessons

#### Creating Technical Drawings in Onshape (~12 min)
**Channel:** Onshape
**Search:** "Onshape drawing tutorial orthographic views" on YouTube
**What to watch for:** How the instructor inserts the base view and then projects the other standard views. Notice how they position dimensions — always in the view where the feature is most clearly visible, never stacked on top of each other.

---

#### Reading Engineering Drawings for FRC (~10 min)
**Channel:** FRC mentor/technical channels
**Search:** "engineering drawing basics FRC manufacturing" on YouTube
**What to watch for:** How experienced FRC fabricators use a drawing in the shop — what they look at first, how they check dimensions before cutting, and what information they use to set up their tools.

### Practice Exercises

#### Exercise 1: Generate Standard Views (15 min)
**Objective:** Create a four-view drawing of the L-bracket from Session 3.3

1. Open your "Bracket_Drawing" tab in your Week 3 document
2. Insert a Front view of the L-bracket, positioned in the lower-left area of the sheet
3. Project the Top view above the Front view
4. Project the Right view to the right of the Front view
5. Add an Isometric view in the upper-right corner
6. Set the drawing scale to 1:1 (full scale)
7. Verify that hidden edges (bolt holes visible from one face but hidden in another view) appear as dashed lines

**Success Criteria:** Four correctly positioned and projected views at 1:1 scale, with hidden edges shown as dashed lines and all centerlines visible at hole locations.

---

#### Exercise 2: Dimensioning the Drawing (20 min)
**Objective:** Add a complete set of dimensions to the L-bracket drawing

1. Add overall dimensions (length, width, height of each leg) in the most appropriate view for each
2. Add hole location dimensions from a reference edge to each hole center
3. Add the hole diameter as a diameter dimension (with the diameter symbol)
4. Add the fillet radius callout
5. Add the material callout: "6061-T6 Aluminum"
6. Add a general tolerance note in the title area: "UNLESS OTHERWISE NOTED: +/-0.010"
7. Check: can a fabricator make this part from your drawing alone, without opening the 3D model?

**Success Criteria:** All features are dimensioned exactly once in the most appropriate view, no dimension requires arithmetic to use, and a mentor confirms the drawing is complete enough to fabricate from.

---

#### Exercise 3: Title Block Completion (15 min)
**Objective:** Fill out a professional title block for the L-bracket drawing

1. Fill in the part name: "L-Bracket, Drivetrain"
2. Fill in the drawing number: your team number followed by -BR-001 (e.g., 1234-BR-001)
3. Fill in your name as the designer and today's date
4. Fill in revision: Rev A
5. Fill in the material: 6061-T6 Aluminum
6. Add a revision block entry: "A | Initial Release | [today's date] | [your initials]"
7. Export the drawing as a PDF using File > Export and verify the exported file looks correct

**Success Criteria:** A complete, professional-looking PDF drawing with no blank title block fields, a revision block entry, and all required content visible at a readable scale.

### Knowledge Check

**1.** What is the US standard projection method for technical drawings?
- A) First-angle projection
- B) Third-angle projection
- C) Isometric projection
- D) Oblique projection

**Answer:** B

**2.** What do dashed lines on a technical drawing represent?
- A) Centerlines of holes and axes of symmetry
- B) Edges that are hidden behind other geometry in that view
- C) Dimensions that are approximate
- D) Lines that will be cut away during fabrication

**Answer:** B

**3.** What information does the title block of a technical drawing contain?
- A) Only the part name and dimensions
- B) Part name, drawing number, revision, designer, date, material, and general tolerances
- C) A complete parts list for the assembly
- D) The fabrication process steps in order

**Answer:** B

**4.** What does a general tolerance note of "+/-0.010" mean?
- A) All dimensions are exact to 0.010 inches
- B) Every dimension on the drawing must be measured to 0.010-inch precision
- C) Unless a dimension has its own tolerance, the acceptable range is +/-0.010 inches from the nominal value
- D) The drawing was created with 0.010-inch grid snapping enabled

**Answer:** C

**5.** Why is revision control important for FRC drawing packages?
- A) FRC rules require all drawings to have three revisions before competition
- B) Revision control is required by Onshape's licensing terms
- C) It tracks design changes and preserves the history so future team members can reconstruct decisions
- D) It prevents multiple students from editing the same drawing simultaneously

**Answer:** C

**6.** What happens to an Onshape Drawing when you change a dimension in the corresponding Part Studio?
- A) Nothing — the drawing must be manually updated
- B) The drawing is automatically deleted and must be recreated
- C) The drawing dimensions update automatically to reflect the model change
- D) The drawing is locked to prevent accidental changes

**Answer:** C

**7.** What are the four standard views typically included in a complete technical drawing?
- A) Front, Side, Back, Bottom
- B) Front, Top, Right-side, and Isometric
- C) Isometric, Perspective, Exploded, and Section
- D) North, South, East, West

**Answer:** B

**8.** What does over-tolerancing mean, and why is it a problem?
- A) Adding too many dimensions to one drawing view; causes clutter
- B) Specifying tighter tolerances than necessary; wastes machining time and increases cost
- C) Using a tolerance format that is not supported by the machinist's software
- D) Tolerancing a dimension that does not affect function

**Answer:** B

**9.** What is the practical value of a complete drawing package for an FRC team at competition?
- A) Teams with drawing packages are exempt from robot inspection
- B) Drawing packages are required for the Engineering Notebook award
- C) A broken part can be quickly reproduced by a local machine shop with a complete drawing
- D) Drawing packages allow teams to order replacement parts at half price from FRC vendors

**Answer:** C

**10.** What does the revision letter on a drawing represent?
- A) The quality grade of the part (A = highest quality)
- B) The generation of design change — each revision updates the letter to track what changed and when
- C) The approval signature of the head mentor
- D) The Onshape document version number

**Answer:** B

### Reflections

1. You have now gone from 2D sketch to 3D model to technical drawing. At which stage did the design feel most "real" to you, and why? What does that tell you about how different engineers might experience the design process differently?

2. A fabricator at your school looks at your drawing and says, "I can't figure out where this hole goes." What are the three most likely causes of that confusion, and how would you fix each one?

3. You learned that Onshape drawings update automatically when the model changes. This is an advantage — but it's also a risk. Describe a scenario where an automatic update to a drawing causes a problem rather than solving one.

4. Technical drawings have been the primary engineering communication tool for over 150 years. What are the advantages of a drawing over just sharing a 3D model file? In what situations might sharing the 3D model directly be better than a 2D drawing?

### Summary

- **Orthographic projection:** The method of representing a 3D object as multiple 2D views (Front, Top, Right, Isometric) projected onto flat planes; the foundation of engineering drawings.
- **Title block:** The standardized information block on a technical drawing that records part name, drawing number, revision, designer, date, material, and tolerances.
- **General tolerance:** A blanket tolerance (e.g., +/-0.010 inches) that applies to all undimensioned features on a drawing; eliminates the need to specify tolerances individually for every dimension.
- **Revision control:** The practice of tracking design changes with revision letters (A, B, C) and notes in the revision block, preserving the history of design decisions.
- **Drawing package:** The complete set of numbered technical drawings for all custom-fabricated parts on an FRC robot; the manufacturing record and institutional knowledge archive.

# WEEK 4: Measurement & Manufacturing

**Duration:** 4-5 hours
**Learning Style:** Hands-On Lab + Conceptual Instruction

You cannot build a competition robot without the ability to measure precisely. Every cut, every hole, every assembled joint depends on measurements that are accurate enough to function. This week transitions from CAD design into the physical world of measurement and manufacturing. You will learn the measurement systems used in FRC, how to use precision instruments, and how measurement feeds into the planning and execution of robot fabrication.

**Week 4 Learning Objectives:**
- Convert fluently between imperial and metric measurement units used in FRC
- Use a digital caliper to measure inside diameter, outside diameter, depth, and step dimensions
- Read and interpret a basic tolerance specification and determine if a measured part is in-spec
- Create a simple manufacturing plan that sequences operations for a custom bracket
- Apply measurement verification to confirm a fabricated part matches its drawing

---

## Session 4.1: Measurement Systems & Conversions
**Duration:** 60 minutes | **Format:** Instruction with conversion practice exercises

### Introduction

FRC robots live in two measurement worlds simultaneously. American vendors like REV Robotics and VEXpro publish hole patterns and tube dimensions in inches; international vendors, many sensors, and the metric hardware standard used by some teams operate in millimeters. The robot itself is inspected against rules written in both systems. Engineers who can convert fluently between systems — not by memorizing conversion factors but by understanding the underlying relationship — eliminate a constant source of errors and confusion.

**What You Will Learn:**
- The relationship between the imperial and metric measurement systems
- How to convert between inches, millimeters, feet, and centimeters
- How to identify which measurement system applies to common FRC hardware

### Core Concepts

#### The Imperial System in FRC
The imperial measurement system — inches, feet, yards, miles — is the dominant system in American manufacturing and in FRC. Structural aluminum tube stock is sold in 1-inch, 1.5-inch, and 2-inch square profiles. Fasteners are specified in a combined size/thread system: 10-32 means a #10 diameter (0.190 inches) with 32 threads per inch, and 1/4-20 means a 0.250-inch diameter with 20 threads per inch. The FRC playing field perimeter dimensions are given in feet and inches. Robot size limits (typically 28 x 28 x... inches) are in inches.

The fractional inch system that carpenters use (1/2 inch, 3/16 inch, 5/32 inch) is used for structural tube wall thicknesses, drill bit sizes, and some fastener head dimensions. Engineers in FRC eventually develop an intuitive feel for these numbers: 1/16 inch is 0.0625 inches, 1/8 inch is 0.125 inches, 1/4 inch is 0.250 inches. The ability to convert fractions to decimals and back instantly is a practical skill you will use every session for the rest of the curriculum.

The inch-pound system also governs torque specifications on FRC fasteners. Motor datasheets published by American vendors give stall torque in inch-pounds (in-lb) or foot-pounds (ft-lb). Robot arm calculations require converting between these units and understanding that 1 ft-lb equals 12 in-lb. When a gearbox datasheet says it can deliver 150 in-lb of continuous torque, you need to know whether that's enough to lift your game piece — and making that calculation requires fluency with the imperial system.

#### The Metric System and Where It Appears in FRC
The metric system — millimeters, centimeters, meters — is the international engineering standard and appears in FRC in several contexts. Many sensors (ultrasonic rangefinders, LIDAR units, cameras) report distances in millimeters or centimeters. The game field is often designed in metric dimensions at the international level. Some high-quality fasteners — particularly button-head cap screws used in weight-sensitive applications — are specified in the metric system (M3, M4, M5 designate thread pitch in millimeters). REV Robotics' NEO motor mounting pattern is in millimeters.

The fundamental metric relationship that matters most in FRC is: 1 inch = 25.4 millimeters exactly. This is not an approximation — the inch is legally defined as exactly 25.4 mm. Everything else follows from that: 1 foot = 304.8 mm, 1 yard = 914.4 mm. For practical purposes, engineers in FRC often remember: one inch is approximately 25 mm, one millimeter is approximately 0.040 inches (one-fortieth of an inch), and one centimeter is approximately 0.4 inches.

Mixing measurement systems is one of the most common sources of error in engineering. A hole drilled at 5mm when 5/16-inch (7.94mm) was needed will not accept the intended fastener. A tube cut at 300mm when 12 inches (304.8mm) was specified will create a 4.8mm gap — invisible to the eye but potentially significant for a precision mechanism. Establishing a clear convention (our robot design uses inches; all sensor data is converted to inches before use) and enforcing it consistently eliminates this entire category of error.

#### Conversion Techniques for FRC Engineers
The most reliable conversion technique is dimensional analysis: multiplying by conversion factors expressed as fractions, where the units cancel like variables in algebra. To convert 8.5 inches to millimeters: 8.5 in x (25.4 mm / 1 in) = 215.9 mm. The "in" units cancel, leaving mm. To convert 450 mm to inches: 450 mm x (1 in / 25.4 mm) = 17.72 inches. This technique generalizes to any unit conversion, including torque, speed, and force.

For rapid mental conversion in the shop, use these approximations: to convert inches to mm, multiply by 25 and add 2% (25.4, not 25). To convert mm to inches, divide by 25 and subtract a small correction. For torque: multiply in-lb by 0.113 to get N-m, or multiply N-m by 8.85 to get in-lb. These mental-math shortcuts let you quickly sanity-check calculations without reaching for a calculator.

FRC teams should establish a measurement policy at the start of each season: which system is primary for structure (almost always imperial), which system is used for sensors (often metric), and how conversions are documented in code and in CAD. Writing this policy down and sharing it with all team members takes ten minutes and prevents weeks of debugging mysterious half-centimeter offset errors in autonomous programs.

### Video Lessons

#### Measurement Systems for Engineers (~8 min)
**Channel:** Engineering educational channels
**Search:** "imperial metric conversion engineering dimensional analysis" on YouTube
**What to watch for:** The dimensional analysis technique — setting up conversion fractions so units cancel. This method works for every unit conversion you will ever need, not just length. Notice how the instructor always writes out the units explicitly rather than doing it "in their head."

---

#### FRC Robot Dimensions and Field Measurements (~6 min)
**Channel:** FRC Game Manual overviews / team training videos
**Search:** "FRC robot size rules measurements inches" on YouTube
**What to watch for:** How the game manual specifies robot constraints in specific units. Notice when they use fractional inches versus decimal inches, and when metric appears. This gives you a realistic sense of what measurement fluency looks like in competition context.

### Practice Exercises

#### Exercise 1: Conversion Drill (15 min)
**Objective:** Build conversion fluency through timed practice

Complete all of the following conversions. Show your work using dimensional analysis (write out the conversion fraction):

1. 18 inches to millimeters
2. 450 millimeters to inches
3. 2.75 feet to millimeters
4. 3/8 inch to millimeters
5. 12.7 millimeters to fractional inches (express as a fraction with denominator 16 or less)
6. 36 inches to centimeters
7. 150 in-lb to ft-lb
8. 4.5 ft-lb to in-lb
9. 500 mm to feet
10. 0.201 inches to millimeters (the 10-32 clearance hole diameter)

**Success Criteria:** All 10 conversions are correct to within 1% of the precise answer. You can perform items 1-6 without a calculator after one practice session.

---

#### Exercise 2: Hardware Identification by System (20 min)
**Objective:** Categorize common FRC hardware by its measurement system

Using hardware catalogs (REV Robotics, VEXpro, McMaster-Carr, or your team's existing hardware):

1. List 5 common FRC hardware items specified in imperial units (give the actual specification, e.g., "10-32 x 0.500 SHCS")
2. List 5 common FRC hardware items specified in metric units (give the actual specification, e.g., "M3 x 0.5 x 10mm SHCS")
3. For each item, explain how you determined which system applies (what was the clue in the part name or specification?)
4. Identify one situation where mixing these two systems on the same robot joint would cause a functional failure

**Success Criteria:** A complete table with 10 hardware items correctly categorized, with clear reasoning for each. Your failure scenario description is specific and plausible.

---

#### Exercise 3: Field Layout Measurement Challenge (15 min)
**Objective:** Apply measurement conversions to FRC field dimensions

Using the current year's FRC game manual (available at firstinspires.org):

1. Find three field element dimensions specified in the manual
2. Convert each dimension to the other measurement system (if given in inches, convert to mm; if given in mm, convert to inches)
3. Identify which measurements would be relevant to robot design (e.g., a game piece height affects intake design)
4. Calculate the FRC robot maximum size volume in cubic centimeters if the robot is 28 x 28 x 48 inches

**Success Criteria:** All three conversions are accurate, and you can explain why each field dimension matters to specific robot design decisions.

### Knowledge Check

**1.** What is the exact conversion factor between inches and millimeters?
- A) 1 inch = 24.5 mm (approximate)
- B) 1 inch = 25.4 mm (exact)
- C) 1 inch = 25.0 mm (rounded)
- D) 1 inch = 2.54 cm only (mm is not used directly)

**Answer:** B

**2.** What does "10-32" mean when specifying a fastener?
- A) 10mm diameter, 32mm thread pitch
- B) Size #10 diameter (0.190 inches) with 32 threads per inch
- C) 10/32 inch diameter (5/16 inch)
- D) Grade 10 fastener with 32 in-lb torque rating

**Answer:** B

**3.** Which measurement system is most commonly used for FRC structural tube stock and custom brackets?
- A) Metric (millimeters)
- B) Imperial (inches and fractional inches)
- C) Both systems equally
- D) Neither; FRC uses its own standardized system

**Answer:** B

**4.** Using dimensional analysis, what is the correct setup to convert 350 mm to inches?
- A) 350 mm + 25.4 = inches
- B) 350 mm / (1 in / 25.4 mm) = inches
- C) 350 mm x (1 in / 25.4 mm) = inches
- D) 350 mm x 25.4 = inches

**Answer:** C

**5.** Why does mixing measurement systems on the same robot joint cause problems?
- A) It violates FRC manufacturing rules
- B) A hole sized for an imperial fastener will not correctly fit a metric fastener of the "same" nominal size
- C) CAD software cannot display mixed-system assemblies
- D) Mixed-system robots are heavier than single-system robots

**Answer:** B

**6.** How many millimeters is 3/8 inch?
- A) 7.94 mm
- B) 9.53 mm
- C) 6.35 mm
- D) 8.00 mm

**Answer:** B

**7.** What is 150 in-lb converted to ft-lb?
- A) 1,800 ft-lb
- B) 150 ft-lb
- C) 12.5 ft-lb
- D) 12 ft-lb

**Answer:** C

**8.** Where does the metric system most commonly appear in FRC robot design?
- A) Structural tube lengths
- B) Fastener thread specifications for all bolts
- C) Sensor data, some motor mounting patterns, and international game field elements
- D) Battery specifications and weight limits

**Answer:** C

**9.** A machinist cuts a tube at 300 mm when the drawing specified 12 inches. What is the error in millimeters?
- A) 0 mm (they are the same)
- B) 4.8 mm
- C) 1.2 mm
- D) 25.4 mm

**Answer:** B

**10.** What is the best practice for preventing measurement system confusion on an FRC team?
- A) Use only metric for everything, since metric is more precise
- B) Use only imperial for everything, since FRC rules are written in imperial
- C) Establish a written measurement policy at the start of season defining which system applies where
- D) Have different subteams use different systems independently

**Answer:** C

### Reflections

1. Think about the last time a measurement error caused a problem on your team — a part that didn't fit, a cut that was wrong, a hole in the wrong place. Was the root cause a unit confusion, a reading error, a calculation error, or something else? How would you prevent it in the future?

2. The text says that "the inch is legally defined as exactly 25.4 mm." What does it mean for a unit to be "legally defined"? Who decides these definitions, and why does international standardization of measurement matter to engineering?

3. Your robot uses imperial hardware for structure but metric sensors reporting in millimeters. Where in your robot code would the conversion happen? Who is responsible for making sure it's correct — the mechanical subteam, the programming subteam, or both?

4. A new team member confidently declares "it doesn't matter which system we use as long as we're consistent." Is this statement fully correct, partially correct, or wrong? Explain your reasoning with a specific example.

### Summary

- **Imperial system:** The measurement system using inches, feet, and pounds; dominant in American FRC hardware, structural tube stock, and fastener specifications.
- **Metric system:** The international measurement system using millimeters, centimeters, and meters; used in sensor data, some motor mounting patterns, and international game field dimensions.
- **Dimensional analysis:** The unit-conversion technique of multiplying by conversion fractions so unwanted units cancel algebraically; reliable for any unit conversion.
- **Fractional inch:** The traditional American expression of sub-inch lengths as fractions (1/4, 3/8, 1/16); used for tube wall thicknesses, drill bit sizes, and some fastener head dimensions.
- **Measurement policy:** A team convention that specifies which measurement system applies in which contexts, preventing the mixed-system errors that cause mysterious fit problems and software bugs.

---

## Session 4.2: Precision Measurement Techniques
**Duration:** 60 minutes | **Format:** Lab with physical calipers (or digital simulation)

### Introduction

The ruler is the most limited precision tool in the shop. A ruler read carefully gives you about 1/32 inch (0.031 inches) of precision — good enough for rough carpentry but completely inadequate for FRC mechanisms where bearing bores must be within 0.002 inches of nominal and shaft fits must be repeatable across multiple parts. Precision measurement in FRC requires the digital caliper, a device that measures to 0.001-inch resolution and can be read unambiguously in seconds. This session teaches you to use a caliper correctly and to understand what your measurements mean.

**What You Will Learn:**
- How to read and use a digital caliper for outside diameter, inside diameter, depth, and step measurements
- How to apply measurement technique (clean surfaces, proper jaw contact, consistent force) for accurate readings
- How to record measurements and compare them to drawing specifications

### Core Concepts

#### The Digital Caliper: Anatomy and Function
A digital caliper has four measurement capabilities built into one instrument. The outside jaws (the large lower jaws) measure the outer dimensions of objects: shaft diameters, tube widths, plate thicknesses. The inside jaws (the small upper jaws) measure inner dimensions: hole diameters, slot widths, groove widths. The depth rod (the thin rod extending from the end of the caliper) measures the depth of holes and slots. The step measurement (the flat face at the end of the upper jaw) measures the height of steps and ledges on a part.

The digital display shows the current measurement in either inches or millimeters — press the in/mm button to switch. The zero button resets the display to zero at the current position, which is extremely useful for measuring differences: close the jaws, zero out, then open to your measurement. Most digital calipers display to 0.0005-inch resolution (half a thou), with accuracy of ±0.001 inches across the range. This is sufficient for all FRC measurement needs including bearing bores, shaft fits, and hole position checks.

Battery life is a practical concern in the shop. Most digital calipers use a single CR2032 lithium coin cell and will last months of regular use — but they always seem to die at the worst moment. Keep a spare battery in your measurement kit. Some calipers have an auto-shutoff feature; learn whether your caliper's off means "display off, remember zero" or "display off, reset to origin." Losing your zero reference mid-measurement because the caliper auto-shut off is a frustrating beginner mistake.

#### Measurement Technique and Sources of Error
Consistent measurement technique is as important as the instrument's precision. The most common source of caliper error is measuring angle — if the jaws are not perfectly perpendicular to the surface being measured, you read a larger value than the true dimension (because you're measuring a diagonal). When measuring a shaft, close the jaws perpendicular to the shaft axis and gently rock the caliper until the display reaches its minimum value — that minimum is the true diameter.

Surface cleanliness directly affects measurement accuracy. A chip of aluminum between the jaw and the part adds to the reading. Always wipe both jaws and the measured surface with a clean cloth before measuring a precision dimension. In an FRC shop environment where aluminum chips and cutting oil are everywhere, this step is especially critical. Measuring a bearing bore without cleaning it first is nearly worthless — the chip sitting in the bore may add 0.010 to 0.020 inches to your reading.

Measurement force — how hard you close the jaws — affects readings on soft or flexible materials. Aluminum tubing with thin walls (0.065-inch wall thickness) can flex when you squeeze the jaws. Soft O-rings and rubber bumpers will compress significantly under caliper jaw pressure. For these measurements, use light, consistent jaw pressure and take multiple readings to check repeatability. If three measurements of the same dimension give you results spread over 0.010 inches, the part or technique has an issue that needs investigation before proceeding.

#### Recording and Interpreting Measurements
Measurements only provide value when they are recorded and compared to specifications. The professional practice is to write measurements on paper (or in a digital table) as you take them, never to rely on memory. For an FRC bracket with 8 holes, create a table with columns: Feature, Nominal, Measured, In-Spec (Y/N), Notes. Nominal is the dimension from the drawing; Measured is what the caliper says; In-Spec compares the difference to the drawing's tolerance.

Statistical thinking improves measurement reliability. Rather than measuring one part once, measure the critical features of each fabricated part and build a small dataset. If you fabricated five brackets and all five have the bearing bore at 1.124 to 1.126 inches against a nominal of 1.125 ± 0.002 inches, your process is in good control. If one part measures 1.130 inches (out of spec), you have identified a problem part before it causes a field failure.

First Article Inspection (FAI) is the practice of fully measuring the first part produced by a new process before committing to producing more. On FRC teams, FAI means: when a new bracket design is cut for the first time, fully measure every critical dimension before bolting it into the robot. Finding that the hole pattern is 0.050 inches off at FAI prevents building an entire robot around a flawed bracket. Rebuilding after discovering the error at competition is far more painful.

### Video Lessons

#### How to Use a Digital Caliper (~10 min)
**Channel:** Adam Savage's Tested / Practical Machinist channels
**Search:** "how to use digital caliper correctly technique" on YouTube
**What to watch for:** The instructor demonstrating all four measurement modes: OD, ID, depth, and step. Pay close attention to how they position the jaws relative to the part — perpendicular, with light consistent pressure. Notice how they wipe the jaws before each measurement.

---

#### Caliper Measurement for FRC Parts (~8 min)
**Channel:** FRC team training / mentorship videos
**Search:** "FRC precision measurement caliper robot parts" on YouTube
**What to watch for:** Real FRC parts being measured — shafts, bearing bores, tube walls. What does the presenter say when the measurement doesn't match the drawing? How do they decide if the part is acceptable?

### Practice Exercises

#### Exercise 1: Caliper Familiarization (15 min)
**Objective:** Use all four caliper measurement modes on household or shop objects

Using a digital caliper and at least 5 different objects from your shop or classroom:

1. Measure the outside diameter (OD) of a round object (pen, marker, shaft)
2. Measure the inside diameter (ID) of a hole or tube
3. Measure the depth of a pocket or blind hole
4. Measure a step height on a machined part or fitting
5. Switch between inch and mm mode and confirm that the readings are consistent (one should be exactly 25.4x the other)
6. Record all measurements in a table with units

**Success Criteria:** Five measurements across all four modes with consistent readings (repeat each measurement 3 times; all three readings within 0.003 inches of each other).

---

#### Exercise 2: Part Inspection Against Drawing (20 min)
**Objective:** Perform a dimensional inspection of a fabricated FRC part

Obtain a fabricated FRC bracket or plate (from your team's spare parts or a sample provided by your mentor):

1. Create an inspection table with columns: Feature, Nominal Dimension, Tolerance, Measured Value, In-Spec (Y/N)
2. Measure and record at least 6 features: overall length, width, thickness, and 3 hole positions
3. Use the caliper depth rod to measure any countersinks or counterbores
4. Compare each measurement to the drawing or specification and mark In-Spec or Out-of-Spec
5. If any feature is out-of-spec, describe what functional problem it would cause on the robot

**Success Criteria:** A complete inspection table with 6 or more measurements, correctly compared to specifications, with a clear pass/fail determination for each feature.

---

#### Exercise 3: Measurement Repeatability Study (15 min)
**Objective:** Quantify the variability in your own measurement technique

1. Select one critical dimension on a part (a shaft diameter or bearing bore works well)
2. Measure that same dimension 10 times, recording each reading
3. Calculate the range (maximum minus minimum) of your 10 readings
4. Calculate the average of your 10 readings
5. Determine: is the variability in your readings due to technique (inconsistent jaw angle, force) or actual part variation?
6. Improve your technique and repeat 10 more measurements — did your range decrease?

**Success Criteria:** Two sets of 10 measurements with documented averages and ranges. Your second set has a tighter range than your first set, showing measurable technique improvement.

### Knowledge Check

**1.** Which part of a digital caliper is used to measure the diameter of a shaft?
- A) Inside jaws (upper small jaws)
- B) Outside jaws (lower large jaws)
- C) Depth rod
- D) Step face

**Answer:** B

**2.** What is the most common cause of incorrect caliper readings when measuring round parts?
- A) Using the wrong battery
- B) Measuring in millimeters instead of inches
- C) Holding the jaws at an angle instead of perpendicular to the measurement axis
- D) Not zeroing the caliper first

**Answer:** C

**3.** Why should you wipe caliper jaws and the measured surface before taking a precision measurement?
- A) To prevent the jaws from scratching the part
- B) Chips or debris between jaw and part will add to the reading, producing an artificially high value
- C) Oil on the jaws causes the display to malfunction
- D) It is a workshop safety rule, not a measurement accuracy issue

**Answer:** B

**4.** What does "First Article Inspection" mean in manufacturing?
- A) The inspection of the robot at the first competition of the season
- B) Fully measuring the first part produced by a new process before committing to more production
- C) The first time a student is taught to use measurement tools
- D) Checking that the first article of hardware purchased matches the order

**Answer:** B

**5.** A bearing bore measures 1.128 inches against a nominal of 1.125 ± 0.002 inches. Is this part in spec?
- A) Yes — 1.128 is close enough
- B) No — 1.128 exceeds the upper limit of 1.127 inches
- C) Yes — the 0.003-inch error is within the general shop tolerance
- D) Cannot determine without knowing the bearing's OD

**Answer:** B

**6.** Which caliper measurement mode would you use to measure the depth of a counterbore?
- A) Outside jaw measurement
- B) Inside jaw measurement
- C) Depth rod measurement
- D) Step face measurement

**Answer:** C

**7.** If you measure the same dimension 10 times and get readings ranging from 0.498 to 0.512 inches, what does this spread indicate?
- A) The part is definitely out of specification
- B) The caliper is broken and needs replacement
- C) High variability in technique or actual part variation that requires investigation
- D) Normal measurement precision; all readings within 0.015 inches are acceptable

**Answer:** C

**8.** What resolution does a typical digital caliper provide?
- A) 0.1 inch
- B) 0.01 inch
- C) 0.001 inch
- D) 0.0001 inch

**Answer:** C

**9.** Why is measurement force important when measuring thin-walled aluminum tube?
- A) More force is needed to get the jaws to close on hard aluminum
- B) Thin-walled tube can flex under jaw pressure, giving a reading smaller than the true outer dimension
- C) Insufficient force causes the caliper display to fluctuate
- D) Force affects the inside jaw measurement only, not outside jaw

**Answer:** B

**10.** What is the purpose of recording measurements in a table rather than relying on memory?
- A) FRC inspection requires a written measurement log
- B) It enables comparison to specifications, detection of out-of-spec parts, and a quality record
- C) It helps the CAD team update the model with as-built dimensions
- D) Written records are required for the Engineering Notebook award

**Answer:** B

### Reflections

1. Before today, what was your mental model of "precise enough"? How has learning about caliper precision (0.001-inch resolution, ±0.001-inch accuracy) changed your standard for when a measurement is good enough?

2. You performed a repeatability study and (hopefully) improved your range in the second set. What specifically did you change in your technique? What does it feel like to measure with good technique versus sloppy technique?

3. First Article Inspection takes time — maybe 15 minutes for a complex bracket. Is that time always justified? Describe the conditions under which you would decide to skip FAI for a particular part, and the conditions under which it would be non-negotiable.

4. Think about the measurement tools your team currently uses. Are there situations where you have been using a less precise tool (ruler, tape measure) for something that should have been measured with a caliper? What are the downstream consequences of those imprecise measurements?

### Summary

- **Digital caliper:** A precision measurement instrument with four modes (outside jaw, inside jaw, depth rod, step face) capable of measuring to 0.001-inch resolution.
- **Outside diameter (OD) measurement:** Using the large lower jaws to measure the external dimension of round or flat objects; the most common caliper measurement mode.
- **Inside diameter (ID) measurement:** Using the small upper jaws to measure the internal dimension of holes, slots, and bores.
- **Measurement repeatability:** The consistency of readings when the same dimension is measured multiple times; high repeatability indicates good technique and a stable part.
- **First Article Inspection (FAI):** The practice of fully measuring the first fabricated part against its drawing before committing to further production or assembly.

---

## Session 4.3: Advanced Measurement Applications
**Duration:** 60 minutes | **Format:** Applied measurement lab

### Introduction

Beyond the caliper, FRC teams use several other measurement tools and techniques for specific applications. Measuring angles, confirming squareness, checking hole patterns, and verifying flatness all require techniques beyond straight-line caliper work. This session expands your measurement toolkit and connects measurement decisions to real fabrication outcomes — teaching you not just how to measure but when each measurement matters most.

**What You Will Learn:**
- How to use a combination square and angle gauge for angular measurement
- How to check squareness and flatness of fabricated assemblies
- How to verify a hole pattern matches a drawing using layout techniques

### Core Concepts

#### Angular Measurement and Squareness Checking
A combination square is a versatile measuring tool that serves multiple functions: a ruler with a sliding head that can be locked at 90 degrees or 45 degrees, a scriber for layout lines, and a level vial for checking horizontal or vertical. The 90-degree blade face lets you check whether two surfaces of a part are truly perpendicular — a check that matters enormously for robot structure where an un-square chassis frame will cause tracking and steering problems.

To check squareness of a corner joint (two tubes joined at 90 degrees), place the body of the combination square flat on one tube and observe whether the blade lies flush against the second tube. Any gap between blade and tube indicates deviation from true 90 degrees. For FRC chassis frames, the allowable squareness error depends on the application: a rough structure might accept 1/16-inch gap over 12 inches, but a precision mechanism with a close-fitting intake demands much tighter squareness.

Digital angle gauges (also called digital inclinometers or protractors) measure surface angles directly. Place the gauge on a surface and it reads the angle relative to horizontal (gravity) in degrees to 0.1-degree resolution. This is useful for checking the angle of an arm mechanism relative to horizontal, verifying that a diagonal frame member is at its intended angle, and confirming that an intake geometry matches the CAD model. Low-cost digital angle gauges (under $30) are a worthwhile investment for any FRC team's measurement toolkit.

#### Hole Pattern Verification
A hole pattern — the arrangement of bolt holes on a bracket or plate — must match the mating part precisely for assembly to work. Verifying a hole pattern requires measuring the distance from one hole center to another (center-to-center distance, or bolt circle diameter for circular patterns). The challenge is that caliper jaws cannot sit inside a hole and give a direct center-to-center reading; instead, you measure from edge to edge and calculate center-to-center.

The edge-to-edge to center-to-center conversion is simple: if you measure from the near edges of two holes, add the hole diameter to get center-to-center. If you measure from far edge to far edge, subtract the hole diameter. For example, two 0.201-inch holes measured from near edge to near edge at 0.799 inches are actually 0.799 + 0.201 = 1.000 inch center-to-center. This arithmetic is so common in FRC measurement that experienced team members do it automatically.

For complex hole patterns with many holes, layout verification is faster than measuring every pair of holes individually. Scribe lines on the part using a layout fluid (blue dye) and a scribe, then use a center punch to mark the intended hole locations before drilling. After drilling, compare the actual hole positions to the scribed marks. Any hole that is not centered on its punch mark indicates a drill drift problem that should be investigated before more holes are drilled with the same setup.

#### Flatness and Alignment Checks
Flatness matters when two parts must mate cleanly along a surface — a motor mounting plate must be flat so the motor sits without rocking, a gearbox mounting face must be flat so the gearbox doesn't preload its own bearings when bolted down. To check flatness, lay a known-flat reference surface (a surface plate, a piece of precision ground stock, or a glass table top) on the part and look for gaps using a feeler gauge or by observing light gaps. A flatness error of more than 0.010 inches on a motor mounting face can cause bearing preload and accelerate motor wear.

Alignment checks verify that two features are correctly positioned relative to each other in three dimensions. Shaft alignment is critical for gearbox and drivetrain systems: if the input shaft is not collinear with the gearbox output shaft, the coupling will wear rapidly and vibrate. FRC teams check shaft alignment by spanning two shafts with a straightedge and observing whether the shafts are parallel and whether they are at the same height. A dial indicator (a precision clock-style gauge that measures small displacements) can quantify misalignment to 0.001-inch precision.

The most important alignment check for FRC drivetrains is wheel parallelism: all drive wheels must point in the same direction (or in the correct toe-in angle for swerve drives). Checking wheel parallelism can be done with a long straightedge alongside both wheels, or by pushing the robot a known distance and measuring how far it veers from a straight line. Teams that skip this check often wonder why their robot can't drive straight and spend hours tuning software when the real problem is a mechanical misalignment.

### Video Lessons

#### Combination Square and Layout Tools (~8 min)
**Channel:** Machining/Workshop skill channels
**Search:** "combination square how to use layout line machining" on YouTube
**What to watch for:** How the instructor uses the combination square to scribe lines parallel to an edge and to check squareness of corners. The layout scribing technique is widely used in FRC fabrication and is faster and more reliable than measuring with a ruler.

---

#### Checking Alignment on FRC Drivetrains (~10 min)
**Channel:** FRC mechanical build channels
**Search:** "FRC drivetrain wheel alignment parallel check" on YouTube
**What to watch for:** What physical check the presenter uses to verify wheel parallelism. What happens to the robot's driving behavior when wheels are misaligned? How does the presenter correct the alignment?

### Practice Exercises

#### Exercise 1: Squareness Check (15 min)
**Objective:** Assess the squareness of a sample assembly using a combination square

1. Obtain or assemble a two-tube corner joint (two aluminum tubes connected at approximately 90 degrees)
2. Place the combination square body flat on one tube and the blade against the second tube
3. Measure the gap between blade and tube at the far end of the blade
4. Calculate the angular error: gap (inches) / blade length (inches) = tan(angle error)
5. Convert to degrees of error
6. Determine: is this assembly square enough for a chassis frame? For a precision mechanism bearing?

**Success Criteria:** A documented squareness measurement with calculated angular error and a justified pass/fail determination for two different application requirements.

---

#### Exercise 2: Hole Pattern Measurement (20 min)
**Objective:** Verify that a fabricated hole pattern matches drawing specifications

1. Obtain a bracket with a 4-hole or 6-hole pattern
2. Using a caliper, measure all edge-to-edge distances between pairs of holes
3. Convert each measurement to center-to-center distance (add hole diameter to near-edge measurements)
4. Compare to the drawing specification (or a known-correct part)
5. Create a table showing: hole pair, edge-to-edge measurement, calculated C-C distance, nominal C-C distance, error
6. Determine which holes (if any) are out of position by more than 0.010 inches

**Success Criteria:** A complete hole pattern measurement table with correct edge-to-edge to C-C conversions and a clear assessment of which holes meet the ±0.010-inch position tolerance.

---

#### Exercise 3: Alignment Assessment (15 min)
**Objective:** Measure shaft or wheel alignment using available tools

1. Set up two shafts or axles in a simple fixture (or use two parallel tubes as stand-ins)
2. Span them with a straightedge and observe the gap at each shaft (or tube face)
3. Measure any gaps with a feeler gauge or caliper
4. Calculate the angular misalignment: gap difference / distance between shaft centers = tan(angle)
5. Assess: would this misalignment be acceptable for a belt drive system? For a chain drive system? Why are tolerances different for different drive types?

**Success Criteria:** A documented alignment measurement with calculated angular misalignment and a reasoned comparison of the tolerance requirements for two different drive transmission types.

### Knowledge Check

**1.** What is a combination square used for in FRC fabrication?
- A) Measuring angles larger than 90 degrees
- B) Scribing layout lines, checking squareness of corners, and verifying 45-degree angles
- C) Measuring hole diameters and thread pitches
- D) Checking shaft runout and alignment

**Answer:** B

**2.** How do you calculate center-to-center distance from an edge-to-edge measurement between two holes?
- A) Subtract the hole diameter from the measurement
- B) Add the hole diameter to the near-edge-to-near-edge measurement
- C) Multiply the measurement by pi divided by 4
- D) The edge-to-edge measurement equals the center-to-center distance for holes of any size

**Answer:** B

**3.** What does wheel parallelism mean, and why does it matter for FRC drivetrains?
- A) All wheels must be the same diameter; different diameters cause speed differences
- B) All drive wheels must point in the same direction; misalignment causes the robot to veer instead of driving straight
- C) All wheels must rotate at the same speed; speed differences cause turning
- D) Wheel parallelism is an aesthetic concern, not a functional one

**Answer:** B

**4.** What tool would you use to check that a motor mounting plate is flat?
- A) Digital caliper — measure thickness at multiple points
- B) Combination square — check corners of the plate
- C) A known-flat reference surface with feeler gauges to detect gaps
- D) A digital angle gauge placed on the plate surface

**Answer:** C

**5.** What is a feeler gauge used for?
- A) Measuring the diameter of small holes
- B) Measuring small gaps between surfaces
- C) Checking the thread pitch of fasteners
- D) Measuring the deflection of bent metal

**Answer:** B

**6.** What does layout fluid (blue dye) do in the fabrication process?
- A) Lubricates drill bits to prevent overheating
- B) Coats the part surface so scribed lines are clearly visible
- C) Marks holes that have been drilled to prevent double-drilling
- D) Protects aluminum from oxidation during fabrication

**Answer:** B

**7.** What is a dial indicator used to measure?
- A) Hole diameters with 0.001-inch precision
- B) Shaft alignment and small surface displacements with 0.001-inch precision
- C) Angle of surfaces relative to horizontal
- D) Center-to-center distances between holes

**Answer:** B

**8.** A motor mounting face has a flatness error of 0.015 inches. What problem can this cause?
- A) The motor screws will not fit
- B) The motor will preload its own bearings when bolted down, accelerating wear
- C) The motor will run hotter due to reduced airflow
- D) Flatness errors above 0.010 inches violate FRC inspection rules

**Answer:** B

**9.** What does "toe-in" mean for a swerve drive FRC robot?
- A) The wheels are angled slightly inward from parallel to provide steering stability
- B) The wheels must be perpendicular to the chassis centerline at all times
- C) The front wheels are set higher than the rear wheels
- D) Individual wheel modules are toed in to reduce carpet scrub during rotation

**Answer:** A

**10.** Why is it faster to use layout scribing with a center punch than to individually measure every hole position after drilling?
- A) Scribing is more accurate than measuring
- B) Layout marks the intended positions before drilling so any drift is immediately visible during or after drilling, preventing multiple rework cycles
- C) FRC rules require all holes to be center-punched
- D) Scribing eliminates the need for a drawing

**Answer:** B

### Reflections

1. You measured squareness and found an angular error. In the physical robot, when does a small squareness error compound into a large functional problem? Give a specific mechanism example where 1 degree of squareness error would be acceptable versus unacceptable.

2. Hole pattern measurement required converting edge-to-edge readings to center-to-center distances. This kind of indirect measurement — measuring one thing to determine another — is common in engineering. Give two other examples of indirect measurement from any field of engineering or science.

3. You assessed shaft alignment. Many FRC teams rely on bolt holes to ensure alignment instead of measuring — they assume that if the holes match, the shafts will be aligned. Under what conditions is this assumption valid, and when does it break down?

4. Your team has limited time before ship day and needs to decide which of the following measurements to perform: (a) check wheel parallelism, (b) verify hole pattern on all six drive tube gussets, (c) check flatness of the battery mounting plate. Rank these by importance and justify your ranking.

### Summary

- **Combination square:** A multi-function layout and measurement tool for scribing parallel lines, checking 90- and 45-degree angles, and verifying squareness of assembled joints.
- **Center-to-center distance:** The distance between the centers of two holes; calculated from edge-to-edge caliper measurements by adding the hole diameter.
- **Flatness:** The condition of a surface lying within two parallel planes of specified separation; critical for motor mounting faces and precision mating surfaces.
- **Layout scribing:** The technique of marking intended hole positions on a part surface before drilling, using layout fluid and a scribe, to verify position accuracy during fabrication.
- **Shaft alignment:** The condition of two connected shafts being collinear (or parallel at a specified offset); misalignment causes vibration, accelerated wear, and power loss in transmissions.

---

## Session 4.4: Manufacturing Planning Workshop
**Duration:** 60 minutes | **Format:** Workshop with planning exercises

### Introduction

Measuring a finished part is quality control. Planning the measurements and operations before you start is manufacturing engineering. FRC teams that produce high-quality parts consistently don't get there by accident — they think through the sequence of cuts, holes, and bends before the first chip is made. This session teaches you to create a manufacturing plan: a step-by-step sequence of operations with the tools, measurements, and verification points needed to fabricate a part correctly the first time.

**What You Will Learn:**
- How to analyze a part drawing and identify the correct fabrication sequence
- How to select appropriate tools and measurement checkpoints for each operation
- How to write a manufacturing plan that another student could follow without supervision

### Core Concepts

#### Reading a Drawing for Fabrication
When a machinist or student receives a technical drawing, the first step is not picking up a saw — it is reading the drawing completely and developing a mental model of the finished part. This reading has a specific order: first look at the overall dimensions to understand size and material, then examine the most complex or most critical feature (usually a precision bore or a tight-tolerance position), then work outward to understand how that feature relates to everything else.

Critical features deserve special attention. A critical feature is any dimension with a tight tolerance, any feature whose error would cause immediate functional failure, or any feature that is difficult to re-machine if done incorrectly. For a drivetrain bearing block, the critical feature is the bearing bore — its diameter and position relative to the mounting holes. For a gusset plate, the critical feature is the hole pattern. Identifying critical features before starting fabrication focuses your attention and measurement effort where they matter most.

Drawing notes carry equal weight to dimensions. A note saying "DRILL AND TAP 10-32 x 0.375 DEEP" means the hole must be tapped (threaded) to 0.375 inches deep, not drilled through. A note saying "BREAK ALL SHARP EDGES 0.030 MAX" means you must deburr every edge to a 0.030-inch maximum. A note saying "ALL DIMS ± 0.010 UNLESS NOTED" establishes the general tolerance for all dimensions that don't have their own tolerance callout. Ignoring notes causes non-conforming parts just as surely as ignoring dimensions.

#### Operation Sequencing
Manufacturing operations have dependencies — some operations must happen before others. The general rule for flat-plate FRC parts is: cut to overall size first, then drill holes (holes in a rough blank are easier to locate than holes in a finished part with other features already present), then tap threaded holes, then deburr, then bend if needed, then verify. The rationale is that each step is easier to perform before subsequent operations constrain where tools can go.

For multi-feature parts (parts with pockets, counterbores, and through-holes in multiple patterns), the sequencing becomes more complex. Parts machined on a CNC router or laser cutter have their sequence determined by the toolpath software. Parts made on manual equipment (drill press, band saw, mill) require the machinist to plan operations to avoid re-fixturing (repositioning and re-clamping the part) more than necessary — each refixturing introduces potential alignment error.

Time estimation is an underrated manufacturing planning skill. How long will it take to cut one bracket on the band saw, drill and tap its holes, and deburr it? If you need eight brackets, what is the total fabrication time? FRC teams that underestimate fabrication time arrive at competitions with unfinished robots. Building a realistic time budget requires knowing the setup time for each operation (often longer than the cut time), accounting for interruptions, and including time for measurement and inspection.

#### Quality Checkpoints
Quality checkpoints are prescribed moments in the fabrication sequence where progress stops and a specific measurement or inspection occurs before proceeding. They prevent compounding errors: fabricating all eight brackets before discovering the first one has a systematically mislocated hole is far more costly than discovering it after the first bracket.

The first checkpoint is always after the first part of a batch: inspect the first bracket fully before cutting the remaining seven. The second checkpoint type is after each operation that has a tolerance that affects the next operation: measure the cut length before drilling, because the drill jig position depends on the cut length being correct. The third checkpoint type is at assembly: test-fit parts together before final assembly with fasteners, and check fit again after each subassembly is complete.

Documenting quality checkpoints in a plan creates accountability. If the plan says "CHECKPOINT: Measure OD of bearing bore, nominal 1.125 ± 0.002, STOP if out of spec," any student following the plan will perform that measurement and know when to stop. Without documentation, quality checkpoints depend entirely on individual judgment — and under time pressure, individual judgment often decides that "close enough" is good enough when it isn't.

### Video Lessons

#### Manufacturing Process Planning Introduction (~10 min)
**Channel:** Manufacturing engineering educational channels
**Search:** "manufacturing process planning operations sequence machining" on YouTube
**What to watch for:** How the instructor reads a drawing and identifies the fabrication sequence. Notice how they identify "critical" features and why those features dictate the sequence of other operations.

---

#### FRC Parts Manufacturing Workflow (~12 min)
**Channel:** FRC team build season content
**Search:** "FRC robot part fabrication bracket drill press sequence" on YouTube
**What to watch for:** The actual tools and sequence a real FRC team uses to produce a batch of brackets. How long does each operation take? Where do they stop to measure? What happens when a part comes out wrong?

### Practice Exercises

#### Exercise 1: Drawing Analysis (15 min)
**Objective:** Read a bracket drawing and identify all critical features and notes

Using the L-bracket drawing created in Session 3.5 (or a drawing provided by your mentor):

1. List all dimensions and categorize each as: overall, feature position, feature size, or tolerance
2. Identify the 2 most critical features and justify your selection
3. List all drawing notes and interpret each note in plain language (what does the fabricator need to do?)
4. Identify any information missing from the drawing that a fabricator would need to complete the part

**Success Criteria:** A complete annotated list of all dimensions and notes, with critical features clearly identified and justified, and any missing information noted.

---

#### Exercise 2: Operation Sequence Development (20 min)
**Objective:** Write a step-by-step manufacturing plan for the L-bracket

1. Write the operation sequence for fabricating the L-bracket from a piece of 6061-T6 aluminum flat stock
2. For each operation, specify: what operation is performed, what tool is used, what dimension or feature is produced, and what measurement verifies success
3. Identify quality checkpoints and mark them clearly in the sequence (e.g., "CHECKPOINT 1: Measure overall length")
4. Estimate the time required for each operation and total fabrication time
5. Have a teammate read your plan and identify any steps they find unclear or missing

**Success Criteria:** A manufacturing plan with at least 8 distinct operations, quality checkpoints after critical operations, time estimates, and peer review comments addressed.

---

#### Exercise 3: Batch Fabrication Analysis (15 min)
**Objective:** Scale a single-part plan to a batch and identify where errors could compound

1. Using your manufacturing plan from Exercise 2, estimate the total time to fabricate 6 L-brackets as a batch
2. Identify which operations benefit from batch processing (doing all 6 at once) versus single-part processing
3. Identify the operation where a systematic error (a jig set 0.050 inches off) would affect all 6 brackets identically
4. Describe the quality checkpoint that would catch this systematic error after the first bracket
5. Calculate: if you discover the systematic error on bracket 4, how much rework is required?

**Success Criteria:** A complete batch analysis with batch/single-part operation classification, systematic error identification, and a calculation of the rework cost of delayed error detection.

### Knowledge Check

**1.** What should a machinist do first when receiving a technical drawing?
- A) Immediately begin setting up tools
- B) Read the drawing completely to understand the finished part before touching any material
- C) Identify the cheapest material that meets the specification
- D) Calculate the total machining time before accepting the job

**Answer:** B

**2.** What is a "critical feature" on a technical drawing?
- A) Any feature that appears in the title block
- B) A dimension with a tight tolerance, a feature whose error causes immediate functional failure, or a feature that cannot be re-machined if wrong
- C) The largest dimension on the drawing
- D) Any feature that requires a CNC machine to produce

**Answer:** B

**3.** What is the general correct sequence for fabricating a flat-plate bracket?
- A) Drill holes first, then cut to size, then deburr
- B) Deburr first, then cut, then drill holes
- C) Cut to size, then drill holes, then tap, then deburr
- D) The sequence does not matter as long as all operations are completed

**Answer:** C

**4.** Why is it important to inspect the first bracket before fabricating the remaining seven in a batch?
- A) FRC rules require inspection of all parts before use
- B) The first part reveals any systematic errors in the setup before they repeat across the entire batch
- C) The first part requires special treatment to "break in" the tooling
