- D) Inspection slows production, so it should be done only at the end

**Answer:** B

**5.** What does a drawing note "DRILL AND TAP 10-32 x 0.375 DEEP" instruct the fabricator to do?
- A) Drill a through-hole with a 10-32 tap drill and tap to 0.375-inch depth from both sides
- B) Drill a hole with the 10-32 tap drill and cut 10-32 threads to 0.375-inch depth (a threaded blind hole)
- C) Drill a 0.375-inch diameter hole and tap it for a 10-32 screw
- D) Thread the hole all the way through and stop at the 0.375-inch mark from the top

**Answer:** B

**6.** What is the primary purpose of a quality checkpoint in a manufacturing plan?
- A) To slow production and allow more time for each operation
- B) To document progress for the Engineering Notebook
- C) To stop fabrication and verify a critical dimension before proceeding, preventing compounding errors
- D) To give students a break during long fabrication sessions

**Answer:** C

**7.** What does "refixturing" mean in manufacturing?
- A) Repairing a fixture that has been damaged
- B) Repositioning and re-clamping a part in a different orientation, which introduces potential alignment error
- C) Using the same fixture for a different type of part
- D) Fixing a quality problem found after fabrication

**Answer:** B

**8.** Why is time estimation an important manufacturing planning skill for FRC teams?
- A) FRC competition rules specify maximum fabrication times
- B) Underestimating fabrication time leads to arriving at competition with an unfinished robot
- C) Time estimates are required in the Engineering Notebook
- D) Manufacturing time determines which students are assigned to which tasks

**Answer:** B

**9.** What is "batch processing" in manufacturing?
- A) Processing parts in alphabetical order
- B) Completing the same operation on multiple parts before moving to the next operation
- C) Fabricating each part completely before starting the next one
- D) Using automated machinery to process parts without human intervention

**Answer:** B

**10.** What is a "drawing note" and why does it carry equal weight to dimensions?
- A) A note is a suggestion, not a requirement; it carries less weight than dimensions
- B) A note is text on a drawing that specifies requirements not captured by dimensions — such as thread depth, surface finish, or edge treatment — and must be followed exactly
- C) Drawing notes are only read by the design engineer, not the fabricator
- D) Notes specify the material cost and are only relevant to purchasing

**Answer:** B

### Reflections

1. You wrote a manufacturing plan and had a teammate review it. What gaps or ambiguities did they find? What does this reveal about the difference between knowledge that lives in your head versus knowledge that can be communicated through written instructions?

2. The session described "systematic errors" — errors in setup that affect every part in a batch identically. How is a systematic error different from a random error? Give an example of each type from your FRC fabrication experience.

3. Think about a batch of parts your team has fabricated. Did you use batch processing (same operation on all parts before moving on) or single-part processing (each part finished before starting the next)? What were the advantages and disadvantages of the approach you used?

4. Manufacturing plans add time upfront but can save significant rework time later. At what point in the FRC build season is taking time to write a detailed manufacturing plan most justified, and at what point might a less formal approach be acceptable?

### Summary

- **Critical feature:** A dimension or geometric feature whose error would cause immediate functional failure or that cannot be re-machined; deserves special planning and measurement attention.
- **Operation sequence:** The prescribed order of fabrication steps for a part; dependencies between operations determine which must come first to avoid error and rework.
- **Quality checkpoint:** A prescribed pause in fabrication where a specific measurement is taken and evaluated before proceeding; prevents compounding errors from propagating through a batch.
- **Batch processing:** Performing the same operation on all parts in a group before moving to the next operation; increases efficiency but requires error detection after the first part.
- **Refixturing:** Repositioning a part in a new fixture or clamp orientation; introduces potential alignment error and should be minimized by planning operations that can be completed in one setup.

---

## Session 4.5: Measurement Assessment & Integration
**Duration:** 60 minutes | **Format:** Practical assessment with reflection

### Introduction

This session is your measurement capstone. You will apply everything from Sessions 4.1 through 4.4 — unit conversion, caliper technique, advanced measurement, and manufacturing planning — to a complete, integrated task. Rather than practicing skills in isolation, you will work through a realistic FRC scenario: receiving a bracket drawing, verifying a fabricated part, documenting results, and writing a manufacturing plan for the next batch. This is what measurement looks like in practice on a competitive team.

**What You Will Learn:**
- How to apply measurement skills in an integrated, realistic FRC fabrication scenario
- How to communicate measurement results clearly in a documentation format
- How to identify where your measurement knowledge is strong and where further practice is needed

### Core Concepts

#### Integration: From Drawing to Verified Part
The complete measurement workflow for an FRC part begins with reading the drawing and ends with a documented inspection record. Between those endpoints, the workflow includes: identifying critical features from the drawing, selecting measurement instruments appropriate to each feature's tolerance, executing measurements with correct technique, recording results against specifications, and making go/no-go decisions about part acceptance.

Each step in this workflow has potential failure modes that the previous sessions have addressed. Drawing reading failures produce wrong nominal values (measuring the right feature against the wrong specification). Instrument selection failures produce insufficient precision (using a ruler to check a bearing bore). Technique failures produce inaccurate readings (angled jaw contact, dirty surfaces). Recording failures produce useless data (numbers without units, dimensions without identification). Go/no-go decision failures produce either false acceptance (shipping bad parts to assembly) or false rejection (scrapping good parts).

Experienced FRC engineers develop intuition about which failure mode is most likely in a given situation. In a noisy, chip-covered shop at 10 PM during build season, technique failures are most common — fatigue and distraction degrade measurement quality. In a classroom setting, drawing reading failures are common — students haven't yet internalized the standard drawing conventions. Understanding which failure mode is most likely helps you apply the right corrective discipline at the right moment.

#### Documentation and Communication
Measurement documentation has two audiences: the person doing the measurement (you, today) and the person reading the record later (your future self, a teammate, a mentor). Good documentation serves both audiences by being specific, complete, and unambiguous. A record that says "hole OK" helps nobody. A record that says "Hole 3 OD: nominal 0.201 in, measured 0.200 in, within tolerance ±0.005, PASS" provides everything needed to verify the part and to understand the process later.

Engineering communication is a professional skill that FRC teams develop by practicing it consistently, not by waiting until it seems important. Every measurement record, every manufacturing plan, and every quality checkpoint that you document this week is practice in the habit of writing things down clearly. Students who develop this habit become the engineers their teams rely on for quality-critical work; students who skip it become the ones whose parts have to be re-measured by someone else.

The inspection record you create in this session will serve as your reference for understanding what a complete measurement document looks like. Keep it as a template — modify it for future parts, adapt it for your team's specific needs, and update it as your measurement knowledge grows. The goal is not a perfect record on the first try but an improving record across the season.

#### Self-Assessment and Learning Direction
Every skill assessment reveals both strengths and areas for development. In measurement, common student strengths after four sessions include: understanding why precision matters, ability to use a digital caliper, and awareness of systematic versus random error. Common areas needing development include: measurement technique consistency (still getting variable readings on repeat measurements), drawing reading speed and confidence, and manufacturing plan completeness.

After this assessment, identify the one measurement skill you will focus on improving most in the next four weeks of build season. Maybe it's caliper technique — committing to measuring every part you cut three times and recording the results. Maybe it's drawing reading — volunteering to read the drawing before every fabrication task your team performs. Focused practice on one weakness produces more improvement than divided attention across all skills simultaneously.

The measurement skills from this week connect directly to Week 5 (fasteners) and Week 7 (robot assembly and testing). Fastener installation requires measuring torque, checking thread engagement depth, and verifying hole pattern alignment. Robot assembly requires confirming that every subassembly meets its dimensional requirements before integration. The investment in measurement fundamentals this week pays dividends every week through the rest of the season.

### Video Lessons

#### Quality Control in FRC Manufacturing (~10 min)
**Channel:** FRC team documentation / engineering review videos
**Search:** "FRC robot quality control manufacturing inspection" on YouTube
**What to watch for:** How experienced FRC engineers check their work. What specific measurements do they take at which points in the build? How do they document problems they find, and what do they do about parts that are out of spec?

---

#### Engineering Documentation Best Practices (~8 min)
**Channel:** Engineering communication / professional development channels
**Search:** "engineering documentation measurement record best practices" on YouTube
**What to watch for:** What makes a measurement record useful versus useless. Notice the specific format choices: columns, units, nominal/measured/tolerance, pass/fail. These habits separate professional-quality documentation from informal notes.

### Practice Exercises

#### Exercise 1: Complete Part Inspection (20 min)
**Objective:** Perform and document a full dimensional inspection of a fabricated part

Obtain a fabricated FRC part (bracket, tube, or plate) and its drawing or specification:

1. Create a complete inspection form with header: part name, drawing number, date, inspector name, lot size
2. List all measurable features from the drawing
3. For each feature, record: feature name, nominal dimension, tolerance, instrument used, measured value, pass/fail
4. Include at least 8 measured features covering multiple measurement modes (OD, ID, depth, step, position)
5. Write a summary: overall part disposition (Accept / Reject / Conditional Accept) with justification
6. If any feature is out of spec, note whether it is a critical or non-critical feature and what the consequence of using the part would be

**Success Criteria:** A professional-quality inspection record with complete header, 8+ features, correct pass/fail assessments, and a clearly justified final disposition.

---

#### Exercise 2: Manufacturing Plan for Next Batch (20 min)
**Objective:** Write a manufacturing plan incorporating lessons from the inspection

Based on the inspection results from Exercise 1 (or a provided inspection record if Exercise 1 found no issues):

1. Identify any out-of-spec features and trace their probable cause (operation, tool setup, measurement technique)
2. Write a manufacturing plan for producing 4 more of the same part
3. Include specific corrections to address any out-of-spec features found
4. Add quality checkpoints at the points where errors were previously found
5. Estimate batch fabrication time and identify the operation with the highest variability risk

**Success Criteria:** A manufacturing plan that incorporates lessons from the inspection — with specific checkpoints targeting the features previously found out of spec and corrective actions addressing probable root causes.

---

#### Exercise 3: Self-Assessment (15 min)
**Objective:** Honestly evaluate your measurement skills and identify development priorities

1. Rate your confidence in each skill from 1 (not confident) to 5 (highly confident):
   - Unit conversion (imperial/metric)
   - Caliper OD measurement technique
   - Caliper ID measurement technique
   - Reading a technical drawing for all dimensions and notes
   - Writing a manufacturing plan
   - Performing a quality checkpoint inspection

2. For your lowest-rated skill, write a specific 30-day practice plan: what will you do differently in sessions, in build, and in homework to improve that skill?

3. Write one example of how each measurement skill from this week will directly apply to your role in the FRC build season

**Success Criteria:** An honest self-assessment with numerical ratings and a specific, actionable 30-day practice plan for the identified weakest skill.

### Knowledge Check

**1.** What are the five steps of a complete measurement workflow for an FRC part?
- A) Design, sketch, model, print, inspect
- B) Read drawing, select instruments, execute measurements, record results, make go/no-go decision
- C) Cut, drill, tap, deburr, assemble
- D) Measure, estimate, fabricate, verify, document

**Answer:** B

**2.** What is a "false acceptance" in quality control?
- A) Accepting a drawing specification that is tighter than necessary
- B) Shipping an out-of-spec part to assembly because the measurement incorrectly showed it as passing
- C) Accepting a budget estimate that is too low
- D) Using a part from a different manufacturer than specified

**Answer:** B

**3.** Why should an inspection record say "Hole 3: measured 0.200 in, nominal 0.201 in, within +/-0.005, PASS" rather than just "hole OK"?
- A) Long records are required for FRC Engineering Notebook submissions
- B) The specific record provides enough information to verify the measurement and understand the process in the future
- C) Short records are only acceptable for non-critical features
- D) The format with numbers is required by manufacturing software

**Answer:** B

**4.** What does "part disposition" mean in quality control?
- A) The physical location where the part is stored after inspection
- B) The final decision about whether a part is accepted, rejected, or conditionally accepted for use
- C) The process of disassembling a failed part for root cause analysis
- D) The assignment of a part to a specific location on the robot

**Answer:** B

**5.** During a high-pressure build season late-night session, which measurement failure mode is most common?
- A) Drawing reading failures — students misread specifications
- B) Instrument selection failures — wrong tool chosen
- C) Technique failures — fatigue and distraction degrade measurement quality
- D) Recording failures — data is not written down at all

**Answer:** C

**6.** What does "root cause" mean when analyzing an out-of-spec measurement?
- A) The person responsible for the error
- B) The fundamental underlying reason a defect occurred, not just the symptom
- C) The earliest measurement in the fabrication sequence
- D) The measurement that first detected the problem

**Answer:** B

**7.** How does the measurement skills from this week directly connect to Week 5 (Fasteners)?
- A) Fastener thread dimensions must be measured with a caliper before installation
- B) Fastener installation requires measuring torque, verifying thread engagement, and checking hole pattern alignment
- C) Fasteners are always metric, so imperial measurement skills are not needed
- D) Measurement is not needed for fasteners; standard hardware is always in-spec

**Answer:** B

**8.** What does a "conditional accept" part disposition mean?
- A) The part meets all specifications and is accepted unconditionally
- B) The part fails all specifications and must be rejected
- C) The part has one or more out-of-spec features that are accepted for a specific use case with documented justification
- D) The part is accepted only if it passes a functional test on the robot

**Answer:** C

**9.** Why is focused practice on one weak skill more effective than divided attention across all skills?
- A) FRC rules limit the number of skills a student can develop
- B) The human brain consolidates skills most effectively when attention is concentrated on one area of weakness
- C) Focusing on one skill reduces measurement errors in all other skills simultaneously
- D) Divided attention works equally well but takes more calendar time

**Answer:** B

**10.** What is the primary purpose of measuring the first part in a batch before continuing with the rest?
- A) FRC fabrication rules require first-article certification
- B) The first part reveals systematic setup errors that would otherwise repeat identically across all remaining parts
- C) The first part is always the most accurate and sets the standard for the rest
- D) First-part measurement is a quality theater ritual, not a technically necessary step

**Answer:** B

### Reflections

1. Complete this sentence in at least 3 sentences: "Before this week, my approach to measurement in FRC was... After this week, I plan to change... because..."

2. You rated your measurement skills from 1 to 5. What would it take for every rating to become a 5? Is that a realistic goal for this build season, or are there skills where "good enough" is the right target? How do you decide?

3. You wrote an inspection record in Exercise 1. Show it to an experienced mentor and ask: "Is this the quality of documentation you would expect from a first-year student? What would you change?" Report their feedback and your reaction to it.

4. The final paragraph of Session 4.5 says: "The investment in measurement fundamentals this week pays dividends every week through the rest of the season." Give three specific examples — one from Week 5 (fasteners), one from Week 6 (mechanisms), and one from Week 7 (integration) — of exactly how a measurement skill from this week will apply.

### Summary

- **Complete measurement workflow:** The five-step process (read drawing, select instruments, execute measurements, record results, make go/no-go decision) that converts a part drawing into a verified, documented acceptance decision.
- **Part disposition:** The formal outcome of a quality inspection: Accept (all features in spec), Reject (critical feature out of spec), or Conditional Accept (minor out-of-spec feature accepted with documented justification).
- **False acceptance:** The quality failure of shipping an out-of-spec part to assembly because a measurement error or technique failure incorrectly showed it as passing.
- **Root cause analysis:** The investigation of why a defect occurred — identifying the fundamental process failure, not just the symptom — so the same error does not repeat.
- **Inspection record:** A structured documentation format that captures part identity, feature specifications, measured values, pass/fail results, and final disposition; the permanent quality record of a fabrication batch.

# WEEK 5: Fasteners & Assembly

**Duration:** 4-5 hours
**Learning Style:** Hands-On Lab + Technical Reference

Fasteners are the unsung heroes of FRC robots. Every mechanism on every robot is held together by screws, bolts, rivets, and nuts — and the quality of every one of those connections determines whether the robot survives a competition. A connection failure during a match is often more damaging than any mechanical design flaw: a gusset that pulls off, a motor that vibrates loose, a chain tensioner that backs out. This week teaches you the complete fastener system — types, sizes, selection criteria, installation technique, and quality control.

**Week 5 Learning Objectives:**
- Identify the major fastener types used in FRC (SHCS, BHCS, button head, rivet, rivet nut) and select the correct type for each application
- Convert between FRC fastener size specifications (10-32, 1/4-20, M3) and understand what each number means
- Install fasteners with correct torque, thread engagement, and locking technique
- Apply thread-locking compound and lock hardware correctly for FRC vibration environments
- Perform a fastener quality audit on a completed assembly

---

## Session 5.1: Fastener Types & Applications
**Duration:** 60 minutes | **Format:** Reference study with identification exercises

### Introduction

Walk through any FRC pit at a regional competition and you will see a dizzying variety of hardware: hex-drive socket head screws, flat head machine screws, blind rivets, rivet nuts, standoffs, and countless lock nuts. Every one of these fasteners exists because it solves a specific connection problem better than the alternatives. Knowing why each type exists — not just what it looks like, but what engineering problem it solves — is the foundation of assembling robots that stay together.

**What You Will Learn:**
- The names, visual identification, and drive types of the major FRC fastener categories
- The functional advantage that makes each fastener type the best choice for specific FRC applications
- How to read a fastener part number and understand every component of the specification

### Core Concepts

#### Machine Screws: SHCS, BHCS, Button Head, and Flat Head
The Socket Head Cap Screw (SHCS) is the workhorse of FRC assembly. Its cylindrical head with an internal hex (Allen) drive allows high tightening torque in a compact head profile. SHCS fasteners come in all standard FRC sizes (6-32, 8-32, 10-32, 1/4-20) and in metric (M3, M4, M5). The compact head is ideal in tight spaces, and the hex socket drive is more reliable than Phillips or slotted drives under high torque. Most FRC brackets, gearboxes, and structural connections use SHCS.

The Button Head Cap Screw (BHCS) has a low-profile dome-shaped head with an internal hex drive. The wider head diameter distributes load over a larger area, making it better for soft materials and applications where clamping over a large footprint matters. Button heads are common in FRC for attaching polycarbonate panels, securing sensors, and any application where the screw head protrudes into a space where it might snag on field elements or other robot parts.

Flat Head Machine Screws (also called countersunk screws) sit flush with or below the surface of the part when installed in a countersunk hole. They are used when protruding screw heads would cause interference or safety issues — a drive wheel sprocket with flat heads allows the chain to run without snagging on screw heads, and a bumper mounting bracket may use flat heads where they face the field perimeter. Flat heads require a countersunk hole drilled with a countersink tool, adding an operation to the fabrication sequence. The drive is typically hex (preferred) or Phillips (less preferred for high-torque FRC applications).

#### Rivets, Rivet Nuts, and Specialty Fasteners
Blind rivets (pop rivets) are one-sided fasteners installed from one side of the joint — "blind" because you cannot access the back side. A rivet gun pulls the rivet mandrel, deforming the rivet body to clamp the joint. Rivets are fast, light, and non-removable, making them ideal for permanent structural connections: tube gussets, bumper frames, and permanent structural joints. The standard FRC rivet sizes are 3/16-inch and 1/4-inch diameter in aluminum or steel. Aluminum rivets in aluminum structure is the most common FRC combination. Steel rivets into aluminum provide greater shear strength for high-load joints.

Rivet nuts (also called Riv-Nuts or nutserts) are expandable threaded inserts installed from one side with a rivet nut tool. Once installed, they provide a permanent threaded hole in a material that is too thin or too soft to hold threads directly — such as 0.065-inch wall aluminum tube or polycarbonate sheet. Rivet nuts allow machine screws to be installed and removed repeatedly at that location, unlike a rivet which is permanent. FRC teams use rivet nuts extensively for removable covers, sensor mounts, and any location that needs to be accessed for adjustment or replacement during the season.

Standoffs are hollow threaded cylinders that space two mounting surfaces apart at a fixed distance. They are used in FRC for PCB (electronics board) mounting, sensor positioning, and creating offset brackets. Standoffs screw into a threaded hole on one side and accept a fastener through the other, sandwiching the standoff between two surfaces. The thread specification (10-32 is most common in FRC) must match at both ends. Nylon standoffs are used for electronics to prevent short circuits; aluminum standoffs are used for structural applications.

#### Nuts, Lock Nuts, and Thread Locking
Standard hex nuts pair with machine screws and bolts for through-bolt connections. The nut provides the threaded partner when a tapped hole is not available or practical. In FRC, hex nuts are used for shaft collars, axle retention, and any through-bolt that cannot be accessed with a tap. However, plain hex nuts can vibrate loose in the high-vibration FRC environment — a robot crossing field obstacles, impacting other robots, and driving over carpet bumps subjects every fastener to constant vibration.

Nyloc nuts (nylon-insert lock nuts) contain a nylon collar at the top of the nut. When a screw is threaded through the nylon, it deforms slightly, creating interference that resists rotation. Nyloc nuts are the FRC standard for vibration-resistant fastening where a nut is accessible. They can be reused a limited number of times before the nylon loses effectiveness; a nyloc that spins on by hand (no resistance) should be replaced. Nyloc nuts add roughly 5 to 15 in-lb of prevailing torque, which is factored into tightening torque calculations.

Threadlocker adhesive (brand name Loctite, commonly called by the brand name generically) is applied to screw threads before installation and cures to fill the thread gaps, preventing rotation. Blue (Loctite 243) is medium-strength and removable with common tools — the standard choice for FRC. Red (Loctite 271) is high-strength and requires heat to remove — reserved for permanent joints or high-shock applications. Threadlocker is the preferred choice when a nut is inaccessible (the screw threads into a tapped hole) or when the fastener must not add any protrusion beyond the tapped hole depth. All FRC competition teams should apply threadlocker to any critical fastener that cannot use a Nyloc nut.

### Video Lessons

#### FRC Fastener Types Identification (~10 min)
**Channel:** FRC team training / hardware overview channels
**Search:** "FRC fastener types identification SHCS rivet nut robotics" on YouTube
**What to watch for:** How the presenter visually identifies each fastener type and describes what drive type it uses. Notice the head geometry comparison — why a SHCS head is better than a Phillips head for FRC torque requirements.

---

#### Why Fasteners Come Loose and How to Prevent It (~8 min)
**Channel:** Engineering / machining channels
**Search:** "why fasteners loosen vibration locking methods Loctite Nyloc" on YouTube
**What to watch for:** The mechanical explanation for why vibration loosens fasteners (thread helix angle and lateral vibration). This is the "why" behind Nyloc nuts and Loctite — once you understand the mechanism, you will never forget to use them on your robot.

### Practice Exercises

#### Exercise 1: Fastener Identification Drill (15 min)
**Objective:** Identify 10 fastener samples by type, drive, and size designation

Using a fastener assortment from your team's hardware inventory:

1. Lay out 10 different fasteners
2. For each, identify: fastener category (SHCS, BHCS, flat head, rivet, rivet nut, standoff, nyloc nut, hex nut, button head)
3. Identify the drive type (hex/Allen, Phillips, Torx, slotted)
4. Estimate the size (use a thread gauge or reference chart if available)
5. State one FRC application where each fastener would be the best choice

**Success Criteria:** At least 8 of 10 fasteners correctly identified by type and drive. All 10 FRC applications are realistic and specific.

---

#### Exercise 2: Fastener Selection Scenarios (20 min)
**Objective:** Select the correct fastener for 6 different FRC assembly scenarios

For each scenario below, select the most appropriate fastener type and size, and justify your choice:

1. Attaching a motor to a gearbox (the motor has 10-32 through-holes, the gearbox has tapped 10-32 holes)
2. Permanently connecting two 1-inch aluminum tubes at a corner with a gusset plate
3. Mounting a polycarbonate sensor shield to 0.065-inch wall aluminum tube
4. Attaching a chain sprocket to a shaft hub where the screw head cannot protrude above the sprocket face
5. Creating a removable access panel on the robot's electronics bay, attached to thin-wall tube
6. Spacing a gyroscope sensor 0.500 inch off the RoboRIO mounting surface

**Success Criteria:** All six selections are correct or defensible, with clear reasoning that references the functional advantage of the chosen fastener type in that specific application.

---

#### Exercise 3: Reading Fastener Specifications (15 min)
**Objective:** Parse fastener part numbers and specifications completely

Decode the following fastener part descriptions (identify every component of the specification):

1. "10-32 x 0.750 SHCS, 18-8 SS, Hex Drive"
2. "1/4-20 x 1.000 BHCS, 6061 Aluminum"
3. "M4 x 0.7 x 10mm SHCS, A2 Stainless"
4. "3/16-inch Aluminum Blind Rivet, 0.125 to 0.250 grip range"
5. "10-32 Rivet Nut, 0.118 to 0.196 grip range, Aluminum"
6. "6-32 x 0.500 Flat Head, Phillips Drive, 18-8 SS, Countersink angle 82 degrees"

**Success Criteria:** Every component of each specification correctly identified and explained (thread spec, length, material, drive type, and any additional attributes). You can explain why each attribute matters in an FRC application.

### Knowledge Check

**1.** What does "SHCS" stand for, and what is the drive type?
- A) Standard Head Cap Screw; Phillips drive
- B) Socket Head Cap Screw; internal hex (Allen) drive
- C) Stainless Hard Chrome Steel; wrench drive
- D) Short Head Cap Screw; Torx drive

**Answer:** B

**2.** What is the primary advantage of a flat head (countersunk) screw over a SHCS in FRC applications?
- A) Flat heads are stronger in shear
- B) The flat head sits flush with or below the surface, eliminating protrusion that could snag or interfere
- C) Flat heads can be tightened with more torque than SHCS
- D) Flat heads are lighter and reduce robot weight

**Answer:** B

**3.** What is a rivet nut, and what problem does it solve?
- A) A nut with a rivet pin; used for permanent non-removable joints
- B) A threaded insert installed from one side; provides reusable threads in thin-wall material that cannot be tapped
- C) A nut with a rubber insert for vibration resistance
- D) A rivet that contains a pre-installed nut for through-bolt connections

**Answer:** B

**4.** What is the difference between a Nyloc nut and a standard hex nut?
- A) Nyloc nuts are larger and require more torque to install
- B) Nyloc nuts contain a nylon collar that creates interference with the screw thread, resisting vibration loosening
- C) Nyloc nuts are used only for metric fasteners; hex nuts are for imperial
- D) Nyloc nuts are single-use and must be discarded after removal

**Answer:** B

**5.** When should you use blue Loctite (243) instead of a Nyloc nut?
- A) When the fastener is larger than 1/4-20
- B) When a nut is inaccessible (threading into a tapped blind hole) or when no protrusion beyond the thread is acceptable
- C) Blue Loctite is used only for temporary connections that will be removed in 24 hours
- D) Blue Loctite is always preferred over Nyloc nuts because it is lighter

**Answer:** B

**6.** What is the FRC standard fastener for most structural bracket and gearbox connections?
- A) 1/4-20 Flat Head with Phillips drive
- B) 10-32 Socket Head Cap Screw (SHCS) with hex drive
- C) 8-32 Button Head with Torx drive
- D) 1/4-28 Nyloc with open-end wrench

**Answer:** B

**7.** What does the "10-32" designation in a fastener specification mean?
- A) 10 mm diameter, 32 mm thread pitch
- B) Size #10 major diameter (0.190 inches), 32 threads per inch
- C) 10/32 inch diameter (5/16 inch)
- D) Grade 10 fastener, 32 ksi yield strength

**Answer:** B

**8.** A blind rivet is described as having a "0.125 to 0.250 grip range." What does this mean?
- A) The rivet can be used in holes between 0.125 and 0.250 inches in diameter
- B) The rivet correctly clamps material stacks between 0.125 and 0.250 inches in total thickness
- C) The rivet mandrel length is between 0.125 and 0.250 inches
- D) The rivet requires 0.125 to 0.250 inch clearance above the surface after installation

**Answer:** B

**9.** Which material combination for blind rivets provides the greatest shear strength in an aluminum structure?
- A) Plastic rivets in aluminum
- B) Aluminum rivets in aluminum
- C) Steel rivets in aluminum
- D) Copper rivets in aluminum

**Answer:** C

**10.** What is a standoff used for in FRC electronics mounting?
- A) To prevent the PCB from overheating by creating air gap
- B) To space a PCB or sensor a fixed distance off a mounting surface, with threads at both ends
- C) To stand the robot upright for weigh-in at competition
- D) To provide an electrical ground path between PCBs

**Answer:** B

### Reflections

1. Before this session, how did you select fasteners for your robot — by going to the hardware bin and grabbing whatever looked right? How does knowing the functional purpose of each fastener type change your selection process?

2. Vibration loosening is the most common fastener failure mode in FRC. What vibration sources exist on a competition FRC robot? Which mechanisms on your robot are subjected to the most severe vibration? What locking method would you use for each?

3. Rivet nuts solve the problem of threading into thin-wall material. But installing rivet nuts requires a specialized tool. Is the capability they provide worth the investment in the tool? What alternatives exist, and what are their disadvantages?

4. FRC teams often have a hardware "graveyard" — a bin of mixed, unlabeled fasteners. What would it take to organize that hardware correctly? What labeling system would you implement, and how would you prevent it from becoming a graveyard again next season?

### Summary

- **Socket Head Cap Screw (SHCS):** The standard FRC machine screw with a cylindrical head and internal hex drive; preferred for structural bracket, gearbox, and mechanism connections.
- **Blind rivet:** A one-sided fastener used for permanent structural joints; installed with a rivet gun and available in aluminum (lighter) and steel (stronger shear) versions.
- **Rivet nut:** A threaded insert installed from one side; provides reusable machine screw threads in thin-wall aluminum tube or sheet that cannot support direct tapping.
- **Nyloc nut:** A lock nut with a nylon insert that creates interference with the screw thread; the standard vibration-resistant nut for FRC joints where a nut is accessible.
- **Thread locker (Loctite):** An anaerobic adhesive applied to screw threads before installation; blue (removable) is the FRC standard for tapped-hole connections that cannot use a Nyloc nut.

---

## Session 5.2: Fastener Sizing Systems
**Duration:** 60 minutes | **Format:** Technical reference with sizing exercises

### Introduction

Specifying the right fastener means knowing not just the type but the exact size. Too small a fastener strips its threads or breaks in shear; too large a fastener requires a hole that weakens the part it's fastening. The FRC fastener sizing system — which combines thread diameter, thread pitch, and fastener length into a compact specification — follows standards that have been in use for over a century. Learning to read and specify fasteners correctly is the prerequisite to ordering parts, writing manufacturing plans, and communicating with your team about hardware needs.

**What You Will Learn:**
- How to read and specify imperial machine screw sizes (unified inch thread series)
- How to read and specify metric machine screw sizes (ISO metric thread series)
- How to select correct drill bit sizes for clearance holes and tap holes

### Core Concepts

#### Imperial Machine Screw Thread Specification
Imperial machine screws follow the Unified National Thread Standard (UNS). The size designation has two parts: the major diameter (expressed as a gauge number from #0 to #12 for small screws, or as a fractional inch for larger screws) and the threads-per-inch (TPI). UNC (Unified National Coarse) and UNF (Unified National Fine) designate the thread pitch standard — UNC has fewer, larger threads per inch and is more tolerant of manufacturing variation, making it the FRC standard. UNF has more threads per inch and is used for fine-adjustment applications.

The most common FRC fastener sizes and their diameters:
- 6-32: 0.138-inch major diameter, 32 TPI (used for light-duty sensor mounts, PCB standoffs)
- 8-32: 0.164-inch major diameter, 32 TPI (light structural, motor encoder retention)
- 10-32: 0.190-inch major diameter, 32 TPI (standard FRC structural fastener, the most-used size)
- 1/4-20: 0.250-inch major diameter, 20 TPI (heavy structural, bumper mounts, chain tensioners)
- 5/16-18: 0.3125-inch major diameter, 18 TPI (high-load axle bolts, bumper corner retention)

Fastener length for machine screws is measured from under the head (the bottom of the head, not the tip of the threads) to the tip for fully-threaded screws. The length must be chosen so that thread engagement equals at least 1.5 times the fastener diameter in aluminum: for a 10-32 (0.190-inch diameter), minimum thread engagement in aluminum is 0.285 inches, so a hole tapped 0.375 inches deep accepts a 0.375-inch length screw with adequate engagement.

#### Metric Machine Screw Thread Specification
ISO metric machine screws specify diameter (M number, in millimeters) and thread pitch (in millimeters). Common FRC metric sizes:
- M3 x 0.5: 3mm diameter, 0.5mm pitch (used for small sensors, encoder mounting, REV Robotics motor face patterns)
- M4 x 0.7: 4mm diameter, 0.7mm pitch (medium sensors, light mechanisms)
- M5 x 0.8: 5mm diameter, 0.8mm pitch (equivalent to approximately 10-32 in load capacity)
- M6 x 1.0: 6mm diameter, 1.0mm pitch (heavy structural, wheel hub bolts)

The "coarse" pitch (listed above) is the standard for structural FRC applications; fine pitches exist for each diameter but are rarely used in FRC. Metric fastener length is specified in millimeters and measured the same way as imperial (under the head to tip for full threads). The REV Robotics motor mounting pattern uses M3 and M4 fasteners, so FRC teams mixing REV products into American-standard designs need both metric and imperial hardware.

#### Drill Bit Sizes for Clearance and Tap Holes
Every fastener installation requires a hole in the material. The hole size depends on whether the fastener will thread into the material (tap hole — smaller diameter) or pass through freely (clearance hole — larger diameter). Using the wrong size hole produces either a loose, weak joint or a screw that won't start.

For 10-32 (the most common FRC fastener):
- Tap hole (drill before tapping): #21 drill bit (0.159-inch diameter)
- Clearance hole (free fit): #7 drill bit (0.201-inch diameter)
- Close clearance hole (snug fit): #10 drill bit (0.194-inch diameter)

For 1/4-20:
- Tap hole: #7 drill bit (0.201-inch diameter)
- Clearance hole: 17/64-inch drill bit (0.266-inch diameter)
- Close clearance hole: 1/4-inch drill bit (0.250-inch diameter)

A critical and easily confused fact: the 10-32 tap hole (#21, 0.159 inch) is a different size than the 10-32 clearance hole (#7, 0.201 inch). Accidentally using the clearance drill for a tapped hole (making it too large) produces a hole with no usable threads. Accidentally using the tap drill for a clearance hole (too small) means the screw won't fit. Posting a drill chart in your team's fabrication area and consulting it every time is the professional habit that prevents these errors.

### Video Lessons

#### Machine Screw Thread Specifications Explained (~10 min)
**Channel:** Engineering/machining educational channels
**Search:** "machine screw thread specification imperial metric how to read" on YouTube
**What to watch for:** How the instructor decodes a complete fastener specification including thread diameter, pitch, length, and material. Pay special attention to how they determine the correct drill size for a tap hole versus a clearance hole.

---

#### FRC Hardware Reference Guide (~8 min)
**Channel:** FRC team training or AndyMark/REV Robotics product channels
**Search:** "FRC hardware guide 10-32 1/4-20 fastener selection" on YouTube
**What to watch for:** How experienced FRC builders talk about fastener selection — which sizes they use for which applications, and why. Notice the drill chart they reference and whether it matches the specifications in this session.

### Practice Exercises

#### Exercise 1: Fastener Specification Decoding (15 min)
**Objective:** Parse and interpret 8 complete fastener specifications

Decode each specification below — identify every component and its meaning:

1. "10-32 UNC x 0.500 SHCS, A2-70 Stainless"
2. "M5 x 0.8 x 12mm BHCS, Grade 8.8"
3. "1/4-20 UNC x 0.750 Nyloc Nut, 18-8 SS"
4. "8-32 UNC x 0.375 Flat Head, Black Oxide Alloy"
5. "M3 x 0.5 x 6mm SHCS, A2 Stainless"

#### Exercise 2: Drill Bit Selection Chart (20 min)
**Objective:** Create a reference drill chart for the 6 most common FRC fastener sizes

Build a table with these columns: Fastener Size | Tap Drill | Tap Drill Decimal | Clearance Drill | Clearance Drill Decimal | Min Thread Engagement in Aluminum

Complete the table for: 6-32, 8-32, 10-32, 1/4-20, M3 x 0.5, M4 x 0.7

Use a machinist's drill chart reference (available in any machinist handbook or online) for the drill designations. Calculate minimum thread engagement as 1.5 x fastener major diameter.

#### Exercise 3: Thread Engagement Calculation (15 min)
**Objective:** Calculate adequate thread engagement depth for fastener selection in 3 scenarios

For each scenario, calculate: (a) minimum thread engagement depth, (b) minimum tapped hole depth, (c) appropriate fastener length, (d) verify the fastener length is commercially available:

1. 10-32 SHCS threading into 6061 aluminum (select the shortest fastener length that provides adequate engagement)
2. 1/4-20 bolt threading into a 1.000-inch thick aluminum plate (calculate if through-threading is needed)
3. M4 x 0.7 SHCS threading into a REV Robotics motor face (motor face is 8mm thick aluminum)

**Success Criteria:** All three calculations are correct, fastener lengths selected are commercially available standard lengths, and you can explain why the minimum engagement rule (1.5x diameter) exists.

### Knowledge Check

**1.** In the specification "10-32," what does the "32" represent?
- A) The grade strength of the fastener
- B) The number of threads per inch
- C) The fastener length in 32nds of an inch
- D) The Rockwell hardness of the material

**Answer:** B

**2.** What is the correct tap drill size for a 10-32 tapped hole?
- A) 10/32 inch (0.3125 inch)
- B) 0.190 inch (the major diameter)
- C) #21 drill (0.159 inch)
- D) #7 drill (0.201 inch)

**Answer:** C

**3.** What is the difference between UNC and UNF thread standards?
- A) UNC is used for inch fasteners; UNF is used for metric
- B) UNC has fewer, larger threads per inch (coarse); UNF has more, finer threads per inch
- C) UNC fasteners are for structural use; UNF fasteners are for decorative use
- D) There is no functional difference in FRC applications

**Answer:** B

**4.** What does "M4 x 0.7" specify?
- A) 4-inch major diameter with 0.7 threads per inch
- B) 4mm major diameter with 0.7mm thread pitch
- C) Metric grade 4, 0.7mm wall thickness
- D) 4mm length screw with 0.7mm head height

**Answer:** B

**5.** For a 1/4-20 screw threading into 6061 aluminum, what is the minimum thread engagement depth?
- A) 0.125 inch (half the diameter)
- B) 0.250 inch (one diameter)
- C) 0.375 inch (1.5 times the diameter)
- D) 0.500 inch (two times the diameter)

**Answer:** C

**6.** Why does accidentally using a clearance drill bit for a tapped hole ruin the hole?
- A) The hole is too small and the tap will break
- B) The hole is too large and the tap will cut inadequate threads with little material engagement
- C) The clearance drill cuts the wrong thread angle
- D) The hole must be exactly the same size as the fastener for tapping to work

**Answer:** B

**7.** What is the clearance hole size for a 10-32 SHCS?
- A) 0.159 inch (#21 drill)
- B) 0.190 inch (major diameter)
- C) 0.201 inch (#7 drill)
- D) 0.250 inch (1/4-inch drill)

**Answer:** C

**8.** REV Robotics NEO motors use which fastener standard for their face mounting pattern?
- A) 10-32 imperial
- B) 1/4-20 imperial
- C) M3 and M4 metric
- D) 8-32 imperial

**Answer:** C

**9.** Why is the "1.5 times diameter" minimum thread engagement rule applied to aluminum specifically?
- A) Aluminum is heavier, requiring more threads to support the weight
- B) Aluminum is softer than steel, so more thread contact area is needed to carry the same load without stripping
- C) FRC inspection rules require 1.5x engagement in all materials
- D) The 1.5x rule applies to all materials equally; aluminum is no different

**Answer:** B

**10.** How is fastener length measured for a standard SHCS?
- A) From the top of the head to the tip of the threads
- B) Total thread length only, not including the head
- C) From under the head (bottom of head) to the tip
- D) From the hex socket depth to the thread tip

**Answer:** C

### Reflections

1. You built a drill chart for six fastener sizes. Before this session, how did your team determine which drill bit to use for a tapped hole? Was the process reliable? How often did drilled holes need to be re-done because the wrong drill was used?

2. The 1.5x diameter minimum thread engagement rule exists because aluminum strips more easily than steel. Can you think of situations on an FRC robot where this rule might be violated and get away with it? Can you think of situations where even 1.5x engagement is not enough?

3. Metric and imperial fasteners coexist on FRC robots because American structural parts use imperial hardware but imported motors and sensors use metric. What organizational challenge does this create for your team's hardware inventory? How would you solve it?

4. Fastener length selection requires calculating thread engagement. This calculation takes about 30 seconds. Do you think FRC students routinely do this calculation when selecting fasteners, or do they guess? What would it take to make the 30-second calculation a default habit on your team?

### Summary

- **Unified National Thread (UNC):** The standard coarse-thread series for imperial machine screws, designated by gauge number (or fractional diameter) and threads per inch (e.g., 10-32 UNC).
- **ISO metric thread:** The international standard for metric machine screws, designated by diameter in millimeters and pitch in millimeters (e.g., M4 x 0.7).
- **Tap drill:** The drill bit size used to prepare a hole before tapping; smaller than the fastener major diameter to leave material for thread cutting.
- **Clearance drill:** The drill bit size used to make a hole that the fastener passes through freely; approximately equal to the fastener major diameter.
- **Minimum thread engagement:** The minimum depth of thread contact in a tapped hole required to develop full fastener strength; standard rule for aluminum is 1.5 times the fastener major diameter.

## Session 5.3: Professional Installation Techniques
**Duration:** 60 minutes | **Format:** Hands-on installation lab

### Introduction

Selecting the right fastener is half the job. Installing it correctly is the other half. A correctly selected fastener installed with the wrong torque, wrong technique, or without locking hardware will fail — the joint that looked perfect on paper becomes the loose bolt that costs you a match. This session is entirely practical: you will install fasteners with correct technique, apply torque specifications, use thread locking hardware, and develop the tactile feedback that tells an experienced mechanic when a fastener is properly tightened.

**What You Will Learn:**
- How to install machine screws with correct starting technique to prevent cross-threading
- How to apply correct tightening torque using a torque wrench or by calibrated feel
- How to install Nyloc nuts, rivet nuts, and blind rivets correctly

### Core Concepts

#### Preventing Cross-Threading and Starting Correctly
Cross-threading is the catastrophic failure mode where a screw is driven into a tapped hole at an angle, cutting new off-angle threads that destroy the hole. A cross-threaded hole in aluminum is difficult to repair — the hole must be oversized and re-tapped to the next larger size, or a thread insert (HeliCoil) installed. Preventing cross-threading takes five seconds and costs nothing; repairing a cross-threaded hole in a critical location takes fifteen minutes and may require re-fabricating the part.

The correct starting technique is: before applying any torque, spin the screw backward (counterclockwise) by hand until you feel a slight click or drop — this is the screw finding the thread start and dropping into the first thread groove. Then apply forward (clockwise) rotation. Starting the screw backward first is universally practiced by professional mechanics but almost unknown among new FRC students. If you apply forward rotation immediately with a hex driver, you are relying on luck to find the thread start instead of using the correct technique.

Thread fit also matters. A screw that starts too easily (spins in with almost no hand resistance) is probably in a worn or oversized hole — stop and assess before tightening. A screw that requires more than light finger pressure to start is probably cross-threaded — stop, back out completely, check the angle, and restart. The feel of a correctly started screw in a correctly sized hole is distinctive: light resistance that builds gradually as the thread engages. Developing this tactile recognition is a core mechanic skill.

#### Tightening Torque
Torque is the rotational force applied to tighten a fastener. Insufficient torque leaves a joint that can shift under load and vibrate loose. Excessive torque strips threads, breaks the fastener, or deforms the clamped material. For each standard FRC fastener, there is a recommended tightening torque range appropriate for aluminum-to-aluminum joints.

Approximate torque targets for common FRC fasteners (dry, unlubricated threads in aluminum):
- 6-32: 4-6 in-lb
- 8-32: 8-12 in-lb
- 10-32: 15-20 in-lb
- 1/4-20: 35-45 in-lb
- M3 x 0.5: 4-6 in-lb
- M4 x 0.7: 10-14 in-lb
- M5 x 0.8: 20-28 in-lb

A torque wrench (or torque-limiting hex driver) is the precise way to apply these values. However, most FRC fabrication happens without a torque wrench on every hex key. Developing calibrated feel — a mental benchmark for what "proper 10-32 torque in aluminum" feels like — is a skill that takes practice but is achievable. The benchmark exercise: tighten a 10-32 screw into a scrap aluminum block until it just strips (you will feel the threads give way and the screw spin without resistance). The correct tightening torque is approximately 60-70% of the torque that stripped it.

Fasteners that thread through aluminum into other materials (e.g., a 10-32 threading into a steel nut plate) can accept higher torque because the steel does not strip as easily. Fasteners threading into polycarbonate or nylon require much lower torque — these soft materials strip at a fraction of the torque aluminum can accept. Always consider the weakest material in the threaded joint when selecting tightening torque.

#### Rivet and Rivet Nut Installation
Blind rivets are installed with a rivet gun (pop rivet tool). The rivet is inserted into the hole (both clamped pieces must be aligned), the mandrel is gripped in the rivet gun jaws, and the handle is squeezed to pull the mandrel. The mandrel's head deforms the rivet body on the back side, clamping the joint. The mandrel snaps off when the correct clamp load is reached — the snap signals a properly set rivet. A rivet that pulls through without snapping (the mandrel pulls all the way out without breaking) indicates the grip range was exceeded or the hole is oversized.

Rivet nut installation requires a rivet nut tool (also called a nutsert tool or Riv-Nut tool). The rivet nut is threaded onto the tool's mandrel, inserted into the hole, and the tool handle is squeezed to pull the mandrel backward. This compresses the rivet nut's body behind the material, expanding it to create a mechanical lock. The installed rivet nut should be flush with or slightly below the surface and should not rotate when a screw is threaded into it. A rivet nut that spins when a screw is installed was not properly set — it must be drilled out and replaced with the next larger size.

Rivet spacing and edge distance rules prevent the material around a rivet from tearing. Minimum edge distance (from rivet center to the nearest edge of the material) should be at least 2.5 times the rivet diameter. Minimum rivet spacing (center-to-center for multiple rivets) should be at least 3 times the rivet diameter. These rules are drawn from aircraft and structural engineering standards and apply equally to FRC robot structure. A row of rivets too close to the edge of an aluminum gusset will tear out under shock loads — exactly the failure mode seen when robots collide on the field.

### Video Lessons

#### Proper Hex Key Fastener Installation Technique (~8 min)
**Channel:** Manufacturing/machining skill channels
**Search:** "how to install machine screw correctly cross threading prevention" on YouTube
**What to watch for:** The backward-start technique. Watch whether the instructor does this — many experienced mechanics do it automatically and don't explain it. If the video doesn't show it, try to observe it in the shop with an experienced mentor.

---

#### Pop Rivet and Rivet Nut Installation (~10 min)
**Channel:** Workshop/fabrication skill channels
**Search:** "pop rivet installation technique rivet nut how to FRC" on YouTube
**What to watch for:** The sound and feel of a correctly set rivet (mandrel snap) versus an incorrectly set rivet (mandrel pulls through). For rivet nuts, watch for the rotation test after installation.

### Practice Exercises

#### Exercise 1: Fastener Starting Technique (15 min)
**Objective:** Practice the correct backward-start technique on 20 fastener installations

Using scrap aluminum with pre-drilled and tapped 10-32 holes:

1. Install 10 screws using the backward-start technique (spin CCW until click, then CW to tighten)
2. Install 10 screws using random direct CW start (to experience the contrast)
3. For each group, count: how many required correction because they started at an angle?
4. Note the tactile difference between a correctly started screw and one that immediately starts at an angle
5. Write a description of the tactile signal that tells you a screw is correctly started

**Success Criteria:** Measurably fewer corrections needed in the backward-start group versus the direct-start group. A written description of the tactile starting signal that a mentor confirms is accurate.

---

#### Exercise 2: Torque Calibration (20 min)
**Objective:** Develop calibrated feel for correct 10-32 and 1/4-20 tightening torque

1. Using a torque wrench (or torque screwdriver), tighten 5 screws of each size to the target torque (10-32: 18 in-lb, 1/4-20: 40 in-lb)
2. After each tightened fastener, loosen it and re-tighten using feel only — no torque wrench
3. Measure the actual torque of your "by feel" tightening using the torque wrench
4. Record the error: how many in-lb over or under the target?
5. Repeat until your by-feel tightening is consistently within +/-5 in-lb of target for 10-32

**Success Criteria:** After 10+ repetitions, your by-feel 10-32 torque is within +/-5 in-lb of the 18 in-lb target in at least 4 of 5 consecutive attempts.

---

#### Exercise 3: Rivet and Rivet Nut Installation (15 min)
**Objective:** Install 4 blind rivets and 2 rivet nuts correctly, verifying each installation

1. Clamp two pieces of scrap aluminum (total stack approximately 0.130 inch) and drill a 3/16-inch hole through both
2. Install a 3/16-inch aluminum blind rivet — listen for and feel the mandrel snap
3. Check: does the rivet head sit flush? Does the set end look correctly deformed?
4. Drill a 10-32 rivet nut hole in a single piece of 0.065-inch wall aluminum tube
5. Install a 10-32 rivet nut using the rivet nut tool
6. Thread a 10-32 screw into the installed rivet nut — verify it does not rotate (correctly set) or spin (incorrectly set)
7. Repeat both exercises and compare your second attempt to the first

**Success Criteria:** All rivets show correct mandrel snap and flush installation. Both rivet nuts pass the rotation test (screw can be fully installed without the rivet nut spinning).

### Knowledge Check

**1.** What is cross-threading, and what causes it?
- A) Using too long a screw; the threads extend past the material
- B) Installing a screw at an angle, cutting off-angle threads that destroy the tapped hole
- C) Using a metric screw in an imperial tapped hole
- D) Over-tightening until threads strip

**Answer:** B

**2.** What is the correct first step when starting a machine screw into a tapped hole to prevent cross-threading?
- A) Apply maximum initial torque to force the screw into alignment
- B) Spin the screw counterclockwise until you feel a click (the thread start), then turn clockwise
- C) Pre-lubricate the threads with oil before inserting
- D) Tap the screw with a hammer to seat it before turning

**Answer:** B

**3.** What is the approximate correct tightening torque for a 10-32 SHCS in aluminum?
- A) 4-6 in-lb
- B) 15-20 in-lb
- C) 35-45 in-lb
- D) 60-80 in-lb

**Answer:** B

**4.** What indicates that a blind rivet has been correctly installed?
- A) The rivet head is above the surface by 0.010 to 0.020 inches
- B) The mandrel snaps off during installation at the correct clamp load
- C) The rivet can be removed with a standard drill bit
- D) The joint can be disassembled by pulling the rivet straight out

**Answer:** B

**5.** What should you do if a rivet nut spins when you thread a screw into it after installation?
- A) Apply Loctite to the threads and re-tighten
- B) The rivet nut was not properly set — drill it out and replace with the next larger size
- C) Use a locking washer under the screw head to prevent movement
- D) Spinning is normal until the screw is fully tightened

**Answer:** B

**6.** What is the minimum edge distance for a rivet, as a multiple of the rivet diameter?
- A) 1.0x diameter
- B) 1.5x diameter
- C) 2.5x diameter
- D) 4.0x diameter

**Answer:** C

**7.** Why does over-tightening a machine screw in aluminum cause more damage than in steel?
- A) Aluminum expands more under heat generated by tightening
- B) Aluminum is softer and strips thread engagement more easily at lower torque values
- C) Over-tightening damages the hex socket, not the threads
- D) Aluminum fasteners are always weaker than steel fasteners of the same size

**Answer:** B

**8.** What does "grip range" mean for a blind rivet?
- A) The diameter of the rivet hole
- B) The total material stack thickness the rivet is designed to correctly clamp
- C) The force required to pull the mandrel
- D) The range of hole diameters the rivet can be used in

**Answer:** B

**9.** If you are tightening a machine screw into polycarbonate rather than aluminum, how does this affect your target torque?
- A) No change — torque targets apply to the fastener material, not the clamped material
- B) Use higher torque because polycarbonate is more brittle and requires more preload
- C) Use lower torque because polycarbonate strips at a much lower torque than aluminum
- D) Polycarbonate requires finger-tightening only; no torque specification applies

**Answer:** C

**10.** What is the tactile signal that a machine screw has been correctly started in a tapped hole (not cross-threaded)?
- A) The screw spins freely with no resistance until fully threaded
- B) Resistance builds gradually from a clean thread engagement start; the feel is smooth and consistent
- C) The screw requires significant initial force to start, then spins freely
- D) A distinct audible click signals correct thread engagement

**Answer:** B

### Reflections

1. You practiced the backward-start technique and measured fewer cross-threading incidents. Why does this technique work? At a mechanical level, what does spinning counterclockwise until the click accomplish?

2. Calibrated torque feel takes practice to develop. Think about other skills in your life where you developed calibrated physical feel (throwing a ball a specific distance, typing at a specific pressure, playing a musical instrument). What was the learning process that built that calibration?

3. The rivet spacing and edge distance rules come from aircraft engineering standards. Should FRC teams follow aircraft standards for their robots? What are the arguments for and against applying these stricter standards in the FRC context?

4. You installed rivet nuts in thin-wall aluminum tube. Think about all the locations on a typical FRC robot where rivet nuts would be the best fastening solution. Are there locations where rivet nuts might be worse than an alternative? What are those locations and why?

### Summary

- **Cross-threading:** The installation of a fastener at an angle, cutting off-angle threads that destroy the tapped hole; prevented by the counterclockwise backward-start technique.
- **Tightening torque:** The rotational force applied to set a fastener to its design preload; too low allows joint loosening, too high strips threads or breaks the fastener.
- **Rivet gun (pop rivet tool):** The tool that installs blind rivets by pulling the mandrel to set the rivet clamp load; the mandrel snap indicates correct rivet installation.
- **Edge distance:** The minimum distance from a rivet center to the nearest edge of the material; must be at least 2.5 times the rivet diameter to prevent edge tearout.
- **Calibrated feel:** The trained tactile sense that tells an experienced mechanic when a fastener is at the correct torque without using a torque wrench; developed through deliberate practice with measurement feedback.

## Session 5.4: Advanced Fastening Concepts
**Duration:** 60 minutes | **Format:** Technical instruction with design application

### Introduction

Beyond correct installation technique, experienced FRC engineers understand the deeper principles that govern why fastened joints succeed or fail. Joint loading — the direction and magnitude of force on a fastened connection — determines which failure mode is most likely and how to design the joint to resist it. This session covers shear versus tension loading, the role of friction in a clamped joint, and how to use fastener patterns strategically to carry the loads your robot generates.

**What You Will Learn:**
- The difference between shear-loaded and tension-loaded fastened joints
- How preload and friction carry most of the load in a well-designed fastened joint
- How to design fastener patterns for specific loading conditions in FRC mechanisms

### Core Concepts

#### Shear vs. Tension Loading
Every force applied to a fastened joint is resolved into two components: shear (force perpendicular to the fastener axis, trying to slide the joint sideways) and tension (force along the fastener axis, trying to pull the joint apart). Most FRC joints experience both, but one component usually dominates.

A gusset plate connecting two tubes at a corner primarily carries shear: the weight of the arm or mechanism above is trying to slide the tubes past each other. The rivets or screws in that gusset are loaded in shear. A motor mount plate primarily carries shear from motor torque reaction: the motor is trying to rotate its mount as it reacts against load. A bumper mounting bracket primarily carries tension and shear: the bumper is being pushed by a robot collision, transmitting both a direct push (tension in some fasteners) and a bending moment (tension/compression in pairs of fasteners) into the chassis.

Fasteners are much stronger in shear than in tension for the same material and diameter — a rough rule of thumb is that a fastener's shear strength is approximately 60% of its tensile strength. However, the friction preload from a properly tightened fastener is what actually carries most shear loads in well-designed FRC joints. When a fastener is tightened to its design torque, it stretches slightly and clamps the joint faces together with a significant normal force. The friction from that normal force can carry shear loads many times the fastener's direct shear strength. This is why proper tightening torque is critical: an under-tightened fastener has insufficient preload, and the shear load is transferred directly into the fastener body rather than the friction interface.

#### Fastener Patterns and Load Distribution
A single fastener at the center of a joint creates a pivot point — apply a moment and the joint rotates around that fastener. A pattern of fasteners distributes the moment across multiple fasteners, with fasteners further from the neutral axis carrying more load. This is why structural joints in FRC always use two or more fasteners, and why they should be spaced as far apart as practical within the joint footprint.

For a joint carrying a pure moment (bending load), the two outermost fasteners carry the most load. Placing additional fasteners between them contributes less than you might expect — the inner fasteners carry very little moment load because they are close to the neutral axis. However, even lightly loaded inner fasteners provide significant value: they maintain clamp pressure over the joint area, preventing the joint faces from separating under dynamic loading. A two-fastener joint can rock about the axis between the two screws; a four-fastener joint is fully constrained against rocking.

FRC joint design guidelines for common situations: tube-to-tube gusset connections should use at least 3 fasteners per tube leg (6 total for an L-corner), spaced at a 1.000-inch pitch. Motor mounts should use all available mounting holes on the motor face — do not use two of four holes to save time, because the motor will vibrate loose much faster. Bumper mounting should use through-bolts with backup plates (not sheet metal screws into thin wall) because bumper loads are the highest impact loads the robot experiences. These guidelines are distilled from thousands of robot failure analyses across hundreds of FRC seasons.

#### Special Fastening Challenges in FRC
Thermal expansion can cause problems in joints between dissimilar materials. Aluminum and steel have different thermal expansion coefficients — a joint tightened at room temperature (70 degrees F) will develop different preload at competition temperature (potentially 40 degrees F in an outdoor venue or 90 degrees F in a warm gymnasium). For most FRC joints, this variation is small compared to other uncertainties, but for precision mechanisms where alignment must remain constant across temperature changes, using the same material for fastener and clamped part minimizes thermal effects.

Vibration fatigue is the failure of a fastener that loosens slowly through repeated micro-movements at the thread contact. Every time the robot hits a bump, crosses a field element, or collides with another robot, the fastener vibrates. Over hundreds or thousands of cycles, the micro-movements can cause thread wear (loosening the joint gradually) or metal fatigue (fracturing the fastener). Using Nyloc nuts and Loctite addresses the loosening mechanism; selecting larger fasteners or reducing the vibration amplitude at the joint addresses the fatigue mechanism. Critical fasteners (bumper mounts, gearbox mounts, drivetrain bearing retention) should be re-checked after every match or after any significant robot impact.

Fastener accessibility must be considered at design time, not at assembly time. A perfectly selected fastener installed in a perfectly designed joint is useless if you cannot reach it with a hex key during a pit repair. Robot assemblies that require removing three other mechanisms to access a fastener create multi-step repair sequences that cannot be completed in the 30-minute match interval. FRC design rule: every fastener used for a component that might need field replacement must be accessible with a standard L-key or T-handle hex wrench, with the robot in its normal folded configuration.

### Video Lessons

#### Fastener Loading: Shear vs. Tension (~10 min)
**Channel:** Mechanical engineering / fastener engineering channels
**Search:** "fastener joint shear tension loading preload clamp force" on YouTube
**What to watch for:** The preload and friction explanation — how tightening a bolt stretches it and creates clamping force, and why that clamping force is the actual load carrier in most shear joints. This concept is counterintuitive for beginners and changes how you think about tightening torque.

---

#### FRC Structural Joint Design Best Practices (~10 min)
**Channel:** FRC team design/build season education channels
**Search:** "FRC structural joints gusset plate fastener pattern best practices" on YouTube
**What to watch for:** The specific fastener count and spacing recommendations for FRC tube-to-tube joints. Does the presenter's guidance match the guidelines in this session? What additional guidelines do they add?

### Practice Exercises

#### Exercise 1: Joint Loading Analysis (15 min)
**Objective:** Classify the dominant loading type for 6 FRC joint scenarios

For each joint, classify the dominant loading as shear, tension, or combined, and explain:

1. A motor mount plate bolted to an aluminum gearbox
2. A bumper mount bolt attaching the bumper frame to the robot chassis
3. A chain tensioner bolt maintaining chain tension in a drive gearbox
4. Rivet nuts in a polycarbonate electronics panel under normal driving forces
5. A gusset plate at the corner of a chassis frame
6. The shoulder bolt retaining a wheel on an axle shaft

**Success Criteria:** All six loading classifications are defensible with clear physical reasoning. You can explain which fasteners in each joint are most loaded and most at risk of failure.

---

#### Exercise 2: Fastener Pattern Design (20 min)
**Objective:** Design a fastener pattern for a specific FRC structural joint

Design the fastener pattern for an L-bracket connecting a vertical tube to a horizontal chassis rail. The mechanism above the vertical tube weighs 3 lb and has a 6-inch moment arm. Requirements: minimum safety factor of 3x, fasteners must be 10-32 SHCS, bracket is 0.125-inch 6061-T6 aluminum.

1. Draw the joint schematic showing force direction and magnitude
2. Calculate the moment at the joint (force x moment arm)
3. Design a fastener pattern with appropriate number and spacing to carry the moment
4. Identify the most loaded fastener in the pattern
5. Verify the shear load on the most loaded fastener is within the fastener's allowable shear capacity (look up 10-32 SHCS shear strength)

**Success Criteria:** A documented joint design with force analysis, fastener pattern sketch, and a verified safety factor calculation. The design is achievable within standard FRC gusset plate geometry.

---

#### Exercise 3: Accessibility Audit (15 min)
**Objective:** Evaluate fastener accessibility on an actual or rendered FRC assembly

Using either your team's current robot, a previous year's robot, or a CAD model:

1. Identify 5 fasteners that are critical (motor mounts, bumper mounts, bearing retention)
2. Evaluate each for pit accessibility: can it be reached with a standard hex key in 30 seconds or less?
3. Rate each fastener 1-5 for accessibility (1=extremely difficult, 5=trivially accessible)
4. For any fastener rated 1 or 2, propose a design modification that would improve accessibility
5. Estimate the time cost at competition for a fastener rated 1 vs. rated 5 during a 30-minute match interval repair

**Success Criteria:** An accessibility audit table with 5 critical fasteners evaluated and improvement proposals for all low-rated fasteners.

### Knowledge Check

**1.** What is the difference between shear loading and tension loading on a fastener?
- A) Shear loads twist the fastener; tension loads pull it axially
- B) Shear loads act perpendicular to the fastener axis; tension loads act along the fastener axis
- C) Shear loads are dynamic; tension loads are static
- D) There is no practical difference in FRC applications

**Answer:** B

**2.** In a well-designed bolted joint, what primarily carries the shear load?
- A) The fastener body directly resisting the shear force
- B) The friction generated by the clamping preload between joint faces
- C) The nut or threaded insert resisting lateral movement
- D) The fastener head bearing area

**Answer:** B

**3.** Why do fasteners in a joint pattern placed further from the neutral axis carry more load?
- A) They are longer and therefore have more material to resist the load
- B) They experience greater displacement in a moment-loaded joint, developing greater reactive force
- C) They are more accessible and therefore tightened to higher torque
- D) Distance from the neutral axis has no effect on load distribution

**Answer:** B

**4.** What is the recommended minimum number of fasteners per tube leg for a tube-to-tube gusset connection in FRC?
- A) 1 fastener
- B) 2 fasteners
- C) 3 fasteners
- D) 5 fasteners

**Answer:** C

**5.** Why should all available motor face holes be used when mounting a motor, not just two of four?
- A) FRC rules require all motor mounting holes to be used
- B) Using all holes distributes the motor reaction torque over more fasteners, reducing vibration loosening
- C) Using only two holes is acceptable if Loctite is applied to those two
- D) Motor mounting holes are threaded by the motor manufacturer and cannot be left empty

**Answer:** B

**6.** What causes vibration fatigue in a fastener?
- A) Thermal cycling between hot and cold environments
- B) Repeated micro-movements at thread contact over many vibration cycles, causing thread wear or material fracture
- C) Corrosion of the thread surface due to humidity
- D) Repeated tightening and loosening during maintenance

**Answer:** B

**7.** Why is fastener accessibility important to consider during design, not during assembly?
- A) Accessibility is a safety requirement in FRC inspection
- B) Inaccessible fasteners cannot be repaired in the 30-minute match interval, preventing field repairs of critical components
- C) Inaccessible fasteners cannot be properly torqued during assembly
- D) The CAD model cannot be completed until all fasteners are accessible

**Answer:** B

**8.** How does preload affect the shear capacity of a bolted joint?
- A) Preload has no effect on shear capacity
- B) Higher preload creates more clamping force and therefore more friction, greatly increasing shear capacity
- C) Higher preload reduces shear capacity by pre-stressing the fastener body
- D) Preload only affects tensile capacity, not shear

**Answer:** B

**9.** What is the best fastener type for a bumper mounting application in FRC?
- A) Self-tapping screws into thin-wall tube
- B) Through-bolts with backup plates, using Nyloc nuts
- C) Blind rivets for quick removal between matches
- D) M6 metric bolts to match the frame rail extrusion standard

**Answer:** B

**10.** Which critical FRC fasteners should be re-inspected after every match or significant impact?
- A) Only electronics mounting fasteners, because electronics are most sensitive to vibration
- B) Bumper mounts, gearbox mounts, and drivetrain bearing retention fasteners
- C) Only fasteners that use Loctite, because Loctite can fail from impact
- D) Re-inspection is only needed after visible damage, not as a regular practice

**Answer:** B

### Reflections

1. The preload-friction concept explains why tightening torque matters more than most students realize. Before this session, did you think of tightening torque as primarily preventing the fastener from loosening, or as creating the actual load-carrying mechanism? How does this change how you will communicate the importance of correct torque to teammates?

2. Fastener accessibility was identified as a design consideration. Think about a robot repair situation you have witnessed or experienced at competition. What made that repair take longer than it should have? Was fastener accessibility part of the problem?

3. The session says to "use all available motor face holes." Many students skip this because it takes longer. Write an argument you would make to a resistant teammate to convince them to always use all motor mounting holes. Include a specific failure scenario.

4. You analyzed six joint loading scenarios. Of the six, which would you consider most safety-critical on an FRC robot, and why? What would the consequence of a fastener failure be in each case — lost match, robot damage, safety hazard, or combination?

### Summary

- **Shear loading:** Force acting perpendicular to a fastener's axis, trying to slide the joint faces past each other; carried primarily by friction from fastener preload in well-designed joints.
- **Tension loading:** Force acting along a fastener's axis, trying to pull the joint apart; carried directly by the fastener's tensile strength and thread engagement.
- **Preload:** The clamp force and fastener stretch created by tightening to the correct torque; the mechanism that generates friction to carry shear loads and prevents joint loosening.
- **Fastener pattern:** The arrangement of multiple fasteners in a joint; spacing fasteners further apart increases their effectiveness in resisting moments, while more fasteners constrain all modes of joint separation.
- **Vibration fatigue:** The gradual failure of a fastener connection through repeated micro-movements at thread contact under cyclic loading; prevented by Nyloc nuts, Loctite, and correct preload.

---

## Session 5.5: Quality Control & Assembly Assessment
**Duration:** 60 minutes | **Format:** Practical quality audit with documentation

### Introduction

A robot built with the right fasteners, correctly installed, and properly locked, is a robot that will stay together through a competition.
