But "correctly installed" is not a subjective feeling — it is a verifiable state that can be checked. This final session in the fastener week applies quality control discipline to real robot assembly: you will perform a systematic fastener audit, document what you find, and implement improvements. The skills practiced here directly replicate the pre-competition inspection process used by top-tier FRC teams.

**What You Will Learn:**
- How to perform a systematic fastener quality audit on a completed assembly
- How to document fastener discrepancies and prioritize corrective actions
- How to establish a pre-competition fastener inspection protocol for your team

### Core Concepts

#### What a Fastener Quality Audit Checks
A fastener quality audit systematically verifies four conditions for every fastener on the robot: presence (is the fastener actually there?), security (is it tightened to appropriate torque?), locking (does it have a Nyloc nut, Loctite, or other vibration-resistant feature?), and accessibility (can it be checked and replaced in the field?). Missing any of these four checks produces a fastener that will fail at the worst possible moment — during a critical match.

The presence check sounds trivial, but missing fasteners are surprisingly common on FRC robots. In the rush of final assembly, holes get skipped, fasteners get cross-threaded and removed but not replaced, and access limitations prevent fasteners from being installed. A robot that looks assembled may have empty holes that compromise joint strength. The audit procedure is: look at every hole that should have a fastener, confirm the fastener is present, and check whether the head shows signs of proper installation (no tool slippage marks from over-torquing, head sitting flush).

The security check (is it tight?) can be done with a torque wrench for critical fasteners (bumper mounts, gearbox mounts, drivetrain bearings) and with a calibrated by-feel check for standard structural fasteners. The feel test is: apply a firm tightening attempt with the hex key — if the fastener moves at all, it was loose and needs to be assessed for damage before re-tightening. If no movement is felt, the fastener passes the security check. Apply the Loctite or Nyloc check: if the fastener is in a tapped blind hole and has no Nyloc nut, is there Loctite on the threads? Check by looking for dried blue liquid on threads visible at the surface, or by applying a small amount of fresh Loctite before re-tightening if in doubt.

#### Audit Documentation and Priority System
A fastener audit produces findings — fasteners that failed one or more of the four checks. Findings need to be documented and prioritized for corrective action. The priority system should reflect the consequence of failure: a missing bumper mount fastener is a P1 (fix immediately, do not run the robot without it); a loose sensor mount screw is P3 (fix before the next match but not a safety issue); a slightly under-torqued gusset screw with Loctite is P2 (fix today but not in the next 30 minutes).

The corrective action for each finding depends on its nature. A loose fastener is re-torqued, checked for thread damage, and either accepted or replaced. A missing fastener is either installed immediately (if hardware is available) or the root cause investigated (why is it missing — was it never installed, or did it fall out?). A fastener with no locking feature has Loctite applied or a Nyloc nut added. A fastener with stripped threads requires the hole to be repaired (HeliCoil insert or next-size re-tap) or the part replaced.

Tracking audit findings over multiple events reveals patterns. If the same gusset joint consistently produces loose fasteners after every match, the root cause is not the assembly — it's the design. The joint is experiencing more vibration or load than expected, and the fastener selection or pattern needs to be revised. Teams that audit consistently build this feedback loop and continuously improve their robot's reliability throughout the season.

#### Pre-Competition Inspection Protocol
Elite FRC teams have a pre-competition inspection protocol: a checklist of fastener checks completed before each competition match starts, not just before the season. The protocol is shorter than a full audit — it focuses on the highest-risk fasteners that are most likely to have moved since the last check. The protocol takes 5-10 minutes and is performed by a dedicated mechanical student (not the driver or coach, who have other pre-match responsibilities).

A minimal FRC pre-competition fastener protocol includes:
1. Bumper mounting bolts — torque check on all 8+ fasteners
2. Drivetrain gearbox mounting bolts — torque check on all motor and gearbox mounts
3. Wheel retention hardware (cotter pins, shaft collars, snap rings) — visual confirmation of presence
4. All game mechanism pivot fasteners — check for looseness and Nyloc/Loctite presence
5. Battery mounting hardware — confirm battery strap and bracket fasteners are secure

This protocol is written on a laminated card kept in the pit, updated at the start of each season to reflect the current robot's architecture. The discipline of consistently following the protocol — even when tired, even when behind schedule, even when confident "it should be fine" — is what separates teams that lose matches to mechanical failures from teams that don't.

### Video Lessons

#### Pre-Match Robot Inspection by Top FRC Teams (~8 min)
**Channel:** FRC competition footage / behind-the-scenes pit videos
**Search:** "FRC pit pre-match robot inspection protocol mechanical" on YouTube
**What to watch for:** What specific checks the pit crew performs, in what order, and how quickly. Do they follow a written checklist? Who performs the check — the same person every time, or whoever is available? What happens when they find a problem?

---

#### FRC Robot Reliability: Lessons from Mechanical Failures (~10 min)
**Channel:** FRC post-season analysis / team retrospective videos
**Search:** "FRC robot mechanical failure analysis fastener reliability" on YouTube
**What to watch for:** Real stories of fastener failures that lost matches or damaged robots. What was the root cause — wrong fastener, incorrect installation, missing locking, or design flaw? Could a proper audit protocol have caught it?

### Practice Exercises

#### Exercise 1: Full Fastener Audit (20 min)
**Objective:** Perform and document a complete fastener audit on an FRC assembly

Using your team's current robot, a previous-year robot, or a provided assembly:

1. Create an audit form with columns: Location, Fastener Type, Present (Y/N), Secure (Y/N), Locked (Y/N), Accessible (Y/N), Notes
2. Systematically audit every fastener you can access on the assembly
3. Mark each of the four conditions for each fastener
4. Count findings by category: missing, loose, unlocked, inaccessible
5. Identify the 3 highest-priority findings and describe the corrective action for each

**Success Criteria:** A complete audit form with every accessible fastener evaluated, findings counted by category, and top-3 priorities identified with specific corrective actions.

---

#### Exercise 2: Corrective Action Execution (20 min)
**Objective:** Implement corrective actions for findings from the audit

Using the findings from Exercise 1 (or a provided list of intentional "planted" problems):

1. Re-tighten all loose fasteners to correct torque (document before-and-after)
2. Add Loctite to all tapped-hole fasteners that lacked locking (verify correct Loctite type)
3. Replace all missing fasteners with correctly specified hardware
4. For any inaccessible fastener that prevents repair, document the design modification that would improve accessibility
5. Re-audit the previously failing fasteners to confirm all have been corrected

**Success Criteria:** All audit findings corrected, re-audit shows zero remaining findings, and all design modifications are documented with specific proposed changes.

---

#### Exercise 3: Write a Pre-Competition Protocol (15 min)
**Objective:** Create a team pre-competition fastener inspection protocol

1. Identify the 10-15 most critical fastener locations on your team's robot (or a standard FRC robot)
2. For each location, specify: fastener type and size, locking method, check procedure (feel, visual, torque), acceptable condition
3. Format as a checklist that can be laminated and used in the pit by any student
4. Include a header with robot name, season year, and "last updated" date
5. Have a mentor review the protocol and provide feedback on completeness and any missing critical fasteners

**Success Criteria:** A printed or digital laminated-ready pre-competition fastener checklist with 10-15 items, verified as complete by a mentor, formatted clearly enough for any team member to follow.

### Knowledge Check

**1.** What four conditions does a fastener quality audit check for every fastener?
- A) Size, material, grade, and installation date
- B) Presence, security, locking, and accessibility
- C) Torque, thread engagement, Loctite color, and head type
- D) Position, alignment, tightness, and visual condition

**Answer:** B

**2.** In a fastener audit priority system, what would make a finding "P1" (fix immediately)?
- A) Any fastener that is slightly under-torqued
- B) Any fastener with no Loctite applied
- C) A fastener whose failure would create a safety hazard or prevent the robot from running
- D) Any fastener missing from the robot

**Answer:** C

**3.** What is the corrective action for a fastener found in a tapped hole with no Nyloc nut and no Loctite?
- A) Replace with a longer fastener
- B) Apply blue Loctite (medium strength) and re-tighten to correct torque
- C) Add a flat washer under the head
- D) The fastener is acceptable if it is currently tight

**Answer:** B

**4.** Why do elite FRC teams perform a pre-competition fastener check before every match, not just before the season?
- A) FRC rules require a mechanical inspection before each match
- B) Fasteners can loosen or fall out during matches and transport, making pre-match checks necessary for reliability
- C) Pre-match checks are required for the Mechanical Excellence award
- D) It is a tradition, not a technical requirement

**Answer:** B

**5.** What does it mean if the same joint consistently shows loose fasteners after every match across multiple events?
- A) The team's assembly technicians are not tightening correctly
- B) The joint design is experiencing higher load or vibration than expected, requiring a design change
- C) Blue Loctite is not strong enough; switch to red Loctite
- D) The fasteners are the wrong grade; switch to higher-grade hardware

**Answer:** B

**6.** What is a HeliCoil insert, and when is it used?
- A) A coil spring used in bump-stop mechanisms
- B) A wire thread insert used to repair a stripped or oversized tapped hole
- C) A rivet nut alternative for thick-wall material
- D) A electrical cable retention device

**Answer:** B

**7.** What is the advantage of assigning fastener auditing to a dedicated mechanical student, separate from the driver and coach?
- A) It reduces the driver's workload so they can focus on game strategy
- B) A dedicated auditor gives full attention to the check rather than splitting focus with other pre-match responsibilities
- C) FRC rules require a specific "Mechanical Inspector" role on the drive team
- D) The driver and coach cannot access the robot during pre-match inspection

**Answer:** B

**8.** What does "tracking audit findings over multiple events" enable a team to do?
- A) Compare their robot reliability to other teams at the same events
- B) Identify recurring problem joints whose design needs to be revised, not just repaired
- C) Qualify for the Quality Award at regional competitions
- D) Generate data for the Engineering Notebook

**Answer:** B

**9.** A fastener with stripped threads is found during an audit. What is the correct repair process?
- A) Apply extra Loctite and re-tighten with higher torque
- B) Drill out and install a HeliCoil thread insert, or re-tap to the next larger standard size
- C) Leave it and monitor at the next competition
- D) Replace with a longer fastener of the same diameter to engage undamaged threads deeper

**Answer:** B

**10.** What information should be on the header of a laminated pre-competition fastener protocol?
- A) The team captain's name and contact information
- B) Robot name, season year, and last updated date so the document is traceable and current
- C) A complete bill of materials for all fasteners on the robot
- D) The FRC team's event schedule for the season

**Answer:** B

### Reflections

1. You performed a fastener audit and found findings. How many findings did you expect to find before starting, and how many did you actually find? What does the gap between expectation and reality tell you about how well your team's assembly process works?

2. The priority system (P1/P2/P3) for audit findings requires judgment about consequence of failure. Describe a situation where two students might legitimately disagree about whether a finding is P1 or P2. How would you resolve that disagreement?

3. Writing the pre-competition protocol required you to identify the most critical fasteners on the robot. Did that exercise reveal any fasteners that you had previously not thought about as critical? What made you re-classify them?

4. Elite teams perform pre-match fastener checks even when tired and behind schedule. The human tendency is to skip the check when confident "everything is fine." Describe a system or commitment device that would help your team maintain this discipline even under pressure.

### Summary

- **Fastener quality audit:** A systematic check of every fastener in an assembly against four criteria (present, secure, locked, accessible), producing a prioritized list of findings for corrective action.
- **Audit priority system:** A classification of findings by consequence of failure (P1=fix immediately/safety, P2=fix today, P3=fix before next match), enabling teams to allocate limited pit time effectively.
- **Pre-competition inspection protocol:** A written checklist of the most critical fastener checks performed before each competition match; the discipline of following it consistently is the primary driver of robot reliability.
- **HeliCoil insert:** A wire thread insert installed in a stripped or oversized tapped hole to restore correct thread geometry; the standard repair for damaged tapped holes in aluminum structure.
- **Root cause analysis (fastener context):** Investigation of why a fastener repeatedly fails — distinguishing between assembly errors (correctable by technique) and design problems (requiring engineering changes).

# WEEK 6: Mechanisms & Actuators

**Duration:** 4-5 hours
**Learning Style:** Design + Build + Test Cycle

Mechanisms are what make an FRC robot competitive. The drivetrain gets you to the game piece; the mechanisms score it. This week moves from structural knowledge into functional design: how to grab, move, and release game pieces, how to use pneumatics and servos as actuators, and how to integrate multiple subsystems into a coherent robot. Every session this week connects directly to the decisions your team will make in the first week of build season.

**Week 6 Learning Objectives:**
- Identify the design requirements for a game piece manipulation mechanism based on game analysis
- Design, build, and test a simple intake mechanism using rollers or compliant wheels
- Understand and connect a basic pneumatic circuit (PCM/PH, solenoid, cylinder)
- Select and configure a servo for a precision positioning application
- Integrate two or more mechanisms into a coordinated assembly and verify function

---

## Session 6.1: Game Piece Manipulation Strategies
**Duration:** 60 minutes | **Format:** Design analysis + conceptual workshop

### Introduction

Before your team builds a single mechanism, you need to understand the game piece. What shape is it? How heavy? How does it need to move — from the field to the robot, from the robot to the scoring location, and sometimes back to the robot? What orientation does the scoring location require? The answers to these questions constrain your mechanism design more than any engineering preference. Great FRC mechanisms start with great game analysis.

**What You Will Learn:**
- How to extract mechanism design requirements from FRC game analysis
- The major categories of game piece manipulation (intake, indexing, outtake/scoring)
- How to evaluate mechanism concepts against design requirements systematically

### Core Concepts

#### Game Analysis: From Rules to Requirements
FRC game analysis starts by reading the game manual with an engineer's eye, not a gamer's eye. You are not trying to figure out the best game strategy — you are trying to identify the physical constraints on your mechanism design. Key questions from the game manual: What are the game piece dimensions and mass? What orientations can it arrive in (floor, human player station, etc.)? What are the scoring location dimensions and required game piece orientation? What is the cycle time target (how many seconds per scoring cycle is needed to achieve a competitive score)?

Converting game observations into engineering requirements follows a specific discipline. A requirement is not "we need a fast intake" — it is "intake must acquire a game piece from the floor within 0.5 seconds of contact, from any orientation within +/-30 degrees of nominal, while the robot is moving at up to 4 ft/s." That specificity is what allows you to test whether your mechanism meets the requirement. Vague requirements produce mechanisms that "kind of work" and are difficult to debug because you cannot define what "working" looks like.

The FIRST Tech Challenge (FTC) and FRC game archives are a valuable resource for mechanism design education. Every game since 2001 has been played by thousands of teams developing novel mechanism approaches. Studying historical game piece manipulation solutions — not to copy them, but to understand the design reasoning behind different approaches — builds the vocabulary of mechanism concepts that you can apply to new games. The FRC blog, YouTube, and The Blue Alliance are all archives of this knowledge.

#### Intake Mechanism Categories
Ground intakes acquire game pieces from the floor without stopping the robot. They require the robot to drive over, through, or alongside the game piece and capture it in a single continuous motion. Ground intakes are typically roller-based (spinning rollers pull the game piece in), compliant-wheel-based (flexible rubber wheels conform to irregular shapes), or brush-based (flexible bristles sweep pieces). The design challenge is width (how much floor coverage needed?), speed (fast enough to collect while driving?), and robustness (what happens when you miss or hit another robot?).

Human player station intakes acquire game pieces fed by a human player from a designated field position. These game pieces arrive at a consistent location and orientation, which is a significant design advantage — you don't need to handle unknown orientations. Human player station intakes can be more precise and reliable than ground intakes, but they limit your team's autonomy strategy (you must visit the human player station to reload).

Passive intakes use the game piece's own momentum and geometry to capture it without active power — a funnel that guides pieces to a holding position, for example. Active intakes use powered rollers, belts, or arms to actively pull and retain game pieces. Most competitive FRC designs use active intakes because they work reliably at higher robot speeds and with more game piece orientation variation than passive intakes.

#### Mechanism Concept Evaluation
After generating multiple mechanism concepts for a game piece manipulation function, you need to evaluate them systematically to select the best approach. The evaluation framework has two parts: feasibility (can we actually build this in 6 weeks with our team's skills and resources?) and capability (if we build it perfectly, does it meet all our requirements?).

A concept matrix (also called a Pugh matrix or decision matrix) is a structured tool for comparing concepts against requirements. List the requirements as rows and the concepts as columns. For each cell, score how well the concept meets that requirement (0 = does not meet, 1 = partially meets, 2 = fully meets). The concept with the highest total score is the strongest candidate — but the matrix also reveals which requirements are hardest to meet and which concepts have fatal weaknesses.

The most important outcome of concept evaluation is not "which concept wins" but "which requirements are in tension." If every concept fails on the same requirement (e.g., all concepts fail on cycle time), the team needs to revisit the requirement (is it actually achievable in this game?) or develop a new concept category that addresses it. Requirements that cannot be met with any concept at adequate feasibility should be discussed with the drive team to ensure the scoring strategy is realistic.

### Video Lessons

#### FRC Game Analysis Workshop (~12 min)
**Channel:** FRC strategy and design channels
**Search:** "FRC game analysis mechanism design requirements" on YouTube
**What to watch for:** How the presenter converts game observations into specific, testable engineering requirements. Notice whether they distinguish between requirements (what the mechanism must do) and solutions (how it will do it). The separation of "what" from "how" is the most important design thinking habit.

---

#### FRC Intake Mechanism Design Overview (~15 min)
**Channel:** FRC team design channels / mechanical build season content
**Search:** "FRC intake mechanism design types rollers compliant wheels" on YouTube
**What to watch for:** The variety of intake approaches used for different game piece shapes. Notice how the game piece geometry drives the mechanism type choice. Pay attention to how teams handle the transition from intake to indexing to scoring.

### Practice Exercises

#### Exercise 1: Game Analysis (15 min)
**Objective:** Extract mechanism requirements from the current or most recent FRC game manual

Using the current year's FRC game manual (or the most recent available at firstinspires.org):

1. Identify the game piece(s) and record their dimensions, mass, and shape
2. Identify all acquisition methods (floor, human player station, dispenser, etc.)
3. Identify all scoring locations and the required game piece orientation at each
4. Estimate the cycle time needed to score competitively (read the scoring matrix and estimate matches-per-game performance)
5. Write 3-5 specific, testable mechanism requirements for the primary scoring mechanism

**Success Criteria:** Requirements are specific (include numerical targets where possible), testable (a clear pass/fail criterion exists), and cover all major mechanism functions (acquire, transport, score).

---

#### Exercise 2: Concept Generation (20 min)
**Objective:** Generate 3 distinct mechanism concepts for a specific game piece manipulation function

Choose one mechanism function from the game analysis (intake, indexer, shooter, elevator, etc.):

1. Generate 3 distinct concepts — each must use a different operating principle (e.g., roller intake, claw intake, suction intake)
2. Sketch each concept (top view and side view, roughly to scale)
3. For each concept, list 3 potential advantages and 3 potential risks
4. Identify which concept you believe is strongest and write a one-paragraph justification

**Success Criteria:** Three genuinely distinct concepts (not just variations of the same principle), each with a sketch and advantage/risk analysis. Your top choice is justified against the requirements, not just personal preference.

---

#### Exercise 3: Concept Evaluation Matrix (15 min)
**Objective:** Apply a decision matrix to select the strongest mechanism concept

1. List the 5 most important requirements from Exercise 1 as matrix rows
2. List the 3 concepts from Exercise 2 as matrix columns
3. Score each concept against each requirement (0=fails, 1=partial, 2=fully meets)
4. Calculate total score for each concept
5. Identify any requirement where all concepts score 0 or 1 — this is a design challenge that needs additional creativity

**Success Criteria:** A completed decision matrix with justified scoring. The winning concept is the highest total score, but you also clearly identify the hardest requirements that every concept struggles to meet.

### Knowledge Check

**1.** What is the most important first step in FRC mechanism design?
- A) Selecting the motor and gearbox before designing the mechanism
- B) Extracting specific, testable requirements from game analysis before generating concepts
- C) Building a prototype as quickly as possible to learn what works
- D) Copying the mechanism approach used by the winning alliance from the previous season

**Answer:** B

**2.** What is the difference between an engineering requirement and a solution?
- A) Requirements are written by engineers; solutions are written by students
- B) A requirement defines what the mechanism must do; a solution defines how it will do it
- C) Requirements are for hardware; solutions are for software
- D) There is no meaningful difference in FRC design

**Answer:** B

**3.** What is a ground intake, and what is its main design challenge?
- A) An intake that collects game pieces from the ground without stopping, requiring sufficient width, speed, and robustness
- B) An intake mounted at ground level for stability during high-speed driving
- C) A passive intake that uses gravity to collect falling game pieces
- D) An intake built from ground-level materials (wood, carpet, foam) for weight savings

**Answer:** A

**4.** What is the purpose of a concept matrix (decision matrix) in mechanism design?
- A) To document which team member is responsible for each concept
- B) To systematically compare concepts against requirements and reveal fatal weaknesses
- C) To estimate the weight and cost of each concept
- D) To assign timeline milestones for each concept development

**Answer:** B

**5.** Why is studying historical FRC mechanism designs from past seasons valuable?
- A) Teams are allowed to reuse mechanisms from previous seasons without changes
- B) Historical designs build the vocabulary of mechanism concepts that can be adapted to new games
- C) Past winning mechanisms are always optimal for future games
- D) FRC rules require all teams to submit historical mechanism analysis in the Engineering Notebook

**Answer:** B

**6.** What is the primary advantage of a human player station intake over a ground intake?
- A) Human player station intakes are always faster
- B) Game pieces arrive at a consistent location and orientation, enabling more precise and reliable mechanism design
- C) Human player station intakes are simpler to build and lighter
- D) FRC rules give bonus points for human player station acquisition

**Answer:** B

**7.** What does a "cycle time" requirement specify?
- A) The time between robot battery charges
- B) The time required to complete one acquire-transport-score sequence, which determines competitive scoring rate
- C) The frequency at which the mechanism must be inspected
- D) The number of times a mechanism can operate in one match

**Answer:** B

**8.** What is a compliant wheel intake, and what game piece property does it exploit?
- A) An intake with wheels that can be adjusted for different game piece sizes
- B) An intake with flexible rubber wheels that deform around irregular game piece surfaces, gripping them securely
- C) An intake that complies with FRC rules by avoiding contact with protected field zones
- D) A passive intake that uses compliance (springiness) to absorb game piece impacts

**Answer:** B

**9.** If a decision matrix reveals that all three concepts score 0 on the same requirement, what should the design team do?
- A) Accept that the requirement cannot be met and remove it from the matrix
- B) Re-examine the requirement and develop new concept categories that might address it; also verify whether the requirement is achievable in this game
- C) Choose the concept with the highest score on other requirements and accept the weakness
- D) Ask the drive team to change the game strategy to avoid that requirement

**Answer:** B

**10.** What makes a mechanism requirement "testable"?
- A) It can be measured or demonstrated with a clear pass/fail criterion
- B) It was written by someone with testing experience
- C) It references a specific FRC rule number
- D) It can be implemented in fewer than 5 build days

**Answer:** A

### Reflections

1. You extracted requirements from a real FRC game. Which requirement was hardest to make specific and testable? What information from the game manual was missing or ambiguous that made precision difficult?

2. During concept generation, you were required to produce 3 genuinely different concepts. What mental barriers did you encounter when trying to think of truly different approaches? How did you overcome those barriers?

3. The decision matrix identifies the hardest requirements to meet. In real FRC design, teams sometimes change their scoring strategy when they discover that certain requirements cannot be met reliably. Have you ever witnessed your team making this kind of strategic adjustment? How was the decision made?

4. This session establishes that mechanism design starts with requirements, not solutions. But experienced FRC mentors sometimes jump directly to "let's build rollers" before writing requirements. What are the risks of that approach? When might experience make it acceptable?

### Summary

- **Game analysis:** The systematic extraction of mechanism design requirements from the FRC game manual; converts game rules into specific, testable engineering constraints.
- **Engineering requirement:** A specific, testable statement of what a mechanism must do (e.g., "intake must acquire in 0.5 sec from any orientation within +/-30 degrees"); the "what" before the "how."
- **Ground intake:** A powered mechanism that acquires game pieces from the floor while the robot is moving; typically uses rollers, compliant wheels, or brushes.
- **Concept matrix:** A structured evaluation tool that scores mechanism concepts against requirements to identify the strongest approach and reveal fatal weaknesses.
- **Cycle time:** The time required to complete one full acquire-transport-score sequence; determines the team's competitive scoring rate and is a critical mechanism design requirement.

---

## Session 6.2: Intake Mechanism Design & Build
**Duration:** 60 minutes | **Format:** Design-build-test workshop

### Introduction

This session is where requirements become hardware. You will design and build a simple roller intake using REV Robotics components (or equivalent), test it against your requirements from Session 6.1, and iterate based on what you observe. The entire design-build-test cycle will happen within 60 minutes — a compressed version of the process your team runs over six weeks during build season. Speed of iteration is a competitive advantage; this session builds the habits that make rapid iteration possible.

**What You Will Learn:**
- How to translate intake design requirements into a physical mechanism layout
- How to build a basic roller intake using REV Robotics tube, dead axles, and compliant wheels
- How to test a mechanism against requirements and identify failure modes

### Core Concepts

#### Roller Intake Geometry
A roller intake works by spinning one or more rollers (or compliant wheels) in the direction that pulls the game piece into the robot. The key geometric parameters are: roller height (the gap between the roller surface and the floor must be sized for the game piece), roller speed (determined by motor speed and roller diameter — higher surface speed means faster intake), roller width (must be wide enough to capture game pieces reliably at the expected approach accuracy), and intake angle (angled rollers or funnel geometry guides off-center game pieces toward center).

The relationship between roller diameter and surface speed is direct: surface speed = roller radius x angular velocity (in rad/s). A 2-inch diameter roller spinning at 600 RPM has a surface speed of 2 * pi * 1 inch * (600/60 rev/sec) = approximately 62.8 inches/second = approximately 5.2 ft/s. This surface speed must be fast enough to pull the game piece from the floor despite friction and inertia. As a rule of thumb, roller surface speed should be at least twice the robot's maximum driving speed for reliable intake during driving.

Compliance (the flexibility of the intake elements) is critical for handling game piece variation. Hard rollers work only for perfectly sized game pieces; compliant wheels (REV Robotics compliant wheels, or "squish wheels") deform around game pieces, accepting dimensional variation, orientation errors, and surface irregularity. FRC teams have moved strongly toward compliant-wheel-based intakes over the past decade because they are more forgiving of game piece inconsistency and floor surface variation than hard rollers.

#### REV Robotics Intake Components
REV Robotics (revrobotics.com) manufactures a system of components specifically designed for FRC mechanism building. The MAX Tube system uses 1-inch square aluminum extrusion with a standard 0.500-inch hole pattern. The Through Bore Encoder, SPARK MAX motor controllers, NEO Brushless motors, and the associated gearbox lineup (MAX Planetary) are the most common FRC drivetrain and mechanism powertrain components. Understanding how these components connect to each other is essential for designing intake mechanisms.

For a basic roller intake, the typical component set is: one NEO 550 motor (small and light) running through a MAX Planetary gearbox at a 3:1 or 5:1 reduction, driving a hex shaft, on which multiple REV compliant wheels or roller wheels are mounted. The intake width is set by the shaft length; multiple wheels are spaced across the shaft. The shaft is supported at both ends by 0.500-inch hex bore bearings pressed into tube end caps or side plates. The entire intake module is then mounted to the robot's front structure.

The REV Robotics and AndyMark product catalogs contain standard sizes and specifications for every component. Before designing a mechanism, download and review the engineering drawings for the motors, gearboxes, and structural components you plan to use — these drawings show the shaft output diameter, bolt circle dimensions, and component envelope that your mechanism must accommodate. Designing without component specifications leads to interference problems discovered at the worst time: during assembly.

#### Testing and Iterating Intake Mechanisms
Testing a mechanism means operating it against real game pieces and measuring whether it meets the requirements. For an intake, the test protocol might be: place the game piece at 5 different angular orientations (0, +15, +30, -15, -30 degrees from nominal), run the intake at robot speed, and record success or failure for each orientation at each of 3 robot speeds (slow, medium, fast). This gives you 15 data points per test session — enough to characterize the mechanism's performance reliably.

Failure mode analysis during testing means identifying not just that the mechanism failed but why. Did the game piece bounce off the rollers? The rollers are running at insufficient surface speed or the wrong direction relative to the approach angle. Did the game piece get grabbed but not fully indexed? The transition from intake rollers to the indexing system has a gap or misalignment. Did the mechanism work at low speed but fail at high speed? The intake geometry needs a wider funnel angle to capture the game piece earlier in the approach.

Each iteration of the mechanism should be motivated by a specific hypothesis: "The game piece bounces off because roller speed is too low, so I will increase motor speed from 600 to 900 RPM and re-test." This hypothesis-driven iteration is the same principle introduced in the rapid prototyping sessions earlier in the curriculum. Random changes without hypotheses produce data that is hard to interpret; hypothesis-driven changes produce understanding that accumulates into reliable mechanism design.

### Video Lessons

#### Building a Basic FRC Roller Intake (~15 min)
**Channel:** REV Robotics / FRC team build channels
**Search:** "FRC intake build tutorial REV compliant wheels roller" on YouTube
**What to watch for:** The specific component selection and assembly sequence for a roller intake. Notice the bearing placement, shaft retention method, and how the motor/gearbox is mounted relative to the intake shaft. Pay attention to how compliance wheels are spaced for different game piece shapes.

---

#### FRC Mechanism Testing Protocol (~10 min)
**Channel:** FRC design/build education channels
**Search:** "FRC mechanism testing iteration protocol data collection" on YouTube
**What to watch for:** How the team structures their testing — do they test systematically (all orientations, multiple speeds) or just "try it out"? Notice what data they collect and how they use it to make design decisions.

### Practice Exercises

#### Exercise 1: Intake Layout Design (15 min)
**Objective:** Create a scaled layout drawing of the intake mechanism before building

1. Based on the game piece dimensions from Session 6.1, determine the required roller gap (typically game piece height +/- 20%)
2. Calculate roller surface speed target (2x robot max speed, approximately 10 ft/s for most FRC intakes)
3. Select motor, gearbox ratio, and roller diameter combination that achieves the target surface speed
4. Sketch the intake layout: roller position relative to robot front, motor placement, bearing locations, and frame mounting points
5. Check your layout against the robot's maximum extension rules for the current game

**Success Criteria:** A scaled sketch with annotated dimensions showing roller position, motor/gearbox, bearings, and frame interface. Calculated surface speed is within 20% of target.

---

#### Exercise 2: Intake Build (20 min)
**Objective:** Build a basic roller intake from available REV Robotics or equivalent components

Using available materials (REV tube, hex shaft, compliant wheels, motor, and gearbox):

1. Cut tube stock to the intake width from your layout design
2. Press or install bearings into bearing holders at each end of the intake shaft
3. Mount compliant wheels on the shaft at appropriate spacing
4. Install the motor and gearbox, connecting the output shaft to the intake shaft
5. Mount the intake assembly to a test stand or robot structure at the designed height
6. Verify by hand-rotation that the mechanism moves freely without binding

**Success Criteria:** A functional intake assembly that rotates freely by hand, with all fasteners properly installed (using Nyloc nuts or Loctite as appropriate). The motor can be powered to spin the intake in the correct direction.

---

#### Exercise 3: Intake Testing and Iteration (15 min)
**Objective:** Test the intake against requirements and perform at least one iteration

1. Power the intake and test game piece acquisition from 5 orientations (0, +/-15, +/-30 degrees)
2. Record pass/fail for each orientation at low, medium, and high motor speed
3. Identify the most common failure mode from your test data
4. Form a hypothesis about the root cause of the failure
5. Make one design change based on your hypothesis (adjust roller height, change spacing, add a second roller, etc.)
6. Re-test and compare results to your pre-change baseline

**Success Criteria:** A test record with 15 data points, an identified primary failure mode with hypothesized root cause, a documented design change, and re-test results that confirm or refute the hypothesis.

### Knowledge Check

**1.** What is the formula for roller surface speed?
- A) Surface speed = roller diameter x motor torque
- B) Surface speed = roller radius x angular velocity (in rad/s)
- C) Surface speed = roller width x motor current
- D) Surface speed = gear ratio x motor no-load RPM

**Answer:** B

**2.** What is the recommended minimum roller surface speed relative to robot driving speed?
- A) Equal to robot max speed
- B) At least twice the robot's maximum driving speed
- C) Three times the robot's maximum driving speed
- D) Surface speed should be as slow as possible to avoid damaging game pieces

**Answer:** B

**3.** Why are compliant (flexible) wheels preferred over hard rollers in modern FRC intakes?
- A) Compliant wheels are lighter
- B) Compliant wheels deform around game pieces, accepting dimensional variation and orientation errors
- C) Compliant wheels can be replaced without tools during competition
- D) Compliant wheels spin faster than hard rollers at the same motor speed

**Answer:** B

**4.** What company manufactures the MAX Tube system, NEO motors, and MAX Planetary gearboxes used in many FRC mechanisms?
- A) AndyMark
- B) VEXpro
- C) REV Robotics
- D) WCP (West Coast Products)

**Answer:** C

**5.** Why should you download component engineering drawings before designing a mechanism?
- A) FRC rules require engineering drawings to be submitted before building
- B) Engineering drawings show shaft output diameter, bolt circles, and component envelopes needed to avoid interference during design
- C) Engineering drawings include the component's maximum allowable torque
- D) FRC inspection requires components to match their engineering drawings exactly

**Answer:** B

**6.** What does "hypothesis-driven iteration" mean in mechanism testing?
- A) Testing without recording data and making changes based on intuition
- B) Making a specific prediction about why the mechanism failed and what change will fix it, then testing to confirm or refute
- C) Using computer simulation to predict mechanism behavior before building
- D) Testing hypothetical game scenarios that haven't been officially announced

**Answer:** B

**7.** What should you do when a mechanism fails during testing?
- A) Rebuild from scratch — the design concept was wrong
- B) Identify the specific failure mode and form a hypothesis about its root cause before making any changes
- C) Ask an experienced mentor what to change
- D) Add more motors to increase power and see if the failure goes away

**Answer:** B

**8.** What is the purpose of testing an intake at multiple game piece orientations (+/-15, +/-30 degrees)?
- A) FRC rules require mechanisms to be tested at all legal game piece orientations
- B) Different orientations reveal the mechanism's robustness to the real-world variation in game piece approach angles during competition
- C) Testing multiple orientations is required before the mechanism can be added to the CAD model
- D) Game pieces always arrive at the same orientation, so multi-orientation testing is only for documentation

**Answer:** B

**9.** What does "funnel geometry" in an intake mechanism accomplish?
- A) Channels excess game pieces away from the robot to prevent overcrowding
- B) Guides off-center game pieces toward the center of the intake, increasing the effective capture width
- C) Creates a vacuum effect that pulls game pieces in from a distance
- D) Ensures game pieces are oriented correctly before entering the indexing system

**Answer:** B

**10.** What is the first step in converting intake design requirements into a physical mechanism?
- A) Select the motor first, because motor selection drives all other design decisions
- B) Create a scaled layout that establishes roller position, height, width, and motor/gearbox placement before cutting any material
- C) Build a rough prototype without drawings to learn what dimensions work experimentally
- D) Ask a more experienced team for their intake design files

**Answer:** B

### Reflections

1. You built and tested a roller intake in 60 minutes. What was the biggest gap between your design intent and the physical result? What caused that gap — a design error, a fabrication error, or a gap between your mental model and reality?

2. You made at least one design change based on a hypothesis. Did the change produce the expected improvement? If not, what does that tell you about your diagnostic process?

3. The session described REV Robotics MAX Tube and MAX Planetary as standard FRC components. What are the advantages of building with a standardized component ecosystem versus designing fully custom mechanisms? What capabilities might you sacrifice by sticking to standard components?

4. Your intake test data is the beginning of a test-analysis-iterate loop. After this session, what would the next three iterations look like? What are the remaining failure modes that your first iteration didn't address?

### Summary

- **Roller surface speed:** The linear velocity at the roller contact point; calculated as roller radius times angular velocity; must be fast enough to pull game pieces against robot motion and friction.
- **Compliant wheel:** A flexible rubber intake wheel that deforms around game pieces, accepting dimensional variation and approach angle error; the modern FRC standard for robust game piece acquisition.
- **Intake geometry:** The configuration of roller height, width, spacing, and funnel angle that determines which game piece orientations can be captured reliably.
- **Hypothesis-driven iteration:** The testing discipline of predicting a specific cause-and-effect relationship before making a design change, then using test results to confirm or refute the prediction.
- **Component engineering drawings:** Manufacturer-provided 2D drawings showing shaft dimensions, bolt patterns, and envelopes for motors, gearboxes, and structural components; essential inputs for mechanism layout design.

---

## Session 6.3: Pneumatic Systems Workshop
**Duration:** 60 minutes | **Format:** Systems instruction + hands-on build

### Introduction

Pneumatics offer FRC teams something that electric motors cannot: fast, force-dense, binary actuation. A pneumatic cylinder can extend and retract in under 0.1 seconds, delivering hundreds of pounds of force in a compact package. FRC teams use pneumatics for mechanisms that need to snap into one of two positions instantly — intake deployment, bumper activation, claw open/close, and game piece ejection. This session teaches the FRC pneumatic system from compressor to cylinder.

**What You Will Learn:**
- The components of an FRC pneumatic circuit and how they connect (compressor, PCM/PH, solenoid, cylinder, tubing)
- How to select a cylinder for a specific force and stroke requirement
- How to connect and test a basic pneumatic circuit safely

### Core Concepts

#### FRC Pneumatic System Architecture
The FRC pneumatic system consists of six major component categories. The compressor pressurizes the system using stored electrical energy from the robot battery; FRC robots use a small Thomas 215 or equivalent compressor controlled automatically by the PCM or PH. The storage tank (pressure vessel) holds compressed air at up to 120 PSI; the tank provides a reservoir so the compressor doesn't need to run during every cylinder actuation. The pressure regulator reduces storage pressure (up to 120 PSI) to working pressure (typically 60 PSI for most FRC mechanisms) for the actuating cylinders.

The Pneumatic Control Module (PCM, CTRE) or Pneumatic Hub (PH, REV Robotics) is the electronic interface between the robot controller and the pneumatic system. The PCM/PH receives signals from the roboRIO, activates the compressor relay, and drives the solenoid valves. Solenoid valves are electrically controlled valves that switch airflow direction in a cylinder — a single-acting solenoid sends air to one side of the cylinder or vents it; a double-acting solenoid (two solenoid coils) controls both extend and retract independently.

The cylinder converts compressed air pressure into linear force. Double-acting cylinders use air pressure to drive both extension and retraction; single-acting (spring-return) cylinders use air to extend and a spring to retract. Bore diameter and pressure determine force: force = pressure x bore area. A 0.75-inch bore cylinder at 60 PSI develops 60 x pi x (0.375)^2 = approximately 26.5 lbs of force. A 1.5-inch bore cylinder at 60 PSI develops approximately 106 lbs. Cylinder stroke determines how far the mechanism moves.

#### Pneumatic Circuit Safety and Handling
Compressed air stores significant energy — a 120 PSI storage tank contains enough energy to cause serious injury if a fitting fails. FRC safety rules for pneumatics include: no component rated below 125 PSI operating pressure in the high-pressure portion of the circuit, all tubing and fittings must be rated for at least 200 PSI, and the system must include a pressure relief valve set to 120 PSI on the high-pressure side. The roboRIO software monitors pressure via a pressure transducer and disables the compressor above 120 PSI and re-enables it below 115 PSI.

Working with compressed air requires specific safety habits. Never point a pressurized fitting at yourself or others. Before disconnecting any fitting, verify the circuit is de-pressurized (bleed the system using the manual relief valve). Always inspect tubing for kinks, abrasion, and fitting security before pressurizing. Fittings should be push-to-connect type (standard in FRC) — push the tube fully into the fitting until it bottoms out, then tug gently to confirm the release collar is engaged. A fitting that pulls out under gentle tug was not fully inserted.

Pneumatic tubing routing in FRC should follow the same cable management principles as wire routing: secured at regular intervals to prevent flexing and chafing, routed away from hot surfaces (motors, gearboxes), kept away from moving mechanisms (tubing caught in a rotating joint is a reliability catastrophe), and labeled at connection points. Color-coded tubing (red for extend, blue for retract, yellow for high pressure) is a best practice that makes troubleshooting faster at competition.

#### Cylinder Selection and Sizing
Bimba, Parker, and Clippard are common FRC cylinder suppliers; Bimba is the most widely used. Cylinders are specified by bore (piston diameter), stroke (travel distance), and mounting style. For FRC mechanism selection:

- For a binary actuation of a lightweight intake flap: 0.75-inch bore, 1-2 inch stroke
- For a medium-load claw mechanism: 1.0-inch or 1.5-inch bore, 2-3 inch stroke
- For a heavy-duty kicker or puncher: 1.5-inch or 2.0-inch bore, 1-3 inch stroke

Mounting styles include clevis (swivel mount, pivot on both ends), rod end (mounting at one end only), and flange mount. Clevis mounting is the most common in FRC because it accommodates the slight angular misalignment that occurs as a mechanism pivots. A cylinder mounted rigidly at both ends to a pivoting mechanism will side-load the piston rod, causing premature wear and seal failure.

### Video Lessons

#### FRC Pneumatic System Overview (~12 min)
**Channel:** FRC team training / AndyMark/REV pneumatics channels
**Search:** "FRC pneumatics overview PCM compressor solenoid cylinder" on YouTube
**What to watch for:** How the instructor connects the system components from compressor to cylinder, naming each component and its function. Pay special attention to the pressure regulation stage — the difference between storage pressure (120 PSI) and working pressure (60 PSI) is critical for safe operation.

---

#### Pneumatic Cylinder Selection and Sizing (~8 min)
**Channel:** Fluid power / FRC mechanism design channels
**Search:** "pneumatic cylinder selection FRC force stroke bore" on YouTube
**What to watch for:** How the presenter calculates force from bore and pressure. Notice the cylinder mounting options and the explanation of why clevis mounts are preferred for pivoting mechanisms.

### Practice Exercises

#### Exercise 1: Pneumatic Circuit Diagram (15 min)
**Objective:** Draw a complete FRC pneumatic circuit schematic

1. Using standard fluid power symbols (available in any reference on pneumatics or online), draw a schematic showing:
   - Compressor and tank (high-pressure side)
   - Pressure relief valve
   - Regulator (high to working pressure)
   - PCM/PH (electronic control)
   - Double-acting solenoid valve
   - Double-acting cylinder
2. Label each component with its name and key specification (pressure rating, bore size, etc.)
3. Label the high-pressure and working-pressure sections of the circuit with their target pressures
4. Add the pressure transducer in the correct location

**Success Criteria:** A complete, correctly connected circuit schematic with all components labeled, pressure zones identified, and standard symbols used. A mentor confirms the circuit is correctly drawn.

---

#### Exercise 2: Cylinder Force Calculation (20 min)
**Objective:** Calculate cylinder force for 3 FRC mechanism scenarios

For each scenario, calculate extend force, retract force (rod side area is smaller), and verify the cylinder meets the requirement. Use working pressure of 60 PSI:

1. Intake deployment arm: requires 15 lbs to deploy against a 3-lb arm weight and spring. Proposed cylinder: 0.75-inch bore.
2. Claw mechanism: must grip a game piece with 40 lbs of force. Proposed cylinder: 1.0-inch bore.
3. Ejector punch: must accelerate a 2-lb game piece to 5 ft/s. Available stroke: 2 inches. What bore is needed?

Show all work using the formula: Force = Pressure x Area = Pressure x (pi x bore^2 / 4)

**Success Criteria:** All calculations are correct to within 5%. For Scenario 3, the minimum required bore is correctly derived from the force-acceleration calculation.

---

#### Exercise 3: Connect a Basic Pneumatic Circuit (15 min)
**Objective:** Physically connect a bench-test pneumatic circuit and verify operation safely

Using a provided bench test kit (regulator, solenoid, cylinder, tubing, fittings, and low-pressure air supply):

1. Connect the circuit following your schematic from Exercise 1
2. Pressurize to 30 PSI (lower pressure for first test)
3. Activate the solenoid manually (or by switch) and observe cylinder extension and retraction
4. Inspect all fittings by running soapy water over them and looking for bubbles (bubble = leak)
5. Correct any leaks found and re-test
6. Document: how many fittings leaked on the first test? What caused the leaks?

**Success Criteria:** A complete, leak-free pneumatic circuit that extends and retracts reliably under manual activation at 30 PSI test pressure.

### Knowledge Check

**1.** What is the maximum allowable storage pressure in an FRC pneumatic system?
- A) 60 PSI
- B) 80 PSI
- C) 120 PSI
- D) 200 PSI

**Answer:** C

**2.** What is the function of the pressure regulator in an FRC pneumatic system?
- A) It limits the compressor's power draw to stay within the robot's electrical budget
- B) It reduces storage pressure (up to 120 PSI) to a lower working pressure (typically 60 PSI) for the cylinders
- C) It monitors system pressure and shuts off the compressor above 120 PSI
- D) It controls the speed of cylinder extension by restricting airflow

**Answer:** B

**3.** What is the difference between the PCM (CTRE) and the PH (REV Robotics)?
- A) The PCM is for single-acting cylinders; the PH is for double-acting cylinders
- B) They are both electronic modules that control compressors and solenoids; PCM is by CTRE, PH is by REV Robotics
- C) The PCM controls pneumatics; the PH controls hydraulics
- D) The PCM is only used in rookie kits; the PH is for advanced teams

**Answer:** B

**4.** A 1.5-inch bore cylinder at 60 PSI working pressure produces approximately how much extend force?
- A) 90 lbs
- B) 106 lbs
- C) 60 lbs
- D) 170 lbs

**Answer:** B

**5.** Why is clevis mounting preferred over rigid mounting for pneumatic cylinders in FRC mechanisms?
- A) Clevis mounts are lighter and cheaper
- B) Clevis mounts allow slight angular pivoting, preventing side-loading the piston rod as the mechanism rotates
- C) Rigid mounts require special FRC-approved mounting hardware
- D) Clevis mounts allow the cylinder to be replaced without disassembling the mechanism

**Answer:** B

**6.** How do you verify that a push-to-connect pneumatic fitting is fully and correctly installed?
- A) Tighten the fitting with a wrench until it is snug
- B) Push the tube fully in until it bottoms, then tug gently to confirm the release collar has engaged
- C) Pressurize the system and listen for air leaks at the fitting
- D) Apply sealant tape to the tube before inserting

**Answer:** B

**7.** What does a double-acting solenoid valve do that a single-acting (spring-return) solenoid cannot?
- A) A double-acting solenoid can switch flow faster
- B) A double-acting solenoid independently controls both extension and retraction with air pressure, rather than using a spring for one direction
- C) A double-acting solenoid can handle higher pressures
- D) A double-acting solenoid requires only one electrical connection

**Answer:** B

**8.** What is the correct method for detecting a pneumatic fitting leak?
- A) Listen for a hissing sound while the system is pressurized
- B) Apply soapy water to the fitting and look for bubbles, which indicate escaping air
- C) Measure the pressure drop over 60 seconds using the pressure transducer
- D) All of the above methods are acceptable and correct

**Answer:** D

**9.** Why should pneumatic tubing be routed away from moving mechanisms?
- A) Compressed air makes noise that could interfere with communication systems
- B) Tubing caught in a rotating joint will be cut or torn, causing an immediate system failure
- C) Moving mechanisms generate heat that degrades plastic tubing over time
- D) FRC rules prohibit tubing within 3 inches of any moving mechanism

**Answer:** B

**10.** In an FRC double-acting cylinder, what is the difference between extend force and retract force?
- A) Extend force equals retract force for all double-acting cylinders
- B) Retract force is slightly lower than extend force because the rod occupies part of the piston area on the rod side
- C) Retract force is higher than extend force because compressed air acts on the smaller rod-side area
- D) Extend and retract forces are set independently by adjusting two separate pressure regulators

**Answer:** B

### Reflections

1. You connected a pneumatic circuit and found leaks on the first test. What is the most common cause of pneumatic leaks in a student-built system? What inspection habits would prevent most leaks before the first pressurization?

2. Pneumatics offer fast, force-dense binary actuation that motors cannot easily replicate. But pneumatics also have disadvantages compared to motors. What are those disadvantages? In what mechanism scenarios would you choose a motor over pneumatics even though pneumatics could technically do the job?

3. FRC pneumatic rules limit storage pressure to 120 PSI for safety reasons. If the limit were raised to 200 PSI, what would change in mechanism design? What additional risks would need to be managed?

4. You calculated cylinder force for three scenarios. Scenario 3 required deriving the bore from a force-acceleration calculation. What additional information would you need to accurately size a cylinder for a game piece ejector that needs to reach a specific distance, not just a specific speed?

### Summary

- **FRC pneumatic system:** The complete circuit from compressor through storage tank, regulator, PCM/PH, solenoid valve, and cylinder; converts electrical energy into linear mechanical force via compressed air.
- **Working pressure:** The regulated pressure supplied to cylinders, typically 60 PSI in FRC; lower than storage pressure (120 PSI) to ensure cylinder force matches mechanism requirements.
- **Double-acting cylinder:** A pneumatic actuator that uses air pressure for both extension and retraction, controlled by a double solenoid valve; provides positive force in both directions.
- **Clevis mount:** A swivel-ended cylinder mounting that accommodates angular misalignment as the attached mechanism pivots; prevents side-loading the piston rod.
- **Push-to-connect fitting:** The standard FRC pneumatic tubing connection; requires full tube insertion to engage the release collar — verified by tugging after insertion.

---

## Session 6.4: Servo Systems & Precision Control
**Duration:** 60 minutes | **Format:** Technical instruction + configuration lab

### Introduction

Not every FRC mechanism needs the raw power of a NEO motor or the snap of a pneumatic cylinder. Some mechanisms need precision: a specific angle, a specific position held reliably, a small controlled movement. Servos — self-contained servo motors with built-in position control — are the right tool for these applications. This session covers servo selection, wiring, and programming integration for common FRC precision positioning applications.

**What You Will Learn:**
- How servo motors work and how they differ from standard DC motors
- How to select a servo for FRC precision positioning requirements
- How to wire and configure a servo through the roboRIO PWM interface

### Core Concepts

#### How Servo Motors Work
A servo motor is a complete position control system in a single package. Inside the servo housing, you will find a small DC motor, a gear reduction train (usually plastic gears for low-cost servos, metal for higher-end units), a position sensor (typically a potentiometer in standard servos, an encoder in digital servos), and a control circuit that compares the commanded position to the sensed position and drives the motor to close the error.

The servo receives position commands as a PWM (Pulse Width Modulation) signal: a pulse sent 50 times per second, where pulse width (in microseconds) encodes the commanded position. The standard servo position range is 0 to 180 degrees commanded by pulses of 1000 to 2000 microseconds, with 1500 microseconds corresponding to the center (90 degrees) position. The roboRIO generates these PWM signals directly from its PWM output headers. In WPILib, a Servo object on a specific PWM channel takes a position value from 0.0 to 1.0 and generates the corresponding pulse.

Standard RC hobby servos cover a 180-degree range; "continuous rotation" servos have modified control circuits that convert the position command into a speed command, allowing full rotation. FRC teams use standard (position) servos for: hood angle adjustment on shooters, bumper pin release, ball gate mechanisms, camera pan/tilt, and any mechanism needing a specific held angle. Continuous rotation servos can serve as very low-torque drive motors for light mechanisms, though brushless motors are usually preferred.

#### Servo Selection for FRC Applications
Servo selection requires matching three parameters: torque, speed, and physical size. Torque is specified in oz-in or kg-cm (both are common); a servo with 100 oz-in stall torque can hold a load of 100 oz at 1 inch from the servo shaft, or 50 oz at 2 inches. Speed is specified in seconds per 60 degrees; a "0.20 sec/60 degrees" servo takes 0.20 seconds to sweep 60 degrees at no load — or approximately 0.60 seconds for a 180-degree sweep.

For FRC applications, the most commonly used servo is the REV Smart Robot Servo (SRS), which has a 0-270 degree range, 25 kg-cm stall torque, and a metal gear train. The wider range (270 vs. 180 degrees) provides more design flexibility for hood mechanisms and camera pan systems. The metal gears survive the vibration and shock environment of competition that typically destroys plastic-gear hobby servos in one or two events.

The primary limitation of servos compared to FRC motors is torque capacity. The largest practical FRC servo produces around 30 kg-cm (approximately 415 oz-in or 26 ft-lb). A NEO motor with a 10:1 gearbox produces over 100 ft-lb. Servos are appropriate for light mechanisms (a gate, a small hood angle, a bumper pin) but inappropriate for structural loads, drive forces, or anything that must resist significant external loading.

#### Wiring and Programming Integration
Servo wiring in FRC is straightforward: three wires (typically red/positive, black/ground, white or yellow/signal) connect to the roboRIO's PWM header. The servo draws power directly from the roboRIO's internal 6V regulated servo rail — no separate motor controller is needed, which simplifies wiring significantly. The roboRIO's servo rail can supply up to approximately 2A total; if multiple servos are needed and their combined stall current exceeds 2A, an external power supply through the PCM's auxiliary power output is needed.

In WPILib (Java or C++), declaring and controlling a servo is simple. A Servo object is created with the PWM channel number (0-9 on the roboRIO), and its position is set from 0.0 to 1.0 using the set() method. For example: Servo myServo = new Servo(0); myServo.set(0.5); positions the servo at its center position (90 degrees for a standard 180-degree servo, or 135 degrees for the REV SRS 270-degree servo).

Servo calibration (mapping the servo's physical range to the WPILib 0.0-1.0 command range) is important when physical position must be precisely known. The REV Smart Robot Servo has a configuration mode (entered by holding the SRS button at power-on) that allows the minimum, maximum, and center positions to be set to match the mechanism's actual range of motion. After calibration, set(0.0) corresponds exactly to the mechanism's minimum position and set(1.0) corresponds to the maximum, making programming the mechanism straightforward.

### Video Lessons

#### How Servo Motors Work (~8 min)
**Channel:** Electronics/engineering education channels
**Search:** "how servo motors work RC hobby servo position PWM explained" on YouTube
**What to watch for:** The internal anatomy of the servo — the potentiometer position sensor and the control circuit. Understanding how the servo closes its own position loop is what distinguishes servos from simple DC motors and explains both their capability (position holding) and their limitation (fixed torque limit).

---

#### FRC Servo Applications and REV Smart Robot Servo (~10 min)
**Channel:** REV Robotics / FRC electronics channels
**Search:** "REV Smart Robot Servo FRC application configuration" on YouTube
**What to watch for:** What physical applications REV demonstrates with their SRS. Notice the configuration mode — the calibration process makes the WPILib software command directly correspond to the physical mechanism position. This is more useful than it might appear.

### Practice Exercises

#### Exercise 1: Servo Torque and Speed Selection (15 min)
**Objective:** Select the correct servo for 3 FRC positioning applications

For each application, calculate required torque, identify minimum acceptable speed, and select a servo model that meets the requirements:

1. Shooter hood angle: Hood mass = 0.5 lbs, CG is 4 inches from servo shaft, must move 90 degrees in under 1 second
2. Camera pan axis: Camera mass = 0.3 lbs, CG is 2 inches from servo shaft, must move 270 degrees in under 2 seconds
3. Bumper pin release: Pin mechanism requires 10 lbs of force at 0.5 inches from the servo shaft, must actuate in under 0.5 seconds

For each application: calculate required torque in oz-in, identify minimum speed in sec/60-degrees, and specify a servo model by name that meets both requirements.

**Success Criteria:** All three torque calculations are correct. Servo selections meet both torque and speed requirements with at least 20% margin for safety factor.

---

#### Exercise 2: Servo Wiring and Basic Test (20 min)
**Objective:** Wire a servo to the roboRIO and command it to three positions via code

1. Connect a servo to the roboRIO PWM header on channel 0 (red to +6V, black to GND, signal to PWM0)
2. In WPILib, add a Servo object on channel 0 to the robot code
3. Add three button commands that set the servo to positions 0.0, 0.5, and 1.0
4. Deploy the code and verify the servo moves to three distinct physical positions when each button is pressed
5. Measure the actual angles at positions 0.0, 0.5, and 1.0 using a protractor or digital angle gauge
6. Document: are the three positions evenly spaced, or is there non-linearity?

**Success Criteria:** Servo moves reliably to three distinct positions under button command. All three position angles are measured and recorded. Any non-linearity between commanded position and physical angle is identified.

---

#### Exercise 3: Servo Calibration (15 min)
**Objective:** Calibrate a REV Smart Robot Servo to match the mechanism's physical range

1. Mount the servo in a simple test jig (or use the shooter hood test stand from Exercise 1)
2. Enter SRS configuration mode (hold SRS button at power-on, LED indicator activates)
3. Command the servo to its minimum physical position (0.0) and press the button to set the minimum endpoint
4. Command the servo to its maximum physical position (1.0) and press the button to set the maximum endpoint
5. Exit configuration mode and verify: does set(0.0) correspond to the minimum physical position? Does set(1.0) correspond to the maximum?
6. Write the code that moves the servo to three specific angles (15 degrees, 45 degrees, 90 degrees from minimum) by calculating the correct 0.0-1.0 command values

**Success Criteria:** After calibration, set(0.0) and set(1.0) correspond exactly to the physical mechanism limits. The angle-to-command-value calculation is correct for all three specified angles.

### Knowledge Check

**1.** What are the three components inside a standard servo motor that make it a position control system?
- A) Motor, battery, and switch
- B) DC motor, gear reduction, position sensor (potentiometer), and control circuit
- C) Motor, encoder, and stepper driver
- D) Brushless motor, ESC, and radio receiver

**Answer:** B

**2.** What PWM pulse width corresponds to the center (90-degree) position for a standard RC servo?
- A) 1000 microseconds
- B) 1500 microseconds
- C) 2000 microseconds
- D) 500 microseconds

**Answer:** B

**3.** What is the main advantage of the REV Smart Robot Servo over standard hobby servos for FRC?
- A) The REV SRS draws less current
- B) The REV SRS has a 270-degree range, metal gears, and a calibration mode — making it more robust for competition
- C) The REV SRS is compatible with more motor controllers than standard servos
- D) The REV SRS costs significantly less than hobby servos

**Answer:** B

**4.** What is the maximum torque a typical FRC servo can produce?
- A) Approximately 1 ft-lb (a small servo application)
- B) Approximately 25-30 kg-cm (around 26 ft-lb for the largest common FRC servos)
- C) Approximately 150 ft-lb (comparable to a NEO 550 with 10:1 gearbox)
- D) Approximately 5 ft-lb

**Answer:** B

**5.** Why do servos not require a separate motor controller (like a SPARK MAX)?
- A) Servos are only used for low-power applications where a motor controller is not needed
- B) Servos contain their own built-in control circuit and are driven directly by the roboRIO's PWM signal
- C) Servo motor controllers are integrated into the PCM/PH
- D) FRC rules prohibit motor controllers for servos

**Answer:** B

**6.** In WPILib, what command sets a Servo object to its center position?
- A) myServo.set(0.0)
- B) myServo.set(0.5)
- C) myServo.set(90.0)
- D) myServo.setAngle(90)

**Answer:** B

**7.** What is the purpose of servo calibration (setting min/max endpoints)?
- A) To increase the servo's torque output
- B) To make the software command range (0.0-1.0) correspond exactly to the mechanism's actual physical range of motion
- C) Calibration is only needed for metric servos
- D) To configure the servo for continuous rotation mode

**Answer:** B

**8.** For a bumper pin release that requires holding 10 lbs at 0.5 inches from the shaft, what is the required servo torque in oz-in?
- A) 5 oz-in
- B) 80 oz-in
- C) 160 oz-in
- D) 320 oz-in

**Answer:** C

**9.** What happens if the total stall current of multiple servos connected to the roboRIO exceeds approximately 2A?
- A) The roboRIO automatically limits current to each servo proportionally
- B) The roboRIO's servo power rail may be overloaded, requiring an external servo power supply
- C) The servos will run slower but not be damaged
- D) Only the first servo (PWM channel 0) will receive adequate power

**Answer:** B

**10.** What is a "continuous rotation" servo, and how does it differ from a standard position servo?
- A) It can rotate continuously in one direction but not the other
- B) Its control circuit converts position commands into speed commands, allowing full continuous rotation instead of position control
- C) It can be programmed to sweep back and forth continuously without input
- D) It is used exclusively for camera pan applications

**Answer:** B

### Reflections

1. You wired and commanded a servo. How does programming a servo compare to programming a motor with a SPARK MAX controller in terms of complexity? What does the simplicity of servo control come at the cost of?

2. Servo position control is "closed-loop" in the sense that the servo has its own internal position sensor and control loop. FRC motors with SPARK MAX controllers can also run in closed-loop position mode using a Through-Bore Encoder. What are the advantages and disadvantages of each approach for a precision positioning application?

3. The session says servos are appropriate for "light mechanisms" but not "structural loads." Where is the boundary between "light" and "structural" in FRC mechanism design? What specific test would you perform to determine whether a given application is within servo capability?

4. Servo calibration makes software commands correspond to physical positions. This is important for reproducibility across competitions — a calibrated mechanism behaves the same at every event. What other mechanism parameters (besides servo position) might drift between events and need to be "calibrated" or re-verified before competition?

### Summary

- **Servo motor:** A self-contained position control actuator consisting of a DC motor, gear reduction, position sensor, and control circuit; receives PWM commands and holds the commanded angle autonomously.
- **PWM (Pulse Width Modulation):** The signal encoding used to command servo position; pulse width in microseconds maps to servo angle (1000-2000 us maps to full range).
- **REV Smart Robot Servo (SRS):** The FRC-standard servo with 270-degree range, metal gears, and a field-configuration mode; more robust than hobby servos for competition use.
- **Servo calibration:** The process of setting the servo's minimum and maximum position endpoints to match the mechanism's physical range, ensuring software commands correspond to real-world positions.
- **Servo power rail:** The roboRIO's built-in 6V regulated supply for servo power; limited to approximately 2A total — exceeded with multiple high-torque servos requiring an external supply.

## Session 6.5: Integrated Mechanism Challenge
**Duration:** 60 minutes | **Format:** Team design-build-test challenge

### Introduction

This session is your Week 6 capstone: you will integrate two mechanisms into a coordinated assembly that completes a complete game piece cycle — acquire, transport, and score. This challenge replicates the most demanding phase of FRC build season: making multiple subsystems work together in sequence. You will face interface problems, timing issues, and integration failures. How you diagnose and resolve them is the core learning of this session.

**What You Will Learn:**
- How to define and verify interface requirements between two interacting mechanisms
- How to debug integration failures that don't occur in either mechanism individually
- How to run a complete game piece cycle test and evaluate system performance

### Core Concepts

#### Interface Requirements Between Mechanisms
A mechanism works correctly in isolation and fails when connected to another mechanism — this is one of the most common FRC build season frustrations, and it always traces to undefined or violated interface requirements. An interface requirement specifies what the "output" of one mechanism must look like to be correctly handled by the "input" of the next mechanism. For an intake feeding an indexer: the intake must deliver the game piece to a specific location (position requirement), in a specific orientation (orientation requirement), at a speed the indexer can accept (velocity requirement), and at a rate the indexer can keep up with (flow rate requirement).

Writing interface requirements before building the two mechanisms prevents integration failures. After building them, verifying interface requirements before integration testing identifies which mechanism is out of spec before you waste time with combined testing. The checklist is: does the intake deliver the game piece to the specified position? (Measure it.) Does the game piece arrive in the correct orientation? (Observe it.) Is the delivery speed within the indexer's design input range? (Measure it.) Only after confirming these interface conditions are met should you proceed to integrated operation testing.

Integration problems that survive this interface verification step are true emergent failures — behaviors that don't appear in either mechanism alone but arise from their interaction. Common FRC integration emergent failures: the indexer sensor triggers prematurely when the intake is running (motor electrical noise); the intake motor slows down when the indexer is loaded (inadequate power budget); the game piece jams at the handoff point because the physical gap between intake and indexer is slightly larger than expected (geometry tolerance stack-up).

#### System Testing and Debugging
System testing evaluates the integrated assembly against the original game piece manipulation requirements from Session 6.1. Run the same test protocol: multiple game piece orientations, multiple robot speeds, multiple starting positions. The system test reveals requirements that individual mechanism tests missed — usually because the individual tests didn't replicate the exact handoff conditions.

Debugging integrated systems requires a systematic approach. When the system fails, resist the temptation to immediately change something. First, observe the failure mode carefully — exactly where in the cycle does it fail? Is it consistent (same failure every time) or intermittent (fails sometimes, not others)? Consistent failures indicate a geometric or design problem; intermittent failures often indicate timing, sensor, or electrical issues. Separate the mechanisms again and test each individually to confirm the isolated performance is still acceptable. This narrows the failure to the interaction, not the individual mechanisms.

Root cause identification for integration failures often requires slowing down the process to observe what is actually happening. Run the intake and indexer at reduced speed so the game piece motion is visible. Use tape marks to record game piece positions at intermediate states. Use slow-motion video (any modern smartphone can do this) to observe handoff events that happen too fast to see at full speed. The investment in observation before changing anything almost always saves time compared to making random changes and hoping.

#### Iteration and Competition Readiness
A mechanism is "competition-ready" when it meets all its requirements reliably across the range of conditions expected at competition.
