"Reliably" means a high success rate — for game piece acquisition, competitive FRC teams target 90%+ success on required cycles. "Range of conditions" includes the variation in game pieces (they won't all be in perfect condition), field surface variation (carpet quality varies between venues), and driver error (the driver won't always approach at exactly the optimal angle and speed).

Competition readiness cannot be declared by looking at the mechanism — it must be demonstrated by running it. The minimum test for competition readiness is: 20 consecutive acquisition cycles with game pieces placed in random orientations within the design range, with the robot driven by a student, not placed carefully by a mentor. If the mechanism succeeds on 18 of 20 cycles (90%), it passes. If it succeeds on 12 of 20 (60%), it needs work. This standard is higher than it sounds — many mechanisms that "seem to work" in casual testing fail this more rigorous test.

### Video Lessons

#### FRC Mechanism Integration Process (~12 min)
**Channel:** FRC team build season documentation
**Search:** "FRC mechanism integration handoff intake indexer testing" on YouTube
**What to watch for:** How the team identifies and resolves interface problems. Do they write down interface requirements before integrating? How do they debug when the combined system fails? Notice whether they test each mechanism in isolation before integration — or go straight to combined testing.

---

#### FRC Competition Reliability Testing (~8 min)
**Channel:** FRC strategy and pit management channels
**Search:** "FRC robot reliability testing competition readiness 90 percent" on YouTube
**What to watch for:** What "competition ready" means to experienced FRC teams — specifically the quantitative success rate they target. Notice how the testing protocol accounts for driver variation and game piece condition variation.

### Practice Exercises

#### Exercise 1: Interface Requirement Definition (15 min)
**Objective:** Write interface requirements for the intake-to-indexer handoff

1. Identify 5 interface requirements for the transition between the roller intake (built in Session 6.2) and a simple indexer or output mechanism
2. For each requirement, specify: the parameter, the nominal value, the acceptable tolerance, and how you will measure it
3. Verify the intake mechanism (from Session 6.2) meets each interface requirement by measuring the actual delivery conditions
4. Document: does the intake as-built meet all interface requirements? Which requirements (if any) need mechanism adjustment before integration?

**Success Criteria:** Five specific, measurable interface requirements. Each requirement is verified against the existing intake performance with a pass/fail determination.

---

#### Exercise 2: Integrated Mechanism Build and Test (20 min)
**Objective:** Integrate two mechanisms and test the complete game piece cycle

1. Connect the intake from Session 6.2 to a second mechanism (indexer, conveyor, or output stage — from your team's parts or a simple build)
2. Define the integrated system test protocol: game piece orientations, robot speed, number of trials
3. Run 10 integration test cycles and record success/fail for each
4. Identify any integration failure mode that did not appear in isolated mechanism testing
5. Form a hypothesis about the root cause and implement one change

**Success Criteria:** Completed test record with 10 trials. At least one integration failure mode identified with a documented hypothesis and implemented corrective change.

---

#### Exercise 3: Competition Readiness Assessment (15 min)
**Objective:** Run a competition-readiness test on the integrated mechanism

1. Prepare 5 game pieces in random orientations within the design range
2. Have a student (not the mechanism designer) drive the robot through 10 acquisition cycles
3. Record success/fail, failure mode for each failure, and any driver feedback
4. Calculate success rate (successes / 10 cycles)
5. Assess: is this mechanism competition-ready (>90%) or does it need additional work?
6. If not competition-ready, identify the top 2 improvements that would have the highest impact on success rate

**Success Criteria:** A documented competition-readiness test with 10 data points driven by a student who is not the mechanism designer. Success rate calculated and compared to the 90% target. Improvement priorities identified if the target is not met.

### Knowledge Check

**1.** What is an "interface requirement" between two mechanisms?
- A) A requirement that both mechanisms use the same fastener standard
- B) A specification of what the output of one mechanism must look like for the next mechanism to handle it correctly
- C) A rule that prevents two mechanisms from occupying the same physical space
- D) A requirement for mechanisms to have compatible electrical connectors

**Answer:** B

**2.** What is an "emergent failure" in integrated mechanism testing?
- A) A failure that was predicted before testing
- B) A failure that appears only in the combined system, not in either mechanism tested alone
- C) A failure that is too small to observe at full speed
- D) A failure caused by incorrect programming, not mechanical issues

**Answer:** B

**3.** What does a consistent (every cycle) integration failure typically indicate?
- A) A timing or sensor issue in the programming
- B) A geometric or design problem in the handoff region
- C) A battery voltage problem causing both mechanisms to run slowly
- D) An intermittent electrical connection

**Answer:** B

**4.** What is the competition-readiness success rate target for game piece acquisition on competitive FRC teams?
- A) 50% (passing majority)
- B) 75% (three-quarters)
- C) 90% or higher
- D) 100% (perfect reliability)

**Answer:** C

**5.** Why should a mechanism be tested by a student driver, not placed carefully by a mentor, in competition readiness testing?
- A) FRC rules require student operation during testing
- B) Student driving replicates the realistic variation in approach angle and speed that will occur at competition
- C) Mentor-placed tests are not repeatable
- D) Student testing is faster and allows more cycles in the same time

**Answer:** B

**6.** What is the first step when the integrated system fails during testing?
- A) Immediately make a change and re-test
- B) Observe the failure mode carefully — exactly where in the cycle does it fail, and is it consistent or intermittent?
- C) Separate the mechanisms and rebuild both from scratch
- D) Check the battery voltage and replace if below 12V

**Answer:** B

**7.** What does "tolerance stack-up" mean in the context of mechanism integration?
- A) Piling extra tolerances on critical dimensions to ensure they are met
- B) The accumulation of small dimensional variations in multiple parts that together create a larger-than-expected gap or interference
- C) Stacking multiple mechanisms on top of each other to reduce floor space
- D) Adding extra tolerance to the competition score to account for mechanism unreliability

**Answer:** B

**8.** What tool can help observe game piece handoff events that happen too fast to see at full speed?
- A) A digital caliper to measure game piece position
- B) Slow-motion video from a smartphone camera
- C) A force gauge to measure handoff impact force
- D) A stopwatch to time each handoff event

**Answer:** B

**9.** What does it mean to "verify interface requirements" before integration testing?
- A) Test the integrated system and observe whether it meets requirements
- B) Confirm that each mechanism individually meets the specified output conditions before connecting them together
- C) Check the software code for correct interface declarations
- D) Ensure the mechanisms' fasteners are all correctly installed

**Answer:** B

**10.** What is the purpose of separating mechanisms and testing them individually when an integration failure occurs?
- A) To reset the mechanisms to their factory settings
- B) To confirm isolated performance is still acceptable, narrowing the failure to the interaction rather than individual mechanisms
- C) To reduce the test scope and complete testing faster
- D) Individual testing is required by FRC inspection before integration

**Answer:** B

### Reflections

1. You ran integration testing and found at least one failure that didn't appear in isolated mechanism testing. What was the failure, and how did you diagnose it? What does this experience teach you about the limits of isolated mechanism testing?

2. Your competition readiness test used a student driver instead of carefully placed game pieces. What was the success rate difference between careful placement and student driving? What does that difference tell you about mechanism robustness?

3. The concept of "interface requirements" between mechanisms mirrors how software interfaces work in programming — both sides of an interface must agree on what the data/object looks like when it passes between them. Where else in engineering or life do you see this kind of interface contract? How does defining the interface first change the design process?

4. This week covered game piece manipulation, pneumatics, servos, and system integration. Of the four topics, which do you feel most prepared to apply in a real FRC build season? Which are you least prepared for? What specifically would you do to improve your readiness in the weakest area before the season starts?

### Summary

- **Interface requirement:** A specification of what one mechanism's output must deliver for the next mechanism's input to function correctly; position, orientation, velocity, and flow rate are common interface parameters.
- **Emergent failure:** A failure mode that appears only in the integrated system, not in either mechanism tested in isolation; arises from tolerance stack-up, timing interactions, or electrical crosstalk.
- **Competition-readiness test:** A systematic evaluation using a student driver and random game piece orientations, targeting 90%+ success across 10+ consecutive cycles.
- **Tolerance stack-up:** The accumulation of small dimensional variations across multiple parts and joints, which together can create gaps or interferences larger than any individual tolerance.
- **Integration debugging:** The diagnostic process of observing failure mode, separating mechanisms to confirm isolated performance, and identifying the interaction as the root cause before changing anything.

# WEEK 7: Integration, Testing & Competition

**Duration:** 4-5 hours
**Learning Style:** Project-Based Build + Systems Thinking

This is the week where everything comes together. You have learned CAD, measurement, fasteners, and mechanisms — now you will integrate those skills into a complete robot assembly sequence, perform electrical integration and cable management, and run the systematic testing required to declare a robot competition-ready. This week's lessons mirror the final week of FRC build season: the most intense, most rewarding, and most revealing week of the program.

**Week 7 Learning Objectives:**
- Create an assembly plan that sequences all robot integration operations in the correct dependency order
- Assemble a chassis and drivetrain subassembly to specifications, verifying alignment at each stage
- Integrate game mechanisms onto the chassis and verify interface requirements
- Route and secure all wiring using professional cable management techniques
- Execute a complete system test and optimization sequence to declare the robot competition-ready

---

## Session 7.1: Assembly Planning & Preparation
**Duration:** 60 minutes | **Format:** Project planning workshop

### Introduction

Final assembly of an FRC robot is a project management problem as much as an engineering problem. You have dozens of fabricated parts, multiple subassemblies, an electrical system, and a software stack — all of which must come together in a sequence that respects dependencies, enables parallel work, and produces a robot that can be verified at each stage. Teams that assemble without a plan discover dependencies the hard way: they install a mechanism that blocks access to a bracket that needs to be replaced. This session teaches you to build the plan that prevents that cascade.

**What You Will Learn:**
- How to create an assembly dependency graph and extract the correct assembly sequence
- How to estimate assembly time and allocate work across team members in parallel
- How to identify critical path items that would delay the entire assembly if they slip

### Core Concepts

#### Assembly Dependency Analysis
An assembly dependency exists when one step cannot be completed before another is done. A wheel cannot be installed before the axle is in place. The electronics panel cannot be mounted before the chassis side rails are assembled. The intake cannot be bolted to the front of the chassis before the front tube is in the chassis. Mapping these dependencies creates an assembly dependency graph — a visual representation of which operations must follow which.

To build the dependency graph: list all assembly operations. For each operation, ask "what must be true before I can start this?" The answer identifies that operation's predecessors. Operations with no predecessors can start immediately. Operations that depend on many predecessors cannot start until all of them are complete. The graph reveals the critical path — the longest chain of dependent operations — which determines the earliest possible completion time for the entire assembly.

In FRC, the critical path typically runs through the chassis (must be complete before any mechanism can be mounted), then through the drivetrain (must be complete and tested before the robot can be driven), then through the highest-priority game mechanisms. Work that is not on the critical path can be performed in parallel without delaying final completion — this is where you assign team members efficiently.

#### Resource Allocation and Parallel Work
Once you have the dependency graph, you can assign team members to work streams that proceed in parallel. The constraint is resources: most FRC teams have 2-4 students working in the shop at any given time, and only certain operations can be performed simultaneously (two students can't both use the drill press at the same time). Parallel work is maximized by identifying independent subassemblies that can be built and tested separately before being brought together.

Time estimation for assembly operations should be based on actual experience, not optimistic guessing. The first time a student assembles a gearbox, it takes 30-45 minutes; an experienced student who has assembled the same gearbox several times can do it in 10-15 minutes. The gap between "in theory" and "in practice" in assembly time is one of the most reliable sources of late competition readiness. Ask experienced mentors for time estimates, or record actual times from previous season assemblies to calibrate your estimates.

Buffer time — time added to the schedule beyond the sum of estimated times — is not waste; it is risk management. The amount of buffer time appropriate for FRC final assembly is typically 25-50% of the total estimated assembly time. A 20-hour assembly that is scheduled for 30 hours has a 50% buffer. Teams that schedule with zero buffer routinely arrive at competition with partially assembled robots. Teams that schedule with appropriate buffer routinely have time for final testing, minor adjustments, and driver practice before bag day (or the equivalent competition ship date).

#### Pre-Assembly Verification
Before starting final assembly, verify that all components are present, correct, and have passed their individual quality inspections. The pre-assembly checklist is: all fabricated parts match their drawings (first article inspections completed), all COTS hardware is ordered and received, all fasteners are organized and available in sufficient quantity, and all electronic components are tested and functional at the bench level.

Missing components discovered during assembly are extremely disruptive — they stop the assembly at the point of discovery and require emergency ordering, local purchasing, or design workarounds. Competitive FRC teams do a complete component inventory check 48 hours before final assembly begins. This window allows time to expedite any missing items. Finding out a bearing is missing at the beginning of assembly day is a manageable problem; finding out 4 hours into assembly when everything depends on that bearing is a crisis.

The pre-assembly verification also includes tool verification. All required tools should be present, functional, and properly calibrated (torque wrenches at correct settings, drill bits sharpened, taps in good condition). Running out of a specific hex key at 11 PM during final assembly is preventable. A simple tool checklist takes 5 minutes to complete and prevents hours of shop-stop searching.

### Video Lessons

#### FRC Assembly Planning and Sequencing (~10 min)
**Channel:** FRC team build season documentation
**Search:** "FRC robot assembly plan sequence dependency build season" on YouTube
**What to watch for:** How experienced FRC teams manage final assembly — do they have a written plan? How do they handle the inevitable "this doesn't fit" discoveries? Notice how they assign students to parallel work streams.

---

#### Project Management for FRC Build Season (~8 min)
**Channel:** FRC strategy/leadership channels
**Search:** "FRC build season project management critical path parallel work" on YouTube
**What to watch for:** How the concept of critical path is applied to FRC build season. What happens when a critical path item is delayed? How do experienced teams recover from schedule slippage?

### Practice Exercises

#### Exercise 1: Dependency Graph Creation (15 min)
**Objective:** Build an assembly dependency graph for a 4-subsystem robot

Using your team's robot (or a provided sample robot specification), list all assembly operations and map their dependencies:

1. List 15-20 assembly operations (chassis frame assembly, wheel installation, gearbox assembly, electronics panel mounting, intake assembly, intake mounting, wiring harness installation, etc.)
2. For each operation, identify all predecessor operations
3. Draw the dependency graph (boxes for operations, arrows for dependencies)
4. Identify the critical path by finding the longest chain of connected dependencies
5. Identify which operations can be parallelized with the critical path

**Success Criteria:** A dependency graph with 15+ operations, all dependency arrows correctly drawn, and critical path identified. At least 3 parallel work streams identified outside the critical path.

---

#### Exercise 2: Assembly Schedule Creation (20 min)
**Objective:** Create a time-based assembly schedule with parallel work streams and buffer time

1. Estimate the time required for each operation in your dependency graph (ask a mentor for calibration)
2. Calculate the critical path duration (sum of critical path operation times)
3. Assign team members (assume a team of 4 students) to parallel work streams
4. Create a time-based Gantt chart showing all operations, their start/end times, and which student is assigned to each
5. Add 30% buffer to the total schedule and identify where the buffer is allocated

**Success Criteria:** A Gantt chart showing all assembly operations with realistic time estimates, parallel work streams for 4 students, and 30% buffer time explicitly called out.

---

#### Exercise 3: Pre-Assembly Verification Checklist (15 min)
**Objective:** Create and execute a pre-assembly verification checklist

1. Create a checklist with three sections: Parts Inventory, Electronic Components, and Tools
2. Under Parts Inventory: list every unique fabricated part and COTS component needed, with quantities
3. Under Electronic Components: list every electrical component (roboRIO, motor controllers, motors, sensors) needed
4. Under Tools: list every tool required for assembly operations
5. Execute the checklist against your team's actual inventory
6. Identify and plan to resolve any shortfalls found

**Success Criteria:** A complete pre-assembly checklist with quantities. All shortfalls identified with a resolution plan (order, substitute, or redesign) and estimated resolution time.

### Knowledge Check

**1.** What is an assembly dependency?
- A) A part that must be purchased from a specific supplier
- B) A constraint that prevents one assembly operation from starting before a specific preceding operation is complete
- C) A design requirement that limits how components can be oriented
- D) The total weight of all assembly operations combined

**Answer:** B

**2.** What is the "critical path" in an assembly schedule?
- A) The sequence of safety checks required before running the robot
- B) The longest chain of dependent operations, which determines the minimum assembly completion time
- C) The path the robot takes during autonomous operation
- D) The sequence of electrical connections that must be made first

**Answer:** B

**3.** Why is discovering a missing component 4 hours into final assembly worse than discovering it 48 hours before?
- A) Parts are more expensive to purchase on short notice
- B) 4 hours into assembly, other operations depend on the missing component and are blocked, stopping multiple work streams
- C) FRC rules prohibit design changes during the final 48 hours before bag day
- D) Parts discovered missing late cannot be substituted

**Answer:** B

**4.** What is "buffer time" in a project schedule, and why is it not waste?
- A) Time allocated for eating during long assembly sessions
- B) Extra time beyond estimated operation times, serving as risk management for the inevitable delays and surprises
- C) Time allocated for rework after inspection identifies errors
- D) The time difference between the earliest and latest possible assembly start times

**Answer:** B

**5.** What is a Gantt chart?
- A) A chart showing the weight distribution of robot components
- B) A time-based visual schedule showing assembly operations, their durations, and their parallel/sequential relationships
- C) A diagram of the robot's electrical circuit
- D) A comparison chart of mechanism concept alternatives

**Answer:** B

**6.** Why should time estimates come from mentor experience or recorded previous season data rather than student guesses?
- A) Students are not allowed to make time estimates under FRC rules
- B) Experience-based estimates account for realistic difficulties; student guesses tend to be optimistically low
- C) Mentor estimates are required for official team documentation
- D) Student guesses are consistently too long; mentors provide shorter, more efficient estimates

**Answer:** B

**7.** What does it mean for an operation to be "on the critical path"?
- A) It is a safety-critical step that requires extra inspection
- B) Any delay in that operation delays the entire assembly completion time
- C) It is the most complex operation in the assembly sequence
- D) It requires the most expensive tooling

**Answer:** B

**8.** What is the purpose of parallel work streams in assembly planning?
- A) To ensure two students always check each other's work
- B) To allow independent operations to proceed simultaneously, completing the total work faster than sequential execution
- C) Parallel streams are required when the team has more than 3 students
- D) Parallel work reduces the need for documentation

**Answer:** B

**9.** How much buffer time is typically appropriate for FRC final assembly?
- A) 5-10% of estimated assembly time
- B) 25-50% of estimated assembly time
- C) 100% of estimated assembly time (double the estimate)
- D) No buffer; schedule should be exactly the sum of estimated times

**Answer:** B

**10.** What is the minimum time before assembly that a component inventory check should be performed?
- A) 1 hour before
- B) 48 hours before, to allow time to resolve shortfalls
- C) One week before, to allow full procurement lead times
- D) At the start of assembly — check while assembling

**Answer:** B

### Reflections

1. You created a dependency graph and found the critical path. Were you surprised by what ended up on the critical path? Is the critical path for your robot the same as what your team intuitively treats as the highest priority?

2. Buffer time is described as "risk management, not waste." In your experience, what kinds of surprises during FRC final assembly have consumed buffer time? If you had not had buffer time, what would have happened?

3. The pre-assembly checklist found shortfalls in your inventory. How did those shortfalls feel to discover at the checklist stage versus how they would have felt to discover during assembly? What organizational habit would prevent those shortfalls from occurring in the first place?

4. The Gantt chart assigned 4 students to parallel work streams. Were there bottlenecks — times when multiple students needed the same tool or workspace simultaneously? How would you resolve those bottlenecks in real assembly?

### Summary

- **Assembly dependency graph:** A visual map of assembly operations and their prerequisite relationships; reveals the critical path and opportunities for parallel work.
- **Critical path:** The longest chain of dependent assembly operations; determines the minimum possible completion time and identifies operations where delays have maximum impact.
- **Parallel work streams:** Independent assembly operations assigned to different team members simultaneously; reduces total assembly time below what sequential execution would require.
- **Buffer time:** Scheduled time beyond estimated operation times, serving as risk management for the inevitable surprises; 25-50% of total estimated time is the FRC standard.
- **Pre-assembly verification:** A systematic check of parts inventory, electronic components, and tools completed 48 hours before final assembly begins; prevents show-stopping shortfall discoveries during assembly.

---

## Session 7.2: Chassis Assembly & Drivetrain Integration
**Duration:** 60 minutes | **Format:** Hands-on assembly with verification checkpoints

### Introduction

The chassis is the foundation of every FRC robot. Every mechanism, every system, every component ultimately attaches to or interacts with the chassis. Getting the chassis right — square, level, strong, and accurately dimensioned — is the highest-leverage quality investment in robot assembly. A chassis that is out of square by 0.5 degrees creates alignment problems that propagate through every mechanism attached to it. This session guides you through a chassis assembly with systematic verification checkpoints at each stage.

**What You Will Learn:**
- How to assemble a rectangular chassis frame to dimensional and squareness specifications
- How to install drive gearboxes and verify their alignment relative to the chassis
- How to install wheels, verify drivetrain function, and perform a straight-line drive test

### Core Concepts

#### Chassis Frame Assembly and Squareness
A typical FRC chassis frame consists of a rectangular arrangement of aluminum tubes connected at corners with gusset plates and rivets or bolts. The assembly sequence that produces maximum squareness is: cut all tubes to length first and verify lengths before assembly, assemble one long rail, assemble the opposite long rail, then connect the two rails with cross members, and verify squareness before finalizing any fasteners.

Squareness verification uses the diagonal measurement method: for a rectangle that is truly square, both diagonals are equal in length. Measure from the front-left corner to the rear-right corner, then from the front-right corner to the rear-left corner. If the diagonals differ by more than 0.125 inches for a typical FRC chassis, the chassis is not square enough — you must adjust before the fasteners are fully tightened. Adjustment technique: the chassis is flexible while fasteners are loose, so pushing opposite corners together while measuring achieves the square configuration.

The gusset plate installation determines the final joint geometry. Gussets must be fully flush against both mating tube surfaces before riveting or bolting. A gusset that is bridging (not fully flush) because of a tube end cut that is not perpendicular will cause the joint to be out of angle even if the tubes appear to be in the right position. Check every gusset for flush seating with a feeler gauge before installing any fasteners at that joint. This inspection takes 10 seconds per joint and is worth doing for every joint.

#### Drivetrain Gearbox Integration
Drive gearboxes (West Coast Drive gearboxes, REV MAXSwerve modules, or other drive systems) mount to the chassis side rails. The critical alignment requirement for a tank drive gearbox is that the output shaft is perpendicular to the chassis side rail — if the shaft is angled relative to the rail, the wheel will not run parallel to the robot's motion direction, causing drive inefficiency and unpredictable steering.

Gearbox mounting holes in the chassis side rail are typically drilled using the gearbox mounting plate as a drill guide — the gearbox is held in position, and the mounting holes are center-punched through the gearbox bolt pattern onto the tube surface. This technique produces mounting holes that exactly match the gearbox bolt pattern, minimizing alignment error. The alternative (drilling holes independently based on measurements from the CAD model) introduces more dimensional tolerance and is less reliable for precise alignment.

After the gearbox is mounted, verify shaft perpendicularity using a square or digital angle gauge held against the side of the shaft while the gearbox is bolted to the chassis. Then install the wheel and verify that the wheel face is parallel to the chassis centerline (toe-in/out check from Session 4.3). A correctly installed drive gearbox will produce a wheel that rolls perfectly in the robot's intended direction of motion.

#### Wheel Installation and Drive Verification
Wheel installation on a hex shaft involves securing the hub onto the hex shaft and retaining it axially (preventing it from sliding off). The standard FRC method uses a shaft collar (a clamp that grips the shaft) or snap ring in a groove machined in the shaft. The shaft collar must be tightened with correct torque (typically 25-30 in-lb for the set screw) and should be visually confirmed to be tight against the hub — not floating away from it.

After all wheels are installed, the first drive verification is hand-rolling the robot: lift the robot slightly and push it forward, observing whether all wheels roll smoothly in the same direction with no resistance or side-skid. Any wheel that scrubs sideways indicates a toe alignment problem. Any wheel that doesn't spin freely indicates a bearing issue or shaft binding. These problems are far easier to fix before the robot is powered than after.

Powered drive testing starts with a brief low-speed forward drive in an open area. Observe: does the robot drive straight, or does it curve? A robot that curves consistently in one direction has a side-to-side speed imbalance (likely a gearbox ratio mismatch or different wheel effective diameters). A robot that wobbles or vibrates has a wheel balance or bearing issue. A robot that drives straight and smoothly on the first powered test has been assembled correctly. This result is achievable — and it is the expected outcome when assembly is done with the discipline taught throughout this curriculum.

### Video Lessons

#### FRC Chassis Assembly Step by Step (~15 min)
**Channel:** FRC team build season content
**Search:** "FRC chassis assembly tube gusset square verification" on YouTube
**What to watch for:** The squareness verification technique — how the team checks both diagonals and what they do when the chassis is out of square. Notice whether they check gusset flush seating before riveting. This is the technique you should apply to your own chassis assembly.

---

#### FRC Drivetrain Gearbox Mounting and Alignment (~10 min)
**Channel:** FRC drivetrain build channels
**Search:** "FRC drivetrain gearbox mounting alignment wheel parallel" on YouTube
**What to watch for:** The gearbox-as-drill-guide technique for drilling mounting holes. Notice how the team verifies wheel parallelism after installation and what they do when a wheel is not parallel.

### Practice Exercises

#### Exercise 1: Chassis Frame Assembly (20 min)
**Objective:** Assemble a rectangular chassis frame and verify squareness

1. Cut all four chassis rails to length (or use pre-cut rails) and verify all four lengths are within 0.010 inches of specification
2. Assemble the frame using corner gussets — do not tighten fasteners fully yet
3. Measure both diagonals
4. Adjust the frame until both diagonals are within 0.125 inches of each other
5. Verify all gussets are flush seated before tightening fasteners
6. Tighten all fasteners to correct torque and re-measure diagonals to confirm squareness was maintained

**Success Criteria:** Both chassis diagonals within 0.125 inches after final fastener tightening. All gussets confirmed flush before fastener installation.

---

#### Exercise 2: Gearbox Mounting and Alignment (20 min)
**Objective:** Mount a drive gearbox to the chassis and verify output shaft perpendicularity

1. Position the gearbox on the chassis side rail in its intended location
2. Use the gearbox as a drill guide to mark (not drill) the mounting hole centers on the rail using a center punch
3. Drill the mounting holes with the correct clearance drill bit (verify size from the gearbox mounting specification)
4. Mount the gearbox with all specified fasteners at correct torque with Loctite
5. Check the output shaft perpendicularity using a combination square or digital angle gauge
6. Document: what is the measured angle of the output shaft relative to the chassis rail surface? Is it within 1 degree of perpendicular?

**Success Criteria:** Gearbox mounted with all fasteners correctly installed and torqued. Shaft perpendicularity measured and within 1 degree of the chassis rail surface.

---

#### Exercise 3: Drive Verification Test (15 min)
**Objective:** Verify drivetrain function through hand-roll and powered tests

1. Install wheels on the gearbox output shafts with correct shaft collar torque
2. Hand-roll the chassis: lift slightly and push forward, observing all wheels for smooth rolling
3. Correct any binding or side-skid identified during hand-roll
4. Perform a powered straight-line drive test: drive forward 10 feet and measure lateral deviation
5. Record: how far did the robot deviate from a straight line over 10 feet?
6. FRC target: straight-line deviation should be less than 6 inches over 10 feet without software correction

**Success Criteria:** All wheels roll smoothly during hand-roll test. Powered test shows straight-line deviation of less than 6 inches over 10 feet (or deviation is identified and root-cause analyzed).

### Knowledge Check

**1.** What is the squareness verification method for a rectangular chassis?
- A) Use a digital angle gauge at each corner to verify 90 degrees
- B) Measure both diagonals — a square rectangle has equal diagonal lengths
- C) Check that all four rail lengths are equal
- D) Use a laser level to verify all four rails are coplanar

**Answer:** B

**2.** What is the gusset-as-drill-guide technique for mounting hole location?
- A) Using the gusset plate as a template to transfer hole locations onto the chassis tube
- B) Using the gearbox mounting plate as a drill guide held against the chassis, center-punching mounting holes through its bolt pattern
- C) Drilling oversized holes and using the gusset to adjust the exact position
- D) Measuring hole positions from the CAD model and drilling independently

**Answer:** B

**3.** How much deviation from equal diagonals is acceptable before a chassis needs squareness adjustment?
- A) 0.010 inches
- B) 0.050 inches
- C) 0.125 inches
- D) 0.500 inches

**Answer:** C

**4.** What is the purpose of the hand-roll test before powered drive testing?
- A) To verify the robot's weight is within FRC limits
- B) To detect binding, misalignment, and wheel toe problems before electrical power is applied
- C) To break in new bearings before full-speed operation
- D) FRC inspection requires a hand-roll test before the robot can be driven

**Answer:** B

**5.** What does consistent lateral deviation (curved driving) during a straight-line drive test indicate?
- A) The chassis is out of square
- B) A side-to-side speed imbalance, likely from a gearbox ratio mismatch or different wheel effective diameters
- C) One battery terminal has higher resistance than the other
- D) The robot's center of gravity is off-center

**Answer:** B

**6.** What does a wheel that scrubs sideways during the hand-roll test indicate?
- A) The wheel is mounted on the wrong side of the chassis
- B) A toe alignment problem — the wheel is not parallel to the robot's direction of motion
- C) The wheel is the wrong diameter for the gearbox output speed
- D) The wheel tread has worn unevenly

**Answer:** B

**7.** What is the correct tightening torque for a shaft collar set screw to retain a drive wheel?
- A) Finger tight only — over-tightening will strip the hex shaft
- B) Approximately 25-30 in-lb for a standard 5/16-18 or 10-32 set screw
- C) As tight as possible with a long hex key for maximum retention
- D) The same torque as structural SHCS fasteners (15-20 in-lb for 10-32)

**Answer:** B

**8.** Why is it important to check gusset flush seating before installing fasteners?
- A) A bridging gusset prevents fasteners from reaching their correct torque
- B) A gusset that is not flush will lock in an angular misalignment of the joint even if the tubes appear to be in the correct position
- C) Non-flush gussets violate the FRC chassis weight distribution rules
- D) Flush seating is required for the CAD model to match the physical robot

**Answer:** B

**9.** What is the straight-line deviation performance target for an FRC chassis over 10 feet without software correction?
- A) Less than 1 inch
- B) Less than 6 inches
- C) Less than 12 inches
- D) No straight-line deviation target exists; software PID corrects all deviations

**Answer:** B

**10.** What should you do if the chassis diagonals are not equal after final fastener tightening?
- A) Accept the deviation if it is less than 0.500 inches
- B) Loosen the fasteners, re-adjust the chassis to equal diagonals, re-verify flush seating, and re-tighten
- C) Add a cross-brace to prevent further distortion
- D) Remeasure in 24 hours — aluminum will settle to its natural position over time

**Answer:** B

### Reflections

1. You verified chassis squareness using the diagonal measurement method. Before this session, did you know this technique? Have you ever assembled a chassis and not checked squareness? What problems might that chassis have exhibited that you attributed to other causes?

2. The gearbox-as-drill-guide technique is preferred over measuring hole positions independently. What does this reveal about the accuracy of hole positions relative to each other versus the accuracy of hole positions relative to an external reference point?

3. Your drivetrain drive test produced a certain amount of lateral deviation. Was this what you expected? What would you need to change to achieve less than 6 inches of deviation over 10 feet?

4. The session describes chassis assembly as "the highest-leverage quality investment in robot assembly." Do you agree? What would the downstream consequences be if the chassis is assembled with a squareness error of 0.5 degrees that is never corrected?

### Summary

- **Diagonal measurement:** The squareness verification technique for rectangular frames; equal diagonals confirm a square rectangle, with difference greater than 0.125 inches indicating adjustment is needed.
- **Gusset flush seating:** The requirement that gusset plates lie fully flat against both mating tube surfaces before fastening; bridging gussets lock angular misalignment into the joint permanently.
- **Gearbox-as-drill-guide:** The technique of using the gearbox mounting plate to locate and center-punch mounting holes on the chassis, producing hole positions that exactly match the bolt pattern.
- **Hand-roll test:** The pre-power drivetrain check of lifting and pushing the robot to detect wheel binding, toe misalignment, and bearing problems before electrical testing.
- **Straight-line deviation:** The lateral distance the robot travels away from the intended direction over a fixed distance; the FRC target is less than 6 inches over 10 feet without software correction.

---

## Session 7.3: Mechanism Integration
**Duration:** 60 minutes | **Format:** Integration workshop with verification

### Introduction

With the chassis and drivetrain complete, the game mechanisms attach. Mechanism integration is when the robot begins to look like a robot — intake, elevator, shooter, climber all taking their places. It is also when the interface requirements you defined in Week 6 are tested in the context of the full robot for the first time. This session guides you through mounting mechanisms to the chassis, verifying their operation in the robot context, and resolving the integration problems that always arise.

**What You Will Learn:**
- How to mount mechanisms to a chassis using defined interface brackets and verify their position
- How to verify that mechanisms meet their interface requirements after mounting on the chassis
- How to resolve geometric integration problems discovered during final robot assembly

### Core Concepts

#### Mechanism Mounting and Interface Verification
Each mechanism mounts to the chassis through a defined set of attachment points. The mechanical interface consists of: the mounting bracket geometry (which face of the chassis does it attach to, and at what position?), the fastener pattern (how many fasteners, what size, what spacing?), and the positional requirement (how accurately does the mechanism need to be located on the chassis for it to function correctly?). These interface parameters should be defined in the CAD model and verified against the physical chassis before and after mounting.

Interface verification after mounting means checking that the mechanism is in the correct position and orientation relative to the chassis, not just that all the fasteners are installed. Measure from a chassis reference (a tube face or a known-correct hole position) to the critical feature of the mechanism (intake roller centerline, shooter exit angle, elevator rail parallelism). If the measured position differs from the CAD specification by more than the mechanism's functional tolerance, the interface must be adjusted.

Shimming is the technique of adding thin spacers (shims) to adjust a mechanism's position when the mounting structure doesn't provide a fine adjustment mechanism. A 0.020-inch aluminum shim placed behind one side of a mounting bracket shifts the mechanism's position by 0.020 inches at that point. FRC teams keep a supply of aluminum shim stock in various thicknesses (0.005, 0.010, 0.020 inches) in the hardware inventory. The ability to shim precisely is a professional fabrication skill that allows mechanisms to be positioned to specifications that the structural accuracy of the chassis alone cannot achieve.

#### Geometric Integration Problems
The most common geometric integration problem is mechanism clearance failure: a mechanism at one extreme of its range of motion collides with another part of the robot or with the chassis. This was supposed to be caught in the CAD interference detection from Session 3.4, but CAD models are not always fully up to date with fabricated part variations, and as-built dimensions can differ from design dimensions enough to cause clearances that should have been 0.200 inches to actually be -0.050 inches (a physical collision).

The systematic approach to resolving clearance failures: first, determine the exact location and magnitude of the interference. Second, determine which side of the interference is easier to move. Third, verify that moving the easier side doesn't violate a different constraint. Fourth, make the smallest possible change that resolves the interference with adequate margin. Fifth, re-check all previously verified clearances after making the change, because moving one mechanism can create new interferences with other mechanisms.

Mechanism interactions also include kinematic conflicts: two mechanisms that move during the same phase of the game cycle that cannot both be in their active positions simultaneously. An elevator that must be raised fully before an intake can fold up (because the intake path crosses the elevator at intermediate heights) is a kinematic conflict — the resolution is to define an operating sequence in software and enforce it. Identifying kinematic conflicts in the final assembly stage is late — they should have been caught in CAD motion studies from Session 3.4 — but catching them during robot assembly is still much better than discovering them at competition.

#### Mechanism Functional Verification on the Robot
After mounting, every mechanism must be functionally verified in its actual robot position and context — not just in the isolated test stand position from Sessions 6.2-6.5. The robot context changes several things: the mechanism is at a different height and orientation than the test stand, the mechanism now interacts with adjacent mechanisms, and the robot's weight distribution and vibration characteristics are now fully representative of competition conditions.

The functional verification protocol for each mechanism on the full robot mirrors the isolated testing protocol from Week 6: run the mechanism through its intended range of motion, feed it game pieces (or simulated equivalents), and measure performance against the original requirements. The expected result is that the mechanism performs at the same level on the full robot as it did in isolation — if it performs worse, the robot context is introducing a new constraint or interference that must be identified and resolved.

Document every verification result: pass or fail against each requirement, any deviation from isolated test performance, and the root cause of any performance difference. This documentation is the basis for the test and optimization plan in Session 7.5.

### Video Lessons

#### FRC Mechanism Installation on Chassis (~12 min)
**Channel:** FRC team build season channels
**Search:** "FRC mechanism mounting chassis integration verification" on YouTube
**What to watch for:** How the team positions the mechanism, what references they use for measurement, and how they adjust when the initial position is wrong. Notice whether they use shims, slotted holes, or bracket redesign for position adjustment.

---

#### Resolving Integration Problems in FRC Robots (~10 min)
**Channel:** FRC build season retrospective channels
**Search:** "FRC robot integration problem solving clearance conflict resolution" on YouTube
**What to watch for:** Real integration problems that teams encountered and how they resolved them. Notice the problem-solving process — do they make minimal targeted changes, or do they make large changes that might introduce new problems?

### Practice Exercises

#### Exercise 1: Mechanism Position Verification (15 min)
**Objective:** Verify that a mounted mechanism meets its positional interface requirements

For a mechanism mounted on your team's robot (or a provided assembly):

1. Identify 3 positional requirements for the mechanism (from the CAD specification or design documentation)
2. Measure each positional parameter from a chassis reference feature
3. Compare to the CAD specification
4. Determine: is each positional parameter within its acceptable tolerance?
5. For any out-of-tolerance parameter, identify the adjustment mechanism (shim, slotted hole, bracket adjustment) and estimate the adjustment needed

**Success Criteria:** Three positional measurements with chassis reference, compared to specifications, with pass/fail determination. All out-of-tolerance parameters have an identified adjustment method.

---

#### Exercise 2: Clearance Check on Fully Assembled Robot (20 min)
**Objective:** Systematically check all mechanism clearances in the fully assembled robot

1. Identify all movable mechanisms and their range of motion (intake fold, elevator travel, arm rotation)
2. Move each mechanism manually through its full range of motion
3. Check for contact with: chassis structure, other mechanisms, electronics, and wiring
4. Document every contact found: which mechanisms, what range of motion position, what is the estimated interference distance?
5. Prioritize findings by severity: P1 (contact prevents mechanism function), P2 (contact at extreme position only, mechanisms never reach simultaneously), P3 (contact is cosmetic or electrical, not structural)

**Success Criteria:** A complete clearance audit with all movable mechanisms checked at full range. All contacts documented with severity classification.

---

#### Exercise 3: Mechanism Functional Verification on Robot (15 min)
**Objective:** Compare mechanism performance on the full robot to isolated test results

1. Select one mechanism that was tested in isolation in Sessions 6.2-6.5
2. Run the same test protocol used in the isolated session (same game piece orientations, same robot speeds)
3. Compare the pass/fail rate to the isolated test results
4. Identify any performance difference and its probable root cause
5. If performance is worse on the full robot, propose and implement one adjustment to recover performance

**Success Criteria:** Comparable test protocol data from both isolated and on-robot testing. Performance difference (if any) identified with a root cause hypothesis. One adjustment implemented with a documented result.

### Knowledge Check

**1.** What is the purpose of measuring mechanism position from a chassis reference feature after mounting?
- A) To verify the mechanism's fasteners are correctly installed
- B) To confirm the mechanism is in the correct position and orientation relative to the chassis, not just that it is attached
- C) FRC inspection requires post-installation position measurement for all mechanisms
- D) To calculate the robot's center of gravity

**Answer:** B

**2.** What is shimming, and when is it used in FRC mechanism integration?
- A) Adding foam padding to mechanisms to reduce vibration noise
- B) Inserting thin metal spacers behind a mounting bracket to precisely adjust a mechanism's position
- C) Covering sharp edges with rubber trim to prevent safety hazards
- D) Adding weight plates to balance the robot's center of gravity

**Answer:** B

**3.** What is the correct first step when a mechanism clearance failure is discovered?
- A) Immediately rebuild the mechanism with modified geometry
- B) Determine the exact location and magnitude of the interference before deciding what to change
- C) Add foam bumpers to absorb the impact
- D) Move the conflicting mechanism to a different position on the chassis

**Answer:** B

**4.** What is a "kinematic conflict" between two mechanisms?
- A) Two mechanisms that use different motor types (brushed vs. brushless)
- B) Two mechanisms that cannot both be in their active positions simultaneously due to geometric interference
- C) Two mechanisms that require conflicting software commands at the same time
- D) Two mechanisms whose combined weight exceeds the robot weight limit

**Answer:** B

**5.** Why does functional verification need to be repeated when a mechanism is on the full robot, even though it passed isolated testing?
- A) Isolated test results are not valid for FRC inspection
- B) The robot context changes mechanism orientation, introduces adjacent interactions, and adds representative vibration — all of which can affect performance
- C) Mechanisms must be re-tested after each assembly operation per FRC rules
- D) Isolated testing is only for development; full-robot testing is the only valid performance measurement

**Answer:** B

**6.** What is the "minimal targeted change" principle for resolving integration problems?
- A) Always make the cheapest change first
- B) Make the smallest possible change that resolves the problem with adequate margin, to minimize the risk of creating new problems
- C) Make only one type of change per session to maintain a controlled test environment
- D) Make changes only when two or more people agree on the same solution

**Answer:** B

**7.** What standard shim thicknesses does an FRC team's hardware inventory typically include?
- A) 1/16, 1/8, and 1/4 inch
- B) 0.005, 0.010, and 0.020 inch aluminum shim stock
- C) 0.001, 0.002, and 0.005 inch precision shims
- D) Only 0.010 inch shims are used in FRC

**Answer:** B

**8.** What happens if you change one mechanism position to resolve a clearance conflict without checking other clearances afterward?
- A) Nothing — changing one mechanism cannot affect clearances with other mechanisms
- B) The change might create new interferences with other mechanisms that were previously not in conflict
- C) All previously verified clearances become invalid and must be completely re-verified from scratch
- D) The CAD model becomes invalid and must be updated before any further assembly

**Answer:** B

**9.** What is "positional tolerance" for a mounted mechanism?
- A) The weight allowance for a mechanism within the robot's total weight budget
- B) The acceptable range of variation from the specified position at which the mechanism will still function correctly
- C) The tolerance on the mechanism's mounting fastener sizes
- D) The time allowed for mechanism position changes during competition

**Answer:** B

**10.** What should be documented during mechanism functional verification on the full robot?
- A) Only pass/fail against requirements
- B) Pass/fail against each requirement, deviation from isolated test performance, and root cause of any performance difference
- C) Only failures — passing mechanisms do not need documentation
- D) Video footage of the mechanism operating, but no written records

**Answer:** B

### Reflections

1. You discovered geometric integration problems during the clearance check. Were these problems visible in the CAD model, or did they arise from as-built dimensional variation? What does this tell you about the limitations of CAD interference detection?

2. The shimming technique allows fine position adjustment without rebuilding brackets. In a perfect world, would shimming be necessary? What would need to be true about the fabrication process and the CAD model for mechanisms to always land in the correct position without shimming?

3. Functional verification on the full robot showed a performance difference from isolated testing. What robot-context factors do you think were responsible? If you were designing the testing program from scratch, how would you structure isolated testing to better predict full-robot performance?

4. Kinematic conflicts are best caught in CAD motion studies, but were caught here during assembly. What is the cost difference between catching a kinematic conflict in CAD versus catching it during assembly versus catching it at the first competition? How does this cost difference justify the investment in thorough CAD motion studies?

### Summary

- **Mechanism mounting interface:** The combination of bracket geometry, fastener pattern, and positional requirement that defines how a mechanism attaches to the chassis and where it must be located.
- **Positional verification:** Post-installation measurement from a chassis reference to confirm the mechanism is within its positional tolerance; catches mounting errors before they affect downstream assembly steps.
- **Shimming:** The technique of inserting thin metal spacers to achieve precise mechanism position adjustment beyond what the structural accuracy of the mounting brackets provides.
- **Kinematic conflict:** A condition where two mechanisms cannot both be in their active positions simultaneously due to geometric interference; requires operating sequence definition in software.
- **Clearance audit:** A systematic check of every movable mechanism through its full range of motion, identifying all contacts with structure, adjacent mechanisms, and wiring before powered operation.

---

## Session 7.4: Electrical Integration & Cable Management
**Duration:** 60 minutes | **Format:** Technical instruction + hands-on wiring lab

### Introduction

Electrical integration is the final system-level task before testing. Every motor, sensor, and communication device must be connected to the robot controller in a way that is correct, reliable, and maintainable. In FRC, poorly managed wiring is one of the most common sources of competition failures: a wire that vibrates out of a connector, a cable caught in a rotating mechanism, a CAN bus interruption that disables the entire robot mid-match. This session teaches professional wiring practices that eliminate these failure modes.

**What You Will Learn:**
- How to route and secure wire runs for reliability in the FRC vibration environment
- How to dress and protect wire terminations against vibration fatigue and pull-out
- How to perform a complete electrical continuity and functionality check before powering the robot

### Core Concepts

#### Wire Routing Principles
Professional wire routing in FRC follows four principles: separation, support, protection, and identification. Separation means routing power wires (main battery cables, motor power) physically separated from signal wires (CAN bus, PWM, sensor cables) to minimize electromagnetic interference. Support means securing wires at regular intervals (every 6-8 inches for small signal wires, every 12 inches for larger power cables) to prevent vibration fatigue at unsupported spans. Protection means routing wires away from sharp edges, moving mechanisms, and heat sources, and using cable sleeves or conduit where wires pass through openings. Identification means labeling wires at both ends with their destination (device name and pin number) so troubleshooting at competition can be performed without a schematic.

Wire routing decisions are made in the assembly sequence before wires are cut and installed, not after the robot is assembled and you are trying to thread wires through a gap that is too small. During chassis assembly, identify where all major wire runs will pass and verify adequate clearance. Plan the route from the battery and PDP (Power Distribution Panel) to each major electrical load before any wires are cut. The CAD model should include a wire routing diagram — many competitive FRC teams include cable routing as a layer in their Onshape assembly.

Heat shrink tubing at every wire termination prevents the most common FRC wiring failure: wire strand breakage at the point where the wire enters a crimp or terminal. At this point, the wire is mechanically supported on one side (by the terminal) and completely flexible on the other — every vibration cycle flexes the wire at exactly this point, ultimately breaking individual strands. A correctly sized piece of heat shrink (or strain relief boot) bridges across this transition zone, distributing the flex over a longer span and dramatically extending wire life.

#### CAN Bus Wiring and Daisy-Chain Management
CAN bus (Controller Area Network) is the communication protocol used by most FRC motor controllers (SPARK MAX, Talon SRX/FX, Victor SPX). All CAN devices share a single two-wire bus (CAN-H and CAN-L), daisy-chained from the roboRIO through each device and terminated at the last device in the chain. The CAN bus is significantly more reliable and feature-rich than PWM, but it requires correct wiring to function.

CAN bus wiring rules: use twisted pair wire (the two CAN conductors twisted together at approximately 1 twist per inch) to reduce noise pickup, maintain consistent wire color coding (yellow for CAN-H, green for CAN-L or the REV convention), do not use T-branches or star topology (daisy-chain only), and terminate the end of the bus with the built-in termination resistor on the last device (most FRC motor controllers have a hardware termination switch or jumper). A CAN bus with a broken connection anywhere in the chain loses communication with all devices downstream of the break — this is why CAN reliability directly determines overall robot reliability.

Robot startup diagnostics for CAN bus: when the roboRIO boots, it scans the CAN bus and reports the number of devices found. A device that is wired correctly but has a wrong device ID (CAN ID) will appear on the bus but will not respond to commands sent to its intended ID. Every CAN device must be configured with its unique device ID before robot assembly — two devices with the same ID create a bus conflict that makes both unresponsive. SPARK MAX devices are configured via the REV Hardware Client software (USB connection to the SPARK MAX while it is powered).

#### Pre-Power Electrical Verification
Before applying battery power to a completed robot for the first time, a systematic electrical verification reduces the risk of damage from wiring errors. The verification sequence:

1. Continuity check: with the main breaker open (no power), use a multimeter in continuity mode to verify that no wire intended to be isolated from ground is connected to ground, and that each intended connection is complete.
2. Motor controller assignment: verify that each motor controller is assigned to the correct CAN ID or PWM channel by cross-referencing the wiring diagram.
3. Sensor wiring check: verify polarity (red to positive, black to ground) for all sensors before connection.
4. Current path verification: trace the complete current path from battery positive through the main breaker, through the PDP, to each load and back to battery negative. Any break in this path will prevent that load from operating.
5. Short circuit check: with a multimeter, verify resistance between the battery positive and negative terminals (with main breaker open and disconnected) is not near zero (which would indicate a short circuit).

First power-up should always be done with the robot on a stand (wheels off the floor), ready to be disabled immediately if unexpected movement occurs. The robot operator should stand at the Driver Station ready to immediately disable the robot if any mechanism moves unexpectedly. Running through a quick enable and disable with no joystick input (drive all motors should be commanded to zero) confirms the basic electrical system is functional before testing motion.

### Video Lessons

#### FRC Cable Management Best Practices (~12 min)
**Channel:** FRC electrical/wiring channels
**Search:** "FRC robot wiring cable management professional best practices" on YouTube
**What to watch for:** How top teams route and secure wires — the support interval, the separation of power and signal, the use of heat shrink and cable ties. Notice whether their wiring looks like it could survive 50 matches of vibration and collision.

---

#### FRC CAN Bus Wiring and Troubleshooting (~10 min)
**Channel:** REV Robotics / CTRE / FRC electrical channels
**Search:** "FRC CAN bus wiring daisy chain troubleshooting SPARK MAX" on YouTube
**What to watch for:** The daisy-chain topology and why T-branches are prohibited. Pay attention to the device ID configuration process — this is a step that many teams skip and then spend hours troubleshooting at their first competition.

### Practice Exercises

#### Exercise 1: Wire Routing Plan (15 min)
**Objective:** Create a wire routing diagram for a 4-motor robot

Using a top-view drawing of your robot's chassis:

1. Mark the location of all electrical components (PDP, roboRIO, motor controllers, motors, sensors)
2. Draw the main power cable route (battery to main breaker to PDP)
3. Draw the CAN bus daisy-chain route through all SPARK MAX controllers in sequence
4. Draw all motor power wires from motor controllers to motors
5. Draw all signal wires (PWM, sensor cables) from roboRIO to their connected devices
6. Identify and resolve any route that passes through a moving mechanism's range of motion

**Success Criteria:** A complete wire routing diagram with all power and signal cables shown, CAN bus daisy-chain sequence documented, and no routes through moving mechanism zones.

---

#### Exercise 2: Professional Cable Installation (20 min)
**Objective:** Route and secure one wire run to professional cable management standards

1. Select one major wire run from your diagram (e.g., CAN bus from roboRIO to the furthest SPARK MAX)
2. Cut the wire to exact length (no excess slack, no tension)
3. Terminate both ends with appropriate connectors (Weidmuller or equivalent at motor controller, JST at roboRIO CAN port)
4. Apply heat shrink to every termination point
5. Secure the wire at 6-8 inch intervals using cable ties or clips
6. Verify the wire route stays at least 1 inch from all moving mechanism zones

**Success Criteria:** Wire run installed with correct-length wire, heat shrink at all terminations, support every 6-8 inches, and confirmed clearance from moving mechanisms.

---

#### Exercise 3: Pre-Power Electrical Verification (15 min)
**Objective:** Complete a pre-power check on the robot before first power-up

1. Perform continuity check: verify 3 critical signal wires are complete (roboRIO CAN-H to first SPARK MAX CAN-H, SPARK MAX 1 CAN-out to SPARK MAX 2 CAN-in, roboRIO PWM 0 to servo PWM 0)
2. Verify all motor controllers are configured with unique CAN IDs (use REV Hardware Client or Phoenix Tuner)
3. Verify polarity of all sensor connections (visual inspection of red/black wire assignment)
4. Short circuit check: with main breaker open, verify >100 ohm resistance between PDP positive and PDP negative
5. Perform first power-up with robot on stand: enable robot, confirm no unexpected mechanism movement, disable robot
6. Document: any discrepancies found in steps 1-4, and the result of step 5

**Success Criteria:** All pre-power checks documented. No short circuits detected. First power-up completed safely with robot on stand and correct disable response.

### Knowledge Check

**1.** What are the four principles of professional wire routing?
- A) Short, straight, labeled, and color-coded
- B) Separation, support, protection, and identification
- C) Crimped, sleeved, routed, and secured
- D) Power, signal, ground, and communications

**Answer:** B

**2.** Why should power wires be physically separated from signal wires?
- A) Mixing power and signal wires is a safety hazard at FRC competition voltages
- B) Power wires generate electromagnetic interference that can corrupt signals in adjacent signal wires
- C) FRC rules prohibit power and signal wires in the same cable bundle
- D) Physical separation makes the robot easier to inspect

**Answer:** B

**3.** What is the purpose of heat shrink tubing at wire terminations?
- A) To provide electrical insulation at the termination point
- B) To create a strain relief that distributes wire flexing over a longer span, preventing vibration fatigue breakage
- C) To identify each wire's destination by color
- D) To prevent oxidation of the copper conductor at the terminal

**Answer:** B

**4.** What topology must a CAN bus use in FRC?
- A) Star topology with the roboRIO at the center
- B) Daisy-chain (series) topology from the roboRIO through all devices in sequence
- C) Ring topology with the last device connected back to the first
- D) T-branch topology for each mechanism subgroup

**Answer:** B

**5.** What happens if two CAN devices on the same bus have the same device ID?
- A) The device connected closer to the roboRIO takes priority
- B) Both devices conflict, making both unresponsive to commands
- C) The roboRIO automatically assigns a new ID to the conflicting device
- D) Only the device with the lower firmware version is affected

**Answer:** B

**6.** What does a short circuit check before first power-up involve?
- A) Measuring the current draw of each motor controller individually
- B) Verifying resistance greater than 100 ohms between PDP positive and negative terminals with the main breaker open
- C) Checking for sparks when the battery is connected
- D) Testing each fuse in the PDP with a fuse tester

**Answer:** B

**7.** Why should the first power-up always be performed with the robot wheels off the floor (on a stand)?
- A) FRC inspection requires all robots to be tested on stands first
- B) Unexpected mechanism movement at first power-up is safely contained with wheels off the floor
- C) The robot's software calibration requires a stationary start
- D) Battery voltage is unstable on the first connection and may damage motor controllers

**Answer:** B

**8.** What is the support interval for signal wires (recommended maximum unsupported span)?
- A) Every 3-4 inches
- B) Every 6-8 inches
- C) Every 12-18 inches
- D) Support is only needed where wires pass through openings

**Answer:** B

**9.** What does the CAN device ID configure?
- A) The maximum current the device will allow to the motor
- B) The unique address on the CAN bus used to direct commands to a specific device
- C) The PWM frequency used for motor control
- D) The device's communication baud rate

**Answer:** B

**10.** Why is wire identification (labeling both ends) important for competition?
- A) FRC inspection requires all wires to be labeled
- B) Labeled wires allow troubleshooting to proceed without a schematic, which is critical during a timed pit repair
- C) Wire labels identify the wire's maximum current rating
- D) Labels are required for the Engineering Notebook electrical documentation

**Answer:** B

### Reflections

1. You routed and secured a wire run to professional standards. What was the most difficult aspect of the installation — wire length estimation, termination, or securing? What technique did you develop to make it easier?

2. CAN bus daisy-chain topology means a single broken connection makes all downstream devices unresponsive. Compare this to a star topology (separate connection from roboRIO to each device). What are the tradeoffs between these two topologies in terms of reliability, wiring complexity, and troubleshooting?

3. The pre-power check revealed (or didn't reveal) problems. If a short circuit had been found, what would be the systematic procedure for locating it? How would you isolate the fault to a specific wire or component?

4. Professional cable management takes significantly longer than informal "just zip-tie it somewhere" wiring. Is the time investment worth it for FRC? At what competition level (rookie local regional vs. World Championship) does the investment become clearly worth it, and why?

### Summary

- **Wire routing principles:** Separation (power from signal), support (every 6-8 inches), protection (away from sharp edges and moving mechanisms), and identification (labeled at both ends).
- **CAN bus daisy-chain:** The required FRC CAN topology where all devices are connected in series; a single break disables all downstream devices, making reliable connections essential.
- **Heat shrink strain relief:** Heat shrink tubing applied at wire terminations to distribute vibration flexing over a longer span, preventing the strand breakage that is the most common FRC wiring failure.
- **Device ID configuration:** The pre-assembly assignment of unique CAN bus addresses to each motor controller; duplicate IDs create bus conflicts that make both devices unresponsive.
- **Pre-power verification:** The systematic continuity, polarity, resistance, and short circuit checks performed before first battery connection, reducing the risk of damage from wiring errors.

---

## Session 7.5: System Testing & Optimization
**Duration:** 60 minutes | **Format:** Structured testing with documentation and iteration

### Introduction

Your robot is assembled and electrically integrated. Now comes the most important session of the curriculum: proving that it works. System testing is not "drive it around and see if it seems okay" — it is a structured evaluation of every requirement against measured performance, followed by targeted optimization of the subsystems that fall short. This session teaches you to be your own robot's harshest and most honest critic, which is the fastest path to competition readiness.

**What You Will Learn:**
- How to create and execute a system test plan that covers all robot requirements
- How to use measurement and observation to identify the root cause of performance gaps
- How to prioritize and execute optimization actions based on competitive impact

### Core Concepts

#### System Test Planning
A system test plan lists every requirement from the original game analysis (Session 6.1) and defines a specific test for each requirement that produces a measurable pass/fail result. The plan is organized by subsystem: drivetrain tests, intake tests, indexer tests, scoring mechanism tests, and endgame mechanism tests. Each test specifies: what is tested, how many trials, what constitutes a pass, what data is recorded, and who performs the test.

The test plan is written before testing begins, not during. This prevents the temptation to declare a mechanism "passing" when it barely meets the requirement, or to change the test when the mechanism fails. A test plan also prevents duplication — if the same test is performed three different ways by three different students, the results are not comparable. Standardized tests produce standardized results that can be compared across matches, configurations, and competitions.

The FRC competition scoring structure creates a natural prioritization for test planning. Requirements that directly translate to match points get more testing attention than requirements that affect reliability but not points directly. A team that scores primarily through the intake-to-indexer-to-scorer pipeline should devote the most test time to that pipeline, and less time to mechanisms used for bonus points that the team has decided not to pursue. This is not a reason to skip testing non-scoring mechanisms — they can still cause DNFs (did not finish) from failures — but it is a reason to prioritize optimization.

#### Performance Gap Analysis and Root Cause
When a test reveals that a mechanism does not meet its requirement, the next step is root cause analysis. The root cause is the fundamental reason for the performance gap — not the symptom. A symptom might be "intake misses game pieces when approaching from the right angle." The root causes might be: intake roller is not wide enough, roller surface speed is too low, game pieces are arriving in an orientation outside the design range, or the gap between roller and floor is too large for the specific game piece batch.

The systematic root cause process: observe the failure carefully (what exactly happens when the mechanism fails?), isolate variables (test with only one thing different from the baseline), form a hypothesis (my theory is that X is causing Y), implement a change (change only X), re-test (did Y improve?). This is the scientific method applied to engineering, and it is the discipline that separates teams that fix problems from teams that change random things and hope.

Performance gaps that cannot be closed with adjustments and tuning indicate design limitations. A roller intake that can never acquire game pieces arriving from more than 20 degrees off-center, despite all tuning attempts, has a fundamental geometric constraint: the effective capture angle is simply not wide enough. This is a design change, not a tuning change — and a design change in the final week before competition is a high-risk intervention. The team must decide: is the performance gap large enough to justify the risk of a design change, or is it better to accept the limitation and compensate through driver training and game strategy?

#### Drive Practice and Driver Feedback
Driver practice is system testing from the human-machine interface perspective. The driver operates the robot through realistic game scenarios and provides feedback on: which mechanisms feel reliable, which require unnatural compensating behaviors, and which control behaviors the software team should address. This feedback loop between the driver and the mechanical/software teams is one of the most valuable and least utilized processes in FRC.

The driver feedback framework: after each practice session, the driver answers three structured questions: What worked well? What didn't work as expected? What one change would most improve my ability to drive this robot effectively? The answers identify both mechanical improvements (change the intake geometry to make it more forgiving of approach angles) and software improvements (add a slow-down zone before the scoring position so the driver doesn't overshoot) that transform a functional robot into a competitive robot.

Drive practice volume matters. Teams that get 10+ hours of drive practice before their first competition consistently outperform teams with less practice, even when the robots are mechanically equivalent. The robot that was designed to require 2 seconds of driver attention per scoring cycle will take 1.5 seconds per cycle after 10 hours of practice — and 1.5 seconds versus 2 seconds per cycle, across 3-4 cycles per match, is the difference between 60 and 80 points. Engineering the robot is necessary; practicing driving it is what converts engineering into results.

### Video Lessons

#### FRC System Testing Before Competition (~12 min)
**Channel:** FRC team build season / competition preparation channels
**Search:** "FRC robot system testing competition preparation structured" on YouTube
**What to watch for:** How organized teams structure their testing time in the final days before competition. Do they follow a written test plan? How do they handle failures discovered late in the process? Notice how they involve the driver in feedback.

---

#### FRC Inspection Checklist Overview (~8 min)
**Channel:** FRC official channels / veteran team training
**Search:** "FRC robot inspection checklist requirements competition readiness" on YouTube
**What to watch for:** What FRC inspection actually checks — size, weight, electrical safety, bumper rules. How much time does a well-prepared team spend in the inspection line versus a team that hasn't prepared? What are the most common inspection failures?

### Practice Exercises

#### Exercise 1: System Test Plan Creation (15 min)
**Objective:** Write a complete system test plan for a 4-subsystem robot

1. List the top 3 requirements for each of 4 subsystems (drivetrain, intake, indexer/transport, scoring mechanism)
2. For each requirement, define: test procedure (step by step), number of trials, pass criterion, data to record
3. Identify the highest-priority requirement and its test
4. Estimate the time required for the complete test plan
5. Schedule the tests in order of priority (highest priority first)

**Success Criteria:** A complete test plan with 12 tests covering all 4 subsystems. Each test has a specific procedure, trial count, and pass criterion. Total time estimate and prioritized schedule included.

---

#### Exercise 2: Execute Test Plan and Record Results (20 min)
**Objective:** Execute the highest-priority 4 tests from your plan and document results

1. Execute the top 4 tests from your plan (or a prioritized subset if the full plan takes longer than 20 minutes)
