2. For each test: follow the documented procedure, record all data, and make the pass/fail determination
3. For each failing test, note the observed failure mode
4. Prioritize the failing tests by their impact on competitive performance

**Success Criteria:** Four tests executed and documented with raw data, pass/fail results, and failure mode descriptions for all failing tests.

---

#### Exercise 3: FRC Inspection Checklist Self-Assessment (15 min)
**Objective:** Apply the FRC inspection checklist to your robot and identify any non-compliant items

Using the current year's FRC Robot Inspection Checklist (available at firstinspires.org):

1. Work through the checklist systematically: size and weight, bumper compliance, electrical safety (breakers, wiring, battery mounting), motor and actuator limits, pneumatic safety
2. Document each item checked and its status: Pass, Fail, or N/A
3. For each Fail, identify the specific compliance issue and the corrective action required
4. Estimate the time required to complete all corrective actions
5. Prioritize: which failures would prevent inspection from passing (must fix before competing) versus which are minor items that could be addressed during competition?

**Success Criteria:** Completed FRC inspection checklist with all items evaluated. All failing items documented with specific corrective actions and time estimates.

### Knowledge Check

**1.** Why is a system test plan written before testing begins, not during?
- A) FRC competition requires test plans to be submitted in advance
- B) Pre-written plans prevent declaring passing results on mechanisms that barely meet requirements and prevent changing tests when mechanisms fail
- C) Writing the plan takes too long to do during testing
- D) Pre-written plans are required for the Engineering Notebook

**Answer:** B

**2.** What is the difference between a performance symptom and a root cause?
- A) Symptoms are mechanical; root causes are electrical
- B) A symptom is the observable failure behavior; a root cause is the fundamental reason it occurs
- C) Symptoms appear during testing; root causes appear only at competition
- D) There is no meaningful difference — they describe the same thing

**Answer:** B

**3.** When should a team accept a performance limitation rather than attempting a design change in the final week before competition?
- A) Never — all performance gaps should be resolved before competing
- B) When the performance gap can be compensated by driver training and strategy, and the risk of a design change outweighs the benefit
- C) Only when the mechanism has never been tested before
- D) When the performance gap affects a mechanism used only for bonus points

**Answer:** B

**4.** What three structured questions should a driver answer after each practice session?
- A) How fast was the robot? How many cycles? What was the battery voltage?
- B) What worked well? What didn't work as expected? What one change would most improve effectiveness?
- C) Which mechanisms broke? What was the root cause? How long did each repair take?
- D) What was the highest-scoring match? What was the average cycle time? What was the autonomous success rate?

**Answer:** B

**5.** What is the competitive impact of 10+ hours of drive practice compared to minimal practice for mechanically equivalent robots?
- A) No impact — robot capability is entirely determined by mechanical and software design
- B) Practiced teams achieve faster cycle times and more consistent performance, converting the same robot capability into higher scores
- C) Minimal impact for experienced drivers, but significant for new drivers
- D) Drive practice mainly benefits autonomous performance; teleoperated performance is robot-limited

**Answer:** B

**6.** What does the FRC Robot Inspection Checklist verify?
- A) Robot scoring capability and autonomous programming
- B) Robot size and weight, bumper compliance, electrical safety, and motor/actuator limits
- C) Battery condition and age of electrical components
- D) Documentation completeness (Engineering Notebook, drawings, bill of materials)

**Answer:** B

**7.** What is the consequence of having two CAN devices with the same ID during system testing?
- A) The robot will fail the FRC inspection
- B) Both devices will be unresponsive to commands, appearing as though the wiring is faulty
- C) One device will work; the other will be permanently disabled
- D) The roboRIO will automatically re-assign IDs to resolve the conflict

**Answer:** B

**8.** Why do the highest-priority requirements get more testing attention than lower-priority ones?
- A) FRC rules require minimum test counts for scoring-related mechanisms
- B) Requirements that directly translate to match points have higher competitive impact; improvement there produces more results than equivalent effort on lower-priority requirements
- C) High-priority requirements are harder to meet and therefore need more attempts
- D) The test plan must be completed in priority order

**Answer:** B

**9.** What does it mean to "isolate variables" during root cause analysis?
- A) Test the mechanism while isolated from the chassis
- B) Change only one thing at a time so test results can be attributed to a specific cause
- C) Perform testing with the robot isolated from other robots on the field
- D) Remove all non-essential mechanisms before diagnosing a failure

**Answer:** B

**10.** Why should the first power-up be done with wheels off the floor?
- A) It is required by the FRC inspection protocol
- B) Unexpected mechanism movement at first power-up is safely contained without the robot being able to drive into anything
- C) Wheel friction affects motor current and makes the first diagnostics inaccurate
- D) Floor contact at first power-up can cause static discharge damage to electronics

**Answer:** B

### Reflections

1. You executed tests and found failing requirements. What is the hardest part of being honest in a test — declaring a mechanism "failed" when you want it to succeed? What organizational culture changes would make your team more comfortable accepting failure data and using it productively?

2. Root cause analysis requires isolating variables — changing only one thing at a time. In the pressure of the final week before competition, teams often change multiple things simultaneously. What is the risk of this approach? Have you seen it cause confusion on your team?

3. Driver feedback identified improvements to either mechanics or software. Were the improvements mechanical (something the student engineers can fix), software-oriented (something the programmers can fix), or both? How should the team prioritize improvements across multiple subsystems when time is limited?

4. You assessed your robot against the FRC inspection checklist. Were there any surprises — items that failed inspection that you were confident were correct? What does this tell you about the assumptions you make when designing and assembling, and how a systematic checklist catches things that "feel right" but aren't?

### Summary

- **System test plan:** A pre-written document specifying the procedure, trial count, and pass criterion for every robot requirement; prevents subjective pass/fail decisions and enables consistent measurement across multiple sessions.
- **Root cause analysis:** The systematic process of observing failure mode, isolating variables (changing only one factor at a time), forming a hypothesis, and testing to confirm or refute the hypothesis.
- **Design limitation:** A performance gap that cannot be closed through adjustment or tuning because it reflects a fundamental geometric or mechanical constraint; requires a design change rather than tuning, with higher implementation risk.
- **Driver feedback loop:** The structured post-practice question session that translates driver operational experience into specific mechanical and software improvement priorities.
- **FRC Robot Inspection Checklist:** The official compliance verification document covering size, weight, electrical safety, bumper construction, and actuator limits; non-compliance items must be resolved before the robot can compete.
